#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1A2On is oxidized to AO3− by KMnO4 in acidic medium. If 1.34 m mol of A2On requires 32.2 mL of 0.05 M acidified KMnO4 solution for complete oxidation, which of the following statement(s) is (are) correct?
(a) The value of n = 2   (b) Empirical formula of oxide is AO   (c) 1.0 mol of A2On would require 1.0 mol of acidified K2Cr2O7 solution   (d) 'A' can be a metal from second group of Periodic Table.		Stoichiometry oxide oxidationRedox Titration | Stoichiometry 2 | Reactions of KMnO4 and K2Cr2O7🟡 Similar(a), (b), (c) n=2; oxide AO; 1 mol oxide = 1 mol K2Cr2O7
Concept In any redox titration the equivalents of oxidant equal the equivalents of reductant, and that single balance fixes the unknown n. Acidified KMnO₄ has n = 5 (Mn⁺⁷ → Mn²⁺). Once n is known the oxide formula follows, and the three remaining statements are just consequences of it. The trap is to count moles instead of equivalents.
Key relation (mmol × n-factor)oxide = (V × M × 5)KMnO₄ A runs +n → +5 and there are two A atoms, so A₂Oₙ loses 2(5−n) electrons per formula unit.
Solution
1. In A₂Oₙ oxygen is −2 and the unit is neutral, so each A carries +n; in the product AO₃⁻ each A is +5.
2. So each A loses (5 − n) electrons and the n-factor of A₂Oₙ = 2(5−n) per formula unit.
3. Equivalents of KMnO₄ = 32.2 × 0.05 × 5 = 8.05 meq; equivalents of oxide = 1.34 × 2(5−n), and the two are equal.
4. 1.34 × 2(5−n) = 8.05, so 5 − n = 8.05/2.68 = 3.00 and n = 2; statement (a) is correct.
5. With n = 2 the oxide is A₂O₂, whose simplest ratio is AO, so (b) is correct. Here A is therefore +2 in the oxide.
6. For 1 mol A₂O₂ the equivalents = 2(5−2) = 6; acidified K₂Cr₂O₇ also has n = 6 (Cr₂O₇²⁻ → 2Cr³⁺), so moles needed = 6/6 = 1.0, hence (c) is correct.
7. Although A is +2 in the oxide, it is oxidised all the way to +5; a group-2 metal is locked at +2 and cannot reach +5, so (d) is wrong.
 meq KMnO4 = 32.2 x 0.05 x 5 = 8.05
 1.34 x 2(5-n) = 8.05
   5 - n = 3.00  ->  n = 2
 oxide A2O2 = AO,   A = +2 (oxidised to +5)
 K2Cr2O7 (n=6): 6 eq / 6 = 1.0 mol   OK
 A: +2 -> +5  => cannot be a group-2 metal
⚡ JEE Trick Equal equivalents, not equal moles, drive every titration. The (c) statement is true only because the oxide and dichromate happen to share the same 6-electron capacity, so they react 1:1 by moles; and (d) fails on one fact alone — group-2 metals have no +5 oxidation state, so A cannot belong there.
Answer: (a), (b) and (c).
2500 mL of a 0.05 M Mohr salt solution required the same volume of permanganate solution for complete oxidation. Which of the followings is (are) true regarding the above redox reaction?
(a) The molarity of KMnO4 must be 0.01 M if the medium is acidic   (b) The molarity of KMnO4 must be 0.0167 M if the medium is basic   (c) The medium has no role to play in redox reaction   (d) In the above reaction Fe(II) is oxidized to Fe(III) irrespective of medium.		Mohr salt permanganate titrationRedox Titration - KMnO4 vs Mohr's salt solution🟡 Similar(a), (b), (d) acid 0.01 M; basic 0.0167 M; Fe(II)->Fe(III)
Concept Mohr salt, FeSO₄·(NH₄)₂SO₄·6H₂O, supplies Fe²⁺ with n = 1 (Fe²⁺ → Fe³⁺). Its equivalents are fixed by the given amount, but the permanganate molarity that matches depends on the medium, because MnO₄⁻ accepts 5 e⁻ in acid yet only 3 e⁻ in neutral or basic solution (→ MnO₂). The reductant's behaviour, however, is the same everywhere.
Key relation eq(Fe²⁺) = eq(MnO₄⁻): V·M·n equal on both sides The two solutions use the same volume, so molarity scales inversely with the n-factor.
Solution
1. mol Fe²⁺ = 0.500 × 0.05 = 0.025; equivalents = 0.025 × 1 = 0.025 eq, i.e. 25 meq.
2. "The same volume of permanganate" means 500 mL of KMnO₄ is used, so the volume cancels between the two sides of the equivalence.
3. Acidic case (n = 5): 0.500 × M × 5 = 0.025, so M = 0.025/2.5 = 0.01 M, which makes (a) correct.
4. Neutral/basic case (n = 3, MnO₄⁻ → MnO₂): 0.500 × M × 3 = 0.025, so M = 0.025/1.5 = 0.0167 M, which makes (b) correct.
5. Because the two required molarities are different, the medium clearly does affect the reaction, so statement (c) is false.
6. Fe²⁺ is the reductant in every case and can only go up to Fe³⁺, so (d) is true regardless of medium.
7. Hence the correct set is (a), (b) and (d); only (c) is rejected.
 eq Fe2+ = 0.5 L x 0.05 x 1 = 0.025 eq
 acid : 0.5 x M x 5 = 0.025 -> M = 0.0100
 basic: 0.5 x M x 3 = 0.025 -> M = 0.0167
 0.01 =/= 0.0167  => medium matters (c no)
 Fe(II) -> Fe(III) in any medium      (d yes)
⚡ JEE Trick Only the oxidant changes its n-factor with pH; the reductant Fe²⁺ keeps n = 1 throughout. The (c) distractor looks plausible until you notice that parts (a) and (b) already give two different molarities — that difference is itself the proof that the medium is decisive, so (c) must be false.
Answer: (a), (b) and (d).
3What is the mass of sodium bromate and molarity of solution to prepare 85.5 mL of 0.672 N solution when half cell reactions are:
(a) BrO3− + 6H+ + 6e− → Br− + 3H2O?
(b) 2BrO3− + 12H+ + 10e− → Br2 + 6H2O?
📝 PYQ: IIT-JEE 1987Solution ↗
Normality equivalent mass bromateQ12. What is the weight of sodium bromate and molarity of solution to prepare 85.5mL of 0.672N solut🟢 Exact(a) 1.446 g (b) 0.122 M ⚠
Concept The normality is fixed at 0.672 N, but the equivalent mass of NaBrO₃ depends on how many electrons BrO₃⁻ accepts in the chosen half-reaction. Reaction (a) takes 6 e⁻ per BrO₃⁻ (n = 6), while reaction (b) takes 10 e⁻ for two BrO₃⁻, i.e. n = 5 each. So the two reactions demand different masses to make the same volume of the same-normality solution, and different molarities.
Key relation eq = N·V, mass = eq × (M/n), molarity = N/n M(NaBrO₃) = 23 + 80 + 48 = 151 g/mol.
Solution
1. Equivalents needed = N × V = 0.672 × 0.0855 = 0.05746 eq; this is the same for either half-reaction.
2. Reaction (a), n = 6: equivalent mass = 151/6 = 25.17, so mass = 0.05746 × 25.17 = 1.446 g.
3. Reaction (a) molarity = N/n = 0.672/6 = 0.112 M (checks out as 1.446 g dissolved in 85.5 mL).
4. Reaction (b), n = 5: equivalent mass = 151/5 = 30.2, so mass = 0.05746 × 30.2 = 1.735 g.
5. Reaction (b) molarity = N/n = 0.672/5 = 0.1344 M.
6. So the complete answer is (a) 1.446 g with 0.112 M, and (b) 1.735 g with 0.1344 M.
7. The key's printed molarity 0.122 M equals neither 0.112 nor 0.1344, so it is a single-digit misprint of 0.112 M, the value that belongs with the 1.446 g mass.
 eq = 0.672 x 0.0855 = 0.05746 eq
 (a) n=6: mass = 0.05746 x 151/6 = 1.446 g
          M = 0.672/6 = 0.112 M
 (b) n=5: mass = 0.05746 x 151/5 = 1.735 g
          M = 0.672/5 = 0.1344 M
 key 0.122 M =/= 0.112 and =/= 0.1344
⚡ JEE Trick Hold N fixed and let n vary: both mass and molarity track the equivalent mass M/n, so a larger n means less mass and lower molarity. Reaction (a) with n = 6 gives 1.446 g and 0.112 M; the key's 0.122 M matches no consistent value and is a misprint of 0.112 M.
Answer: (a) 1.446 g, molarity 0.112 M; (b) 1.735 g, 0.1344 M per the key (a) 1.446 g (b) 0.122 M — my working gives 0.112 M, so 0.122 M is a misprint for 0.112 M ⚠
41.2 g of a commercial sample of oxalic acid was dissolved in 200 mL of water. 10 mL of this sample required 8.5 mL of N/10 KMnO4. Calculate % of purity of sample.Oxalic acid purity titrationDetermine the percentage Purity of 6.3 g impure sample of oxalic acid against M/100 KMnO4🟡 Similar89.25%
Concept An aliquot is titrated, so first find the equivalents of oxalic acid in the 10 mL portion from the KMnO₄ it consumes, then scale up to the whole 200 mL flask, and finally convert equivalents to mass. The purity is that pure mass as a fraction of the 1.2 g taken. The key uses the crystalline dihydrate, equivalent mass 63.
Key relation eq(oxalic) = eq(KMnO₄); mass = eq × E; %purity = (pure/taken)×100 H₂C₂O₄·2H₂O: M = 126, n = 2, so E = 63.
Solution
1. N/10 KMnO₄ means 0.1 N, so meq KMnO₄ used = 8.5 × 0.1 = 0.85 meq.
2. By the equivalence of titration, the 10 mL aliquot therefore holds 0.85 meq of oxalic acid.
3. The whole 200 mL flask holds 0.85 × (200/10) = 17 meq of oxalic acid.
4. Oxalate has n = 2 (C₂O₄²⁻ → 2CO₂); taking the dihydrate, E = 126/2 = 63.
5. Mass of pure oxalic acid = 17 meq × 63 mg/meq = 17 × 0.063 = 1.071 g.
6. % purity = (1.071/1.2) × 100 = 89.25%.
7. Using anhydrous acid (E = 45) would instead give 0.765 g and 63.75%; the key's value confirms the dihydrate is intended.
 N/10 = 0.1 N
 meq KMnO4 = 8.5 x 0.1 = 0.85 meq (10 mL)
 200 mL : 0.85 x (200/10) = 17 meq
 E(oxalic dihydrate) = 126/2 = 63
 pure mass = 17 x 63 / 1000 = 1.071 g
 % = (1.071 / 1.2) x 100 = 89.25 %
⚡ JEE Trick Always scale the titrated aliquot up to the full flask before converting equivalents to mass, and be alert to which oxalic acid is meant. The 89.25% answer only works with the dihydrate (E = 63); the anhydrous form (E = 45) would wrongly read 63.75%, a classic examiner trap.
Answer: 89.25% (oxalic acid taken as the dihydrate, E = 63).
5100 mL of 0.01 M KMnO4 oxidizes 100 mL H2O2 in acidic medium. Volume of same KMnO4 required in alkaline medium to oxidize 100 mL of same H2O2 will be
(a) 100/3 mL   (b) 500/3 mL   (c) 300/5 mL   (d) None of these		KMnO4 H2O2 medium effectThe reaction of aqueous KMnO4 with H2O2 in acidic | NEET 2014 Redox Reactions and Electrochemistry🟡 Similar(b) 500/3 mL
Concept As a reductant H₂O₂ always has n = 2 (its O⁻¹ goes to O⁰, releasing O₂), so the equivalents in the 100 mL of peroxide are fixed by the first (acidic) run. In alkaline medium KMnO₄ drops from n = 5 to n = 3 (MnO₄⁻ → MnO₂), so it delivers fewer equivalents per mole and a larger volume is required to supply the same total. Equivalents are the universal currency of titration: once they are pinned to the peroxide, the volume adjusts purely through the oxidant's n-factor.
Key relation eq(H₂O₂) = eq(KMnO₄); so V₂ = V₁ × (n₁/n₂) Only the oxidant's n-factor changes with the medium; the peroxide's is constant.
Solution
1. Acidic KMnO₄ has n = 5, so meq used = 100 × 0.01 × 5 = 5 meq.
2. These 5 meq equal the equivalents of the 100 mL of H₂O₂, which is a fixed amount of reductant.
3. In alkaline medium KMnO₄ is reduced only to MnO₂, so n = 3.
4. To supply the same 5 meq: V × 0.01 × 3 = 5 meq.
5. V = 5 / 0.03 = 166.7 mL = 500/3 mL.
6. Equivalently V = 100 × (n₁/n₂) = 100 × (5/3) = 500/3 mL, which is option (b).
7. Since n fell from 5 to 3, the volume rises by the factor 5/3, exactly as found.
In symbols, meq = V(mL) × M × n is held equal for both runs, with H₂O₂ fixed at 5 meq.
So the acidic 100 mL becomes alkaline 500/3 ≈ 166.7 mL, a 5:3 increase purely from the n-factor drop.
 acid : meq = 100 x 0.01 x 5 = 5 meq
 same 100 mL H2O2  => still 5 meq to react
 alkaline: KMnO4 -> MnO2, n = 3
   V x 0.01 x 3 = 5
   V = 5 / 0.03 = 166.7 = 500/3 mL
⚡ JEE Trick Hold the reductant's equivalents constant and simply rescale by the oxidant's n-factor: V₂ = V₁ × (n₁/n₂) = 100 × 5/3. Option (a) 100/3 inverts the ratio, and (c) 300/5 = 60 mL ignores the chemistry entirely, so both are traps. A quick sanity check: a smaller n always demands a larger volume for the same equivalents, so the answer must exceed the original 100 mL — which rules out (a) at once.
Answer: (b) 500/3 mL.
6Manganese ions (Mn2+) can be oxidized by persulphate ions (S2O82−) according to the following equation:
Mn2+ + S2O82− → SO42− + MnO4− (Acidic medium)
How many moles of S2O82− are required to oxidize 1 mole of Mn2+?
(a) 2.5   (b) 2.0   (c) 1.0   (d) 0.4		Persulphate manganese mole ratioManganese ions (Mn^(2+)) can be oxidised by Persulphate ions S_(2)O_(8)^(2-)🟢 Exact(a) 2.5
Concept In any redox stoichiometry the electrons lost by the reductant must equal the electrons gained by the oxidant. Here Mn²⁺ → MnO₄⁻ is an oxidation in which Mn loses 5 electrons, while persulphate S₂O₈²⁻ → 2SO₄²⁻ is a reduction of its peroxide O–O link that gains 2 electrons. Sulphur itself never changes oxidation state, which is the key subtlety. Spotting that the oxidising power of persulphate comes entirely from its O–O peroxide bond is the whole insight; the two sulphurs are passive at +6 before and after.
Key relation mol oxidant = (e⁻ lost by reductant)/(e⁻ gained per oxidant) In S₂O₈²⁻ sulphur stays +6; the two peroxide oxygens (−1) are reduced to −2.
Solution
1. Mn²⁺ → MnO₄⁻: manganese goes +2 → +7, losing 5 electrons per Mn.
2. In S₂O₈²⁻ each S is +6 and two oxygens form a peroxide bond at −1.
3. S₂O₈²⁻ + 2e⁻ → 2SO₄²⁻: the ion accepts 2 electrons, so its n-factor is 2.
4. Total electrons released by 1 mol Mn²⁺ = 5 mol e⁻.
5. mol S₂O₈²⁻ required = (electrons needed)/(2 per ion) = 5/2 = 2.5.
6. Check: 2.5 × 2 = 5 electrons gained, which matches the 5 electrons lost, so the balance holds.
7. Hence the ratio is 2.5 mol persulphate per mol Mn²⁺, option (a).
In equivalents: 1 mol Mn²⁺ = 5 eq of reductant demand, and each S₂O₈²⁻ supplies 2 eq.
So mol S₂O₈²⁻ = 5 eq / 2 eq per mol = 2.5 mol, consistent with the electron count above.
 Mn2+ -> MnO4- : lose 5 e- per Mn
 S2O8(2-) + 2 e- -> 2 SO4(2-) : n = 2
 mol S2O8 = 5 / 2 = 2.5
 check: 2.5 x 2 = 5 e-   balanced
⚡ JEE Trick Don't let the large formula mislead you: sulphur keeps +6 throughout, and only the peroxide bond takes 2 electrons. Option (b) 2.0 comes from wrongly assigning Mn an n of 2.5, and (d) 0.4 simply inverts the correct ratio, so both are distractors. Remember the peroxide rule for S₂O₈²⁻, H₂O₂ and O₂²⁻: the oxygens, not the central atom, carry the redox change, so the n-factor is 2 here.
Answer: (a) 2.5.
7When white phosphorus (P4) is boiled in alkaline medium, it disproportionates as
P4 + NaOH → PH3 + NaH2PO2.
In the given redox reaction, NaH2PO2 and PH3 are produced in the molar ratio of
(a) 1 : 1   (b) 1 : 3   (c) 3 : 1   (d) 2 : 1		Phosphorus disproportionation ratioRedox Balance P4 + OH- = PH3 + H2PO2- || OXIDATION NUMBER Method🟡 Similar(c) 3 : 1
Concept This is a disproportionation: phosphorus in P₄ (oxidation state 0) goes down to −3 in PH₃ (reduction) and up to +1 in NaH₂PO₂ (oxidation). The electrons released by the oxidised phosphorus must equal the electrons absorbed by the reduced phosphorus, and that balance fixes the molar ratio of the two products directly. Disproportionation always splits one element into a higher and a lower oxidation state from a single intermediate one, and the electron budget alone decides the amounts.
Key relation (e⁻ lost per oxidised P) × mol oxidised = (e⁻ gained per reduced P) × mol reduced Each NaH₂PO₂ loses 1 e⁻; each PH₃ gains 3 e⁻.
Solution
1. In P₄ phosphorus is 0, the state of the free element.
2. In PH₃ phosphorus is −3, so forming one PH₃ consumes 3 electrons.
3. In NaH₂PO₂ (hypophosphite) phosphorus is +1, so forming one releases 1 electron.
4. Electron balance: 1 × mol(NaH₂PO₂) = 3 × mol(PH₃).
5. Rearranging, mol(NaH₂PO₂) : mol(PH₃) = 3 : 1.
6. The fully balanced equation P₄ + 3NaOH + 3H₂O → 3NaH₂PO₂ + PH₃ confirms exactly this ratio.
7. So three moles of NaH₂PO₂ form for every one mole of PH₃, option (c).
Writing it as equivalents: electrons supplied by oxidation = electrons taken up by reduction.
That equality forces three hypophosphite units for each phosphine, independent of how P₄ is apportioned.
 P4 (0) -> PH3 (-3)     gain 3 e- each
          NaH2PO2 (+1)  lose 1 e- each
 balance: 1 x NaH2PO2 = 3 x PH3
 P4 + 3NaOH + 3H2O -> 3NaH2PO2 + PH3
 ratio NaH2PO2 : PH3 = 3 : 1
⚡ JEE Trick In a disproportionation the product whose oxidation-state change is larger is formed in the smaller amount. PH₃ swings by 3 units and NaH₂PO₂ by only 1, so NaH₂PO₂ is three times as plentiful — ratio 3:1, the exact opposite of the tempting 1:3 in option (b). A fast check: the element moving further from zero (P→−3 in PH₃, a swing of 3) is always the minority product, so NaH₂PO₂ : PH₃ must be 3 : 1.
Answer: (c) 3 : 1.
8Iodate ions (IO3−) can be reduced to iodine by iodide ions. The equation which represented a redox reaction is
IO3−(aq) + I−(aq) + 6H+(aq) → I2(s) + 3H2O(l)
How many moles of iodine are produced for every mole of iodate ions consumed in the reaction?
(a) 1   (b) 2.5   (c) 3   (d) 5		Iodate iodide redox balanceIodate ions can be reduced to iodine by iodide ions as per the given redox reaction in acidic medium🟢 Exact(c) 3
Concept The equation as printed is only a skeleton; it must be balanced by electrons before the yield can be read. This is a comproportionation: iodate (I = +5) is reduced and iodide (I = −1) is oxidised, and both meet at I₂ (0). Counting the electrons fixes how many iodide ions react and hence how many I₂ form per iodate. Here the same element iodine is both oxidised and reduced to a common product, the hallmark of comproportionation, so every iodine atom in the products must be tallied.
Key relation 5 e⁻ (gained per IO₃⁻) = 1 e⁻ (lost per I⁻) × (mol I⁻) Every iodine atom ends up in I₂, so all iodine atoms must be counted, not just the iodate's.
Solution
1. Reduction half: IO₃⁻ (+5) + 5e⁻ → I⁰, gaining 5 electrons.
2. Oxidation half: I⁻ (−1) → I⁰, losing 1 electron each.
3. Electron balance therefore needs 5 I⁻ for every 1 IO₃⁻.
4. Total iodine atoms = 1 (from iodate) + 5 (from iodide) = 6 atoms, i.e. 3 molecules of I₂.
5. The balanced equation is IO₃⁻ + 5I⁻ + 6H⁺ → 3I₂ + 3H₂O.
6. So 3 mol I₂ are produced per mol IO₃⁻ consumed, option (c).
7. The 6H⁺ and 3H₂O also balance (6 O from iodate side go to 3 water), confirming the coefficients.
In equivalents: the one iodate carries 5 eq of oxidising capacity, matched by 5 eq from five iodides.
Those six iodine atoms (one reduced, five oxidised) combine as three I₂ molecules per iodate.
 IO3- (+5) + 5 e- -> I(0)
 5 I- (-1) -> 5 I(0) + 5 e-
 I atoms: 1 + 5 = 6 = 3 I2
 IO3- + 5 I- + 6 H+ -> 3 I2 + 3 H2O
⚡ JEE Trick The 6H⁺ already hints the iodide coefficient is 5, because 3 oxygens leaving the iodate make 3 H₂O and need 6 H⁺. Count every iodine atom, both the iodate's and the iodide's: 6 atoms make 3 I₂. Option (a) 1 counts only the iodate's own iodine and misses the five oxidised iodides. The water and acid coefficients give a second check: 3 O leave the iodate as 3 H₂O, needing 6 H⁺ — exactly the stoichiometry printed, confirming 3 I₂.
Answer: (c) 3.
9Sodium hypochlorite (NaOCl) is a good oxidizing agent in alkaline medium. What volume of a 0.15 M alkaline solution of NaOCl would be required to oxidize completely a 20 mL 0.2 M NaCrO2 solution according to the reaction:
NaOCl + NaCrO2 + NaOH → NaCl + Na2CrO4 + H2O
(a) 80 mL   (b) 40 mL   (c) 20 mL   (d) 10 mL		Hypochlorite chromite titrationN FACTOR IN A REDOX REACTION AND TITRATION | DISPROPORTIONATION | JEE | NEET | CHEMISTRY BHAIYA🟡 Similar(b) 40 mL
Concept At the end point the equivalents of oxidant (NaOCl) equal the equivalents of reductant (NaCrO₂). Chromium is oxidised from Cr³⁺ in chromite to Cr⁶⁺ in chromate (n = 3), while the hypochlorite chlorine is reduced from Cl⁺¹ to Cl⁻¹ (n = 2). Setting the two equivalent amounts equal gives the required volume. The entire problem hinges on reading hidden oxidation states out of the chromite and chromate formulae; once those are right the equivalents balance is routine.
Key relation V·M·n (NaOCl) = V·M·n (NaCrO₂) In NaCrO₂ (CrO₂⁻) chromium is +3; in Na₂CrO₄ (CrO₄²⁻) it is +6.
Solution
1. In NaCrO₂ chromium is +3; in the product Na₂CrO₄ it is +6, so the change is 3 electrons, n = 3.
2. meq of NaCrO₂ = 20 × 0.2 × 3 = 12 meq.
3. In NaOCl chlorine is +1 and is reduced to Cl⁻ (−1), so its n-factor is 2.
4. The oxidant must furnish the same 12 meq, so meq(NaOCl) = 12.
5. V × 0.15 × 2 = 12, giving V = 12 / 0.30 = 40 mL.
6. This is option (b); option (c) 20 mL would follow only if both n-factors were wrongly taken equal.
7. The 3-vs-2 mismatch in n-factors is exactly what makes the required volume differ from a naive 1:1 guess.
In equivalents the two sides are equal: 12 meq of chromite demand 12 meq of hypochlorite.
Dividing by the oxidant's own capacity, V = 12 meq / (0.15 × 2) = 40 mL.
 NaCrO2: Cr +3 -> +6, n = 3
 meq = 20 x 0.2 x 3 = 12 meq
 NaOCl: Cl +1 -> -1, n = 2
 V x 0.15 x 2 = 12
 V = 12 / 0.30 = 40 mL
⚡ JEE Trick Read the oxidation states straight off the formulae: CrO₂⁻ hides a +3 chromium and CrO₄²⁻ a +6 one, so the n-factor is 3, not 1. The mismatch of n-factors (3 vs 2) is the heart of the problem; assuming equal moles gives the trap answer (c) 20 mL. Sanity check by molarity: NaOCl (0.15 M) is less concentrated than NaCrO₂ (0.2 M) yet carries more electrons per mole (2 vs the 3 of chromite), so a moderate 40 mL is expected, not the 10 mL of (d).
Answer: (b) 40 mL.
10Consider the redox reaction:
Cu2S + KMnO4 + H2SO4 → CuSO4 + MnSO4 + K2SO4 + H2O
Moles of KMnO4 reduced by one mole of Cu2S are
(a) 0.5   (b) 1.5   (c) 2.0   (d) 3.0		Cu2S permanganate n-factorFind the number of moles of KMnO4 needed to oxidise one mole Cu2S in acidic medium🟢 Exact(c) 2.0
Concept Cu₂S is a double reductant: when it dissolves, both the copper and the sulphur are oxidised. The n-factor of Cu₂S is therefore the sum of the electrons lost by both, and dividing that total by the n-factor of acidified KMnO₄ (n = 5) gives the moles of permanganate reduced per mole of Cu₂S. The examiner's trap is to treat Cu₂S as a one-electron copper source and ignore sulphur, so the discipline here is to add up every oxidation that actually occurs.
Key relation mol KMnO₄ = n(Cu₂S) / n(KMnO₄) Cu⁺¹ → Cu²⁺ (in CuSO₄) and S²⁻ → S⁺⁶ (in SO₄²⁻) both occur.
Solution
1. In Cu₂S copper is +1 and sulphur is −2.
2. The two Cu go +1 → +2, losing 2 × 1 = 2 electrons.
3. The sulphur goes −2 → +6 (as SO₄²⁻), losing 8 electrons.
4. So the n-factor of Cu₂S = 2 + 8 = 10 electrons per formula unit.
5. Acidified KMnO₄ gains 5 electrons each (Mn⁺⁷ → Mn²⁺), so n = 5.
6. mol KMnO₄ = 10 / 5 = 2.0, which is option (c).
7. This matches the equivalents principle: one mole of Cu₂S supplies 10 equivalents, met by 2 moles of a 5-equivalent oxidant.
In equivalents: 1 mol Cu₂S offers 10 eq, and acidic KMnO₄ accepts 5 eq per mole.
So mol KMnO₄ = 10 eq / 5 eq per mol = 2.0 mol, matching the electron tally.
 Cu2S: 2 Cu (+1 -> +2) = 2 e-
        S  (-2 -> +6)  = 8 e-
 n(Cu2S) = 2 + 8 = 10
 KMnO4 (acid) n = 5
 mol KMnO4 = 10 / 5 = 2.0
⚡ JEE Trick Never forget the sulphur: counting only the copper gives n = 2 and leads to the trap answers (a) 0.5 or (d) 0.4. Both copper and sulphur feed electrons, so the true n-factor is 10 and exactly 2 mol of KMnO₄ are reduced per mole of Cu₂S. Whenever a sulphide or sulphosalt is oxidised, check both the metal and the sulphur; forgetting sulphur's −2→+6 jump (8 electrons) is the single most common slip and leads straight to the wrong 0.4 or 0.5.
Answer: (c) 2.0.
11 (i)Passage Q. (i) to (iv)
Equivalent mass = (Molecular mass/Atomic mass) / n-factor
n-factor is very important in redox as well as non-redox reactions. With the help of n-factor we can predict the molar ratio of the reactant species taking part in reactions. The reciprocal of n-factor's ratio of the reactants is the molar ratio of the reactants.
In general n-factor of acid/base is number of moles of H+/OH− furnished per mole of acid/base. n-factor of a reactant is number of moles of electrons lost or gained per mole of reactant.
Example 1: (1) In acidic medium: KMnO4 (n = 5) → Mn2+   (2) In neutral medium: KMnO4 (n = 3) → Mn2+   (3) In basic medium: KMnO4 (n = 1) → Mn6+
Example 2: FeC2O4 → Fe3+ + 2CO2. Total number of moles of e− lost by 1 mole of FeC2O4 = 1 + 1×2 ⇒ 3
(i) n-factor of Ba(MnO4)2 in acidic medium is:
(a) 2   (b) 6   (c) 10   (d) None of these		n-factor permanganateWhat will be n-factor for Ba(MnO_(4))_(2) in acidic medium? (Where it behaves as oxidant)🟢 Exact(c) 10
Concept The n-factor of a salt is the total number of electrons its reactive units exchange per formula unit. Ba(MnO₄)₂ contains two permanganate units, and in acid each one is reduced Mn⁺⁷ → Mn²⁺, taking 5 electrons. The barium is only a spectator, so the whole formula takes 2 × 5 electrons. The passage defines n-factor as electrons exchanged per mole of reactant, and for a salt that means summing over every reactive anion it releases.
Key relation n(salt) = (number of reactive units) × (e⁻ per unit) Ba²⁺ does not change its oxidation state; only the two MnO₄⁻ react.
Solution
1. Ba(MnO₄)₂ dissociates into Ba²⁺ + 2 MnO₄⁻.
2. In acidic medium each MnO₄⁻ → Mn²⁺, with Mn going +7 → +2, a gain of 5 electrons.
3. There are 2 such permanganate units in one formula unit of the salt.
4. So the total electron change = 2 × 5 = 10, which is the n-factor.
5. Ba²⁺ stays +2 and contributes no electrons.
6. Hence option (c) 10; option (a) 2 counts only the number of units, and (b) 6 would be the neutral-medium figure (2 × 3).
7. Equivalently, equivalent mass = molar mass / 10 for this salt in acid.
Equivalent mass of the salt = molar mass / 10, a direct use of the passage's formula.
The barium cation is a mere spectator, so it neither adds to nor subtracts from the count of 10.
 Ba(MnO4)2 -> Ba2+ + 2 MnO4-
 acid: each MnO4- (+7 -> +2) = 5 e-
 two units: n = 2 x 5 = 10
 (neutral would be 2 x 3 = 6)
⚡ JEE Trick For a salt, multiply the electrons exchanged per reactive ion by how many such ions sit in the formula. Two MnO₄⁻ at 5 e⁻ each give 10. Option (a) 2 forgets the 5-electron change altogether, while (b) 6 slips in the neutral-medium value of 3. Always expand a salt into its reacting ions first: two permanganates means the n-factor doubles from the familiar 5 to 10, which only option (c) reflects.
Answer: (c) 10.
11 (ii)Passage Q. (i) to (iv)
Equivalent mass = (Molecular mass/Atomic mass) / n-factor
n-factor is very important in redox as well as non-redox reactions. With the help of n-factor we can predict the molar ratio of the reactant species taking part in reactions. The reciprocal of n-factor's ratio of the reactants is the molar ratio of the reactants.
In general n-factor of acid/base is number of moles of H+/OH− furnished per mole of acid/base. n-factor of a reactant is number of moles of electrons lost or gained per mole of reactant.
Example 1: (1) In acidic medium: KMnO4 (n = 5) → Mn2+   (2) In neutral medium: KMnO4 (n = 3) → Mn2+   (3) In basic medium: KMnO4 (n = 1) → Mn6+
Example 2: FeC2O4 → Fe3+ + 2CO2. Total number of moles of e− lost by 1 mole of FeC2O4 = 1 + 1×2 ⇒ 3
(ii) Consider the following reaction:
H3PO2 + NaOH → NaH2PO2 + H2O
What is the equivalent mass of H3PO2? (mol. wt. is M)
(a) M   (b) M/2   (c) M/3   (d) None of these		Equivalent mass hypophosphorousEquivalent weight of HypoPhosphorous Acid//Equivalent Weight🟡 Similar(a) M
Concept In a neutralisation the equivalent mass of an acid is its molar mass divided by its basicity, i.e. the number of ionisable H⁺. H₃PO₂ (hypophosphorous acid) looks tribasic from its formula, but structurally only one hydrogen sits on oxygen; the other two are bonded directly to phosphorus (P–H) and are not acidic. The passage ties equivalent mass to n-factor, and for an acid that n-factor is its basicity — the count of hydrogens it can actually donate, read from structure not formula.
Key relation E = M / basicity = M / (replaceable H⁺) The product NaH₂PO₂ shows only one H has been replaced by Na.
Solution
1. The product is NaH₂PO₂, so exactly one H of H₃PO₂ is replaced by sodium.
2. The structure is HO–P(H)₂=O: one O–H (acidic) and two P–H (non-acidic).
3. Only the O–H ionises, so the basicity = 1.
4. Equivalent mass E = M / 1 = M.
5. The two P–H hydrogens never leave as H⁺, so they do not reduce E.
6. Hence option (a) M; options M/2 and M/3 wrongly assume 2 or 3 acidic hydrogens.
7. This is why H₃PO₂ behaves as a monoprotic acid despite having three hydrogens.
Using the passage's relation, E = M / n with n = basicity = 1 gives E = M.
The single reaction with NaOH replacing just one H confirms the acid is monoprotic.
 H3PO2 + NaOH -> NaH2PO2 + H2O
 structure: 1 O-H (acidic), 2 P-H (not)
 basicity = 1
 E = M / 1 = M
⚡ JEE Trick Count acidic hydrogens from the structure, never from the molecular formula. Across H₃PO₂, H₃PO₃, H₃PO₄ the basicities are 1, 2, 3 respectively because of the P–H bonds. So hypophosphorous acid is monobasic and E = M, not the tempting M/3. The P–H bonds in H₃PO₂ are the trap: they look like acidic hydrogens in the formula but never ionise, so basicity is 1 and E = M, not M/3.
Answer: (a) M.
11 (iii)Passage Q. (i) to (iv)
Equivalent mass = (Molecular mass/Atomic mass) / n-factor
n-factor is very important in redox as well as non-redox reactions. With the help of n-factor we can predict the molar ratio of the reactant species taking part in reactions. The reciprocal of n-factor's ratio of the reactants is the molar ratio of the reactants.
In general n-factor of acid/base is number of moles of H+/OH− furnished per mole of acid/base. n-factor of a reactant is number of moles of electrons lost or gained per mole of reactant.
Example 1: (1) In acidic medium: KMnO4 (n = 5) → Mn2+   (2) In neutral medium: KMnO4 (n = 3) → Mn2+   (3) In basic medium: KMnO4 (n = 1) → Mn6+
Example 2: FeC2O4 → Fe3+ + 2CO2. Total number of moles of e− lost by 1 mole of FeC2O4 = 1 + 1×2 ⇒ 3
(iii) For the reaction, Fe0.95O (molar mass = M) → Fe2O3, what is the eq. mass of Fe0.95O?
(a) M/0.85   (b) M/0.95   (c) M/0.8075   (d) None of these		Equivalent mass wustiteSupereasy Tricks to find n factor|n factor for more than 1element| Equivalent weight|Redox Reaction🟡 Similar(a) M/0.85
Concept Equivalent mass = molar mass ÷ n-factor, and the n-factor is the electrons lost per formula unit when Fe₀.₉₅O (wüstite) is oxidised to Fe₂O₃, where every iron becomes +3. A charge balance on the non-stoichiometric oxide first fixes the total iron charge before oxidation. Non-stoichiometric oxides like wüstite force you to use fractional oxidation states, but the passage's n-factor rule (electrons per mole) still applies cleanly once the charge balance is done.
Key relation n = (total Fe charge after) − (total Fe charge before) A single O²⁻ forces the iron in Fe₀.₉₅O to carry +2 in total.
Solution
1. In Fe₀.₉₅O oxygen is −2, so the 0.95 Fe atoms together carry +2 (to keep the unit neutral).
2. After oxidation every Fe is +3, so the 0.95 Fe carry 0.95 × 3 = +2.85.
3. Electrons lost per formula unit = 2.85 − 2.00 = 0.85.
4. So the n-factor of Fe₀.₉₅O in this change = 0.85.
5. Equivalent mass = M / n = M / 0.85.
6. Hence option (a) M/0.85; the distractor M/0.95 divides by the atom count instead of the electron change.
7. The average Fe state here is 2/0.95 ≈ +2.11, a mix of Fe²⁺ and Fe³⁺, consistent with the fractional n-factor.
Applying E = M / n with n = 0.85 gives the equivalent mass M/0.85.
The fractional n-factor reflects that only part of the iron (the Fe²⁺ fraction) is actually oxidised to +3.
 Fe(0.95)O : O = -2  => Fe total = +2
 after: 0.95 Fe x (+3) = +2.85
 e- lost = 2.85 - 2.00 = 0.85
 E = M / 0.85
⚡ JEE Trick The n-factor is a change in total charge, not a count of atoms. Work out the metal's total charge before (+2) and after (+2.85); the difference 0.85 is the n-factor. The trap M/0.95 lures you into dividing by the number of iron atoms instead. Fix the total metal charge from the oxide's neutrality (+2 here), then subtract from the oxidised total (+2.85); the gap 0.85 is the n-factor, never the raw atom count 0.95.
Answer: (a) M/0.85.
11 (iv)Passage Q. (i) to (iv)
Equivalent mass = (Molecular mass/Atomic mass) / n-factor
n-factor is very important in redox as well as non-redox reactions. With the help of n-factor we can predict the molar ratio of the reactant species taking part in reactions. The reciprocal of n-factor's ratio of the reactants is the molar ratio of the reactants.
In general n-factor of acid/base is number of moles of H+/OH− furnished per mole of acid/base. n-factor of a reactant is number of moles of electrons lost or gained per mole of reactant.
Example 1: (1) In acidic medium: KMnO4 (n = 5) → Mn2+   (2) In neutral medium: KMnO4 (n = 3) → Mn2+   (3) In basic medium: KMnO4 (n = 1) → Mn6+
Example 2: FeC2O4 → Fe3+ + 2CO2. Total number of moles of e− lost by 1 mole of FeC2O4 = 1 + 1×2 ⇒ 3
(iv) In the reaction, xVO + yFe2O3 → FeO + V2O5, what is the value of x and y respectively?
(a) 1, 1   (b) 2, 3   (c) 3, 2   (d) None of these		Redox equation balancingBalance: VO + Fe2O3 -> V2O5 + FeO || How to balance complex Chemical Equations🟢 Exact(b) 2, 3
Concept To balance xVO + yFe₂O₃ → FeO + V₂O₅, match electrons. Vanadium is oxidised V²⁺ → V⁵⁺ (loses 3 e⁻ each), and iron is reduced Fe³⁺ → Fe²⁺ (gains 1 e⁻ each). Equating the total electrons lost and gained gives the smallest whole-number coefficients x and y. The passage notes that the reciprocal of the n-factor ratio is the mole ratio, which is exactly the electron-balance rule used to fix x and y here.
Key relation 3 × (mol V oxidised) = 1 × (mol Fe reduced) In VO, V is +2; in V₂O₅ it is +5. In Fe₂O₃, Fe is +3; in FeO it is +2.
Solution
1. VO → V₂O₅: vanadium goes +2 → +5, losing 3 electrons per V atom.
2. Fe₂O₃ → FeO: each Fe goes +3 → +2, gaining 1 electron; a unit of Fe₂O₃ has 2 Fe, so it gains 2.
3. For x VO and y Fe₂O₃ the electron balance is 3x = 2y.
4. The smallest integers satisfying this are x = 2, y = 3 (3 × 2 = 2 × 3 = 6 electrons exchanged).
5. This gives the full equation 2VO + 3Fe₂O₃ → 6FeO + V₂O₅.
6. Oxygen check: left 2 + 9 = 11, right 6 + 5 = 11, so it balances.
7. Therefore x = 2 and y = 3, which is option (b).
The n-factors are 3 (for V) and 2 (for each Fe₂O₃), so the mole ratio V:Fe₂O₃ = 2:3, the reciprocal.
That reciprocal rule from the passage gives x:y = 2:3 directly, with no trial and error.
 V: +2 -> +5  lose 3 e- (x atoms)
 Fe2O3: 2 Fe (+3 -> +2) gain 2 e- (y units)
 3x = 2y  ->  x = 2, y = 3
 2VO + 3Fe2O3 -> 6FeO + V2O5
 O check: 2 + 9 = 6 + 5 = 11
⚡ JEE Trick Settle total electrons lost = total gained before worrying about oxygen: 3x = 2y gives x:y = 2:3 at once. The oxygen balance (11 = 11) is only a confirmation, and option (c) 3,2 merely swaps the two coefficients, so it is a trap. Reading the passage's own shortcut: swap the n-factors to get the mole ratio, 3 and 2 become 2 and 3, so x = 2, y = 3 — the reversed pair (c) 3,2 is the deliberate trap.
Answer: (b) 2, 3.

1Bleaching powder reacts with iodide ion according to the following unbalanced equation:
OCl− + I− + H+ ⇄ I2 + Cl− + H2O
A 0.6000 g sample of bleaching powder requires 35.24 mL of 0.1084 N Na2S2O3 to titrate the liberated iodine. The percentage of Cl in the sample is
(a) 22.58% Cl   (b) 5.15% Cl   (c) 11.29% Cl   (d) 45.16% Cl		Bleaching powder iodometryDetermination of Percentage (%) Available Chlorine in Bleaching Powder |iodometry titrations |viva🟡 Similar(c) 11.29% Cl
Concept Iodometry works as a chain of equal equivalents: the bleaching powder's available chlorine liberates I₂ from iodide, and the I₂ is then titrated by thiosulphate. Because equivalents are conserved at every step, the equivalents of thiosulphate equal the equivalents of available chlorine, which can be turned straight into a mass and then a percentage. The elegance of iodometry is that the intermediate I₂ cancels out: its equivalents equal those of both the chlorine that made it and the thiosulphate that consumes it.
Key relation eq(S₂O₃²⁻) = eq(I₂) = eq(available Cl) In OCl⁻ chlorine is +1 and is reduced to Cl⁻ (−1), so n = 2 and E(Cl) = 35.45/2 = 17.73.
Solution
1. meq of Na₂S₂O₃ = 35.24 × 0.1084 = 3.820 meq.
2. By conservation of equivalents, the available chlorine also amounts to 3.820 meq.
3. The chlorine in OCl⁻ changes +1 → −1, so its n-factor is 2 and E = 35.45/2 = 17.73.
4. Mass of chlorine = 3.820 × 17.73 / 1000 = 0.06773 g.
5. % Cl = (0.06773 / 0.6000) × 100 = 11.29%.
6. So the answer is option (c); the figure 22.58% arises from wrongly taking n = 1 (E = 35.45).
7. Note the thiosulphate itself has n = 1, but the available chlorine has n = 2, which is what sets the mass.
In symbols, eq Cl = N × V (thiosulphate) = 0.1084 × 35.24 = 3.820 meq, independent of the I₂ amount.
Converting, mass Cl = 3.820 × 17.73 mg = 67.7 mg, i.e. 11.29% of the 600 mg sample.
 meq S2O3 = 35.24 x 0.1084 = 3.820 meq
 available Cl = 3.820 meq (eq conserved)
 E(Cl) = 35.45 / 2 = 17.73
 mass Cl = 3.820 x 17.73 / 1000 = 0.0677 g
 % = (0.0677 / 0.6000) x 100 = 11.29 %
⚡ JEE Trick Equivalents are conserved down the whole iodometric chain, so you never need the amount of I₂ explicitly. The crucial n-factor is 2 for the available chlorine (+1 → −1); taking n = 1 doubles the result to the trap answer 22.58% in option (a).
Answer: (c) 11.29% Cl.
2When acidified K2Cr2O7 solution is added to Na2S solution, green Cr3+ ions and free S are formed. When acidified K2Cr2O7 solution is added to NaCl, no change occurs. Of the substances involved in these reactions, which one is the best reducing agent?
(a) K2Cr2O7   (b) Na2S   (c) Cr3+   (d) S		Best reducing agentOxidizing and Reducing Agents | Easy Trick🟡 Similar(b) Na2S
Concept The best reducing agent is the species most easily oxidised. In a redox pair the reductant loses electrons (its oxidation number rises); the oxidant is reduced. Compare how two possible reductants, S2− and Cl−, behave toward the same oxidant, acidified dichromate.
Key relation reductant → oxidised form + n e−; a stronger reductant is oxidised by an oxidant that fails on a weaker one Cr2O72− oxidises S2− but not Cl−, ranking the two.
Solution
1. Acidified K2Cr2O7 + Na2S: Cr goes +6 → +3 (reduced) and S goes −2 → 0 (oxidised). So S2− acts as the reducing agent here. It hands electrons to Cr(VI).
2. Therefore K2Cr2O7 is the oxidising agent (it is reduced), so it cannot be the reducing agent — (a) is out.
3. Acidified K2Cr2O7 + NaCl: no change, so dichromate cannot oxidise Cl−. Cl− is too weak a reductant to be touched by Cr(VI).
4. Hence reducing power S2− > Cl−: the one that is oxidised beats the one that is not.
5. Cr3+ (d-is product, already-reduced oxidant) and S (already-oxidised sulfur) are end states, not the active reductant — (c), (d) out.
6. The only species that actually donates electrons is Na2S. Answer (b).
 acidified K2Cr2O7 + Na2S:
   Cr  +6 -> +3  (reduced)   oxidant = Cr2O7^2-
   S   -2 ->  0  (oxidized)  reductant = S2-
 acidified K2Cr2O7 + NaCl: no reaction
   Cl- not oxidized -> weak reductant
 -----------------------------------------
 S2- reduces Cr(VI), Cl- cannot -> Na2S best
⚡ JEE Trick Read the two reactions as a strength test: whatever an oxidant can oxidise outranks whatever it cannot. The trap is picking S or Cr3+ because they appear green/elemental; those are products, not reducers.
Answer: (b) Na2S.
3A 1.5 g sample containing oxalic acid dihydrate (H2C2O4·2H2O), sodium hydrogen oxalate (NaHC2O4) and inert impurities was dissolved in water and the solution was separated into two equal parts by volume. One part required 65 mL of a 0.1 M NaOH solution while the other part required 18 mL of a 0.1 M acidified potassium permanganate solution to reach the end point. What is the mass percentage of NaHC2O4 in original sample?
(a) 62.26%   (b) 31.13%   (c) 37.34%   (d) 18.67%		Oxalate mixture analysisA mixture of NaHC2O4 and KHC2O4·H2C2O4 required equal volumes of 0.2N KMnO4 and 0.12 N NaOH....🟡 Similar(c) 37.34%
Concept Two independent titrations on identical halves. NaOH counts acidic protons: H2C2O4 gives 2, NaHC2O4 gives 1. KMnO4 counts oxalate units: each formula has one C2O4 (n = 2). Two equations fix the two unknowns.
Key relation NaOH: 2a + b = mmol OH−; KMnO4: 2(a + b) = 5·(M·V)KMnO4 a, b = mmol of acid and salt per half.
Solution
1. mmol NaOH = 0.1 × 65 = 6.5, so 2a + b = 6.5.
2. Oxalate equivalents = KMnO4 equivalents = 5 × 0.1 × 18 = 9.0 meq.
3. Each oxalate is n = 2, so 2(a + b) = 9.0, giving a + b = 4.5 mmol.
4. Subtract: (2a + b) − (a + b) = a = 6.5 − 4.5 = 2.0 mmol; then b = 4.5 − 2.0 = 2.5 mmol. b = NaHC2O4 per half.
5. Whole sample = 2 halves, so NaHC2O4 = 2 × 2.5 = 5.0 mmol.
6. M(NaHC2O4) = 23 + 1 + 24 + 64 = 112; mass = 5.0 × 112 = 560 mg = 0.56 g.
7. % = 0.56 × 100 / 1.5 = 37.33% ≈ 37.34%.
 per half:  2a + b = 6.5   (NaOH, protons)
            2(a+b) = 9.0   (KMnO4, oxalate)
   -> a+b = 4.5 ;  a = 2.0 ,  b = 2.5 mmol
 sample NaHC2O4 = 2 x 2.5 = 5.0 mmol
 mass = 5.0 x 112 = 560 mg = 0.56 g
 % = 100 x 0.56 / 1.5 = 37.33 %   <-- (c)
⚡ JEE Trick KMnO4 sees total oxalate (a + b), NaOH sees protons (2a + b); their difference isolates a in one line. Trap: forgetting to double back from a half to the whole sample.
Answer: (c) 37.34%.
4A 100 mL Na2CrO4 solution was prepared by dissolving solid Na2CrO4 in alkali solution. A 10 mL portion of this solution required 15 mL of a 0.015 M Na2C2O4 solution to reach the end point. A 20 mL portion of the same stock solution was treated with concentrated H2SO4 solution where all CrO42− got converted into Cr2O72− ion. What volume of a 0.02 M oxalic acid solution would be required to reduce all dichromate ions?
(a) 45 mL   (b) 22.5 mL   (c) 90 mL   (d) 11.25 mL		Chromate dichromate titration🔥 Titration Masterclass | Double Titration, Back Titration & Redox Titration | JEE Main + Advanced🟡 Similar(b) 22.5 mL
Concept Chromium equivalents are conserved through the CrO42−⇄Cr2O72− change, because that interconversion is not redox (Cr stays +6). First standardise the stock with oxalate, then reduce a fresh portion with oxalic acid. Every Cr(VI) carries n = 3 (→ Cr3+).
Key relation eq(Cr) = MCr·V·3 = eq(oxalate) = M·V·2 n = 3 per Cr(VI), n = 2 per oxalate.
Solution
1. Standardise: 10 mL stock vs 15 mL of 0.015 M Na2C2O4. eq oxalate = 0.015 × 15 × 2 = 0.45 meq.
2. So CCrO4 × 10 × 3 = 0.45 → C = 0.015 M in CrO42−. n = 3 since Cr6+→Cr3+.
3. Fresh 20 mL portion: mmol CrO42− = 0.015 × 20 = 0.30 mmol Cr.
4. 2CrO42− → Cr2O72− conserves Cr atoms; reducing equivalents are unchanged = 0.30 × 3 = 0.90 meq.
5. Reduce with 0.02 M oxalic acid (n = 2): 0.02 × V × 2 = 0.90.
6. V = 0.90 / 0.04 = 22.5 mL.
 standardise: eq oxalate = 0.015 x 15 x 2 = 0.45
   C x 10 x 3 = 0.45  -> C = 0.015 M (CrO4^2-)
 20 mL portion: Cr = 0.015 x 20 = 0.30 mmol
   eq(Cr) = 0.30 x 3 = 0.90 meq (conserved)
 oxalic: 0.02 x V x 2 = 0.90
   V = 0.90 / 0.04 = 22.5 mL      <-- (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Skip the dichromate stoichiometry entirely: Cr equivalents in 20 mL (0.015×20×3) equal the oxalic-acid equivalents, so 0.9 = 0.04V directly. Trap (c) 90 mL comes from dropping the n = 2 on oxalic acid.
Answer: (b) 22.5 mL.
515 mL of an oxalic acid solution, when titrated against a 0.1 M acidified KMnO4 solution, required its 8.0 mL to reach the end point. If the same volume of oxalic acid, from the same stock, is titrated against a 0.2 M NaOH solution, what volume of alkali would be required to reach the equivalence point?
(a) 10 mL   (b) 12 mL   (c) 16 mL   (d) 20 mL		Oxalic acid dual titrationN FACTOR OF ACIDS AND BASES | STOICHIOMETRY 2 | REDOX REACTION| CHEMISTRY BHAIYA |EQUIVALENT CONCEPT🟡 Similar(b)12 mL ⚠
Concept The equivalents of a substance are the same in any reaction when its n-factor is the same. Oxalic acid has n = 2 as a reductant (C2O42− → 2CO2 + 2e−) and n = 2 as a diprotic acid. So the equivalents found by KMnO4 are exactly the equivalents NaOH must neutralise.
Key relation eq(oxalic) = N·V (KMnO4) = N·V (NaOH) n = 2 in both titrations, so equivalents transfer directly.
Solution
1. KMnO4 in acid has n = 5 (Mn7+→Mn2+). eq = 0.1 × 5 × 8.0 = 4.0 meq.
2. Redox endpoint ⇒ eq(oxalic acid in the 15 mL) = 4.0 meq.
3. As an acid, oxalic acid is also n = 2, so its acid equivalents = 4.0 meq (same number). equivalents are n-factor × mol; n is 2 either way.
4. Neutralisation needs equal equivalents of NaOH (n = 1): N·V = 0.2 × V = 4.0.
5. V = 4.0 / 0.2 = 20 mL — option (d).
6. Cross-check by moles: mol oxalic = 4.0/2 = 2.0 mmol; NaOH = 2 × 2.0 = 4.0 mmol; V = 4.0/0.2 = 20 mL. The key’s 12 mL does not follow from any consistent n-factor.
 KMnO4: eq = 0.1 x 5 x 8.0 = 4.0 meq
   -> eq oxalic acid (15 mL) = 4.0 meq
 acid n-factor also = 2  => acid eq = 4.0 meq
 NaOH: 0.2 x V = 4.0  -> V = 20 mL   (d)
 check: mol oxalic=2.0; NaOH=4.0 mmol ->20 mL
 key prints 12 mL (b) -- not reproducible
⚡ JEE Trick Same n-factor in both roles means the KMnO4 volume × its normality gives the NaOH equivalents straight off — no need to find the oxalic concentration. 12 mL cannot be recovered from the printed 0.1/8.0/0.2 data.
Answer: (b) 12 mL per the key — my working gives (d) 20 mL ⚠
6For titration of 20 mL of an aqueous solution of KI against an acidified potassium dichromate solution. 30 mL of oxidizing agent was required to reach the equivalence point. If acidified potassium permanganate solution of same molarity as that of potassium dichromate solution, were used as an oxidizing agent, what volume of the oxidizing agent would have been used to reach the end point? Assume the same final oxidation state of iodine in both titration.
(a) 25 mL   (b) 30 mL   (c) 36 mL   (d) Final oxidation number of iodine is required		Dichromate vs permanganate volumeJEE Main 2022 | Redox Reactions And Equivalent Concept | Equivalent Concept & Titration | Chemistry🟡 Similar(c) 36 mL
Concept KI is the reductant; its equivalents are fixed (same final iodine state in both runs). Equate them to the oxidant equivalents. At equal molarity the only difference is the oxidant n-factor: Cr2O72− takes 6 e−, MnO4− takes 5 e−.
Key relation eq(KI) = nCr·M·V1 = nMn·M·V2 same M cancels; only 6 vs 5 matters.
Solution
1. Dichromate: n = 6 (Cr2O72− + 14H+ + 6e− → 2Cr3+). eq used = 6 × M × 30.
2. That equals the KI equivalents titrated. iodine ends in the same oxidation state, so KI equivalents are identical in run 2.
3. Permanganate: n = 5 (MnO4− + 8H+ + 5e− → Mn2+). eq used = 5 × M × V.
4. Same M (given). Set equal: 6 × M × 30 = 5 × M × V.
5. M cancels: V = (6 × 30)/5 = 180/5 = 36 mL.
6. Since the actual iodine state cancels out, option (d) is a trap — the number is fully determined.
 eq KI = 6 x M x 30   (dichromate, n=6)
       = 5 x M x V    (permanganate, n=5)
 same molarity M cancels:
   V = 6 x 30 / 5 = 180 / 5 = 36 mL   <-- (c)
 iodine state cancels -> (d) not needed
⚡ JEE Trick Volume scales as the n-factor ratio 6/5, so 30 → 36 mL: the stronger (per-mole) oxidant needs less volume, so the weaker-per-mole one (same M) needs more. Trap (d) wrongly claims you must know the iodine state — it cancels.
Answer: (c) 36 mL.
7 (i)Passage Q. (i) to (v)
2.5 g sample of copper is dissolved in excess of H2SO4 to prepare 100 mL of 0.02 M CuSO4(aq). 10 mL of 0.02 M solution CuSO4(aq) is mixed with excess of KI to show the following changes.
CuSO4 + 2KI → K2SO4 + CuI2
2CuI2 → Cu2I2 + I2
The liberated iodine is titrated with hypo (Na2S2O3) and requires V mL of 0.1 M hypo solution for its complete reduction.
(i) The volume (V) of hypo required is:
(a) 2 mL   (b) 20 mL   (c) 1 mL   (d) 10 mL		Copper iodometry hypo volumeiodometric Titration of Copper using Sodium thiosulphate with Calculation🟡 Similar(a) 2 mL
Concept Iodometry: Cu2+ oxidises I−, liberating I2, which is back-titrated with thiosulfate (hypo). Net, 2Cu2+ + 4I− → Cu2I2 + I2, so 2 Cu give 1 I2; then I2 + 2S2O32− → 2I− + S4O62−. The trap is forgetting one of the two stoichiometric factors (2 Cu per I2, and 2 hypo per I2), which happen to cancel.
Key relation mol hypo = 2·mol I2 = mol Cu2+ each Cu2+ (n = 1) ultimately needs one hypo (n = 1).
Solution
1. mmol Cu2+ in 10 mL of 0.02 M = 0.02 × 10 = 0.20 mmol.
2. 2Cu2+ → 1 I2, so mmol I2 = 0.20/2 = 0.10 mmol.
3. I2 + 2S2O32−: mmol hypo = 2 × 0.10 = 0.20 mmol.
4. Equivalents view: eq Cu = 0.20 × 1 = eq hypo = 0.20 meq (both n = 1), same answer. Cu2+→Cu+ is one electron.
5. V = mmol hypo / molarity = 0.20 / 0.1 = 2 mL.
6. Sanity check: the 10 mL aliquot is dilute (0.2 mmol Cu), so only a tiny I2 (0.1 mmol) is freed and a small 2 mL of hypo is reasonable. a 20 mL answer would wrongly need ten times the iodine.
7. So V = 2 mL, option (a).
 Cu2+ = 0.02 x 10 = 0.20 mmol
 2 Cu2+ -> 1 I2    => I2 = 0.10 mmol
 I2 + 2 S2O3^2-    => hypo = 0.20 mmol
 (or: eq Cu = eq hypo = 0.20 meq, n=1 each)
 V = 0.20 / 0.1 = 2 mL            <-- (a)
⚡ JEE Trick The I2 halving and the hypo doubling cancel, so mol hypo = mol Cu2+ — a one-step shortcut. Trap (d) 10 mL comes from treating each Cu as giving one whole I2.
Answer: (a) 2 mL.
7 (ii)Passage Q. (i) to (v)
2.5 g sample of copper is dissolved in excess of H2SO4 to prepare 100 mL of 0.02 M CuSO4(aq). 10 mL of 0.02 M solution CuSO4(aq) is mixed with excess of KI to show the following changes.
CuSO4 + 2KI → K2SO4 + CuI2
2CuI2 → Cu2I2 + I2
The liberated iodine is titrated with hypo (Na2S2O3) and requires V mL of 0.1 M hypo solution for its complete reduction.
(ii) Percentage of purity of sample is:
(a) 10.16   (b) 5.08   (c) 2.54   (d) 1.27		Copper sample purityiodometric Titration of Copper using Sodium thiosulphate with Calculation🟡 Similar(b) 5.08
Concept Purity = mass of actual copper in the 2.5 g sample ÷ 2.5 g, expressed as a percentage. The entire sample was dissolved and made up to 100 mL of 0.02 M CuSO4, so the copper content follows from the total moles of Cu2+ in that whole flask — the 10 mL aliquot was only for the titration and must not be used for the bulk composition.
Key relation % purity = (mol Cu × 63.5)·100 / 2.5 g 1 mol Cu per mol CuSO4; use the full 100 mL, atomic mass Cu = 63.5.
Solution
1. Total mol Cu2+ = 0.02 M × 0.100 L = 2.0 mmol = 2.0 × 10−3 mol.
2. Each CuSO4 holds one Cu atom, so mol Cu in the sample = 2.0 × 10−3 mol. the dissolved copper is all of it, pure plus combined.
3. Mass Cu = 2.0 × 10−3 × 63.5 = 0.127 g.
4. The remaining 2.5 − 0.127 = 2.373 g is non-copper (acid-insoluble / inert) material.
5. % purity = 0.127 × 100 / 2.5 = 5.08%.
6. Cross-check against the aliquot: 10 mL carried 0.2 mmol = 0.0127 g Cu, exactly one-tenth of 0.127 g, confirming the 100 mL scaling. consistency of the dilution.
7. Hence the purity is 5.08%, option (b).
 Cu (whole) = 0.02 x 0.100 L = 2.0 mmol
 mass Cu = 2.0e-3 x 63.5 = 0.127 g
 % = 100 x 0.127 / 2.5 = 5.08 %    <-- (b)
 (use 100 mL, NOT the 10 mL aliquot)
⚡ JEE Trick Low purity is expected — only 0.127 g of 2.5 g is copper. Trap: using the 10 mL aliquot (0.2 mmol) gives one-tenth, i.e. option (c)-style 0.508, so always scale back to the full 100 mL for a bulk-composition question.
Answer: (b) 5.08.
7 (iii)Passage Q. (i) to (v)
2.5 g sample of copper is dissolved in excess of H2SO4 to prepare 100 mL of 0.02 M CuSO4(aq). 10 mL of 0.02 M solution CuSO4(aq) is mixed with excess of KI to show the following changes.
CuSO4 + 2KI → K2SO4 + CuI2
2CuI2 → Cu2I2 + I2
The liberated iodine is titrated with hypo (Na2S2O3) and requires V mL of 0.1 M hypo solution for its complete reduction.
(iii) The amount of I2 liberated in the reaction of 10 mL of 0.02 M solution with KIexcess is:
(a) 0.051 g   (b) 0.0254 g   (c) 0.102 g   (d) 0.204 g		Iodine liberated massiodometric Titration of Copper using Sodium thiosulphate with Calculation🟡 Similar(b) 0.0254 g
Concept The iodine liberated comes straight from the Cu2+ that reacts with excess KI: 2Cu2+ + 4I− → Cu2I2 + I2. Two coppers release one I2. Convert the 10 mL aliquot’s Cu to moles of I2, then to grams.
Key relation mass I2 = (mol Cu2+/2) × 254 M(I2) = 2 × 127 = 254 g/mol.
Solution
1. mmol Cu2+ in 10 mL of 0.02 M = 0.02 × 10 = 0.20 mmol.
2. 2Cu2+ → 1 I2, so mmol I2 = 0.20/2 = 0.10 mmol.
3. M(I2) = 254 g/mol, so mass = 0.10 × 10−3 × 254. 0.10 mmol = 1.0 × 10−4 mol.
4. mass I2 = 1.0 × 10−4 × 254 = 0.0254 g.
5. This matches the hypo result of part (i): 0.10 mmol I2 needs 2 × 0.10 = 0.20 mmol hypo, i.e. 2 mL of 0.1 M. the two parts must agree on the same 0.10 mmol I2.
6. Note 0.10 mmol is a very small mass because the 10 mL aliquot is dilute; scaling to the whole 100 mL would give ten times as much.
7. So the iodine liberated in this reaction = 0.0254 g, option (b).
 Cu2+ = 0.02 x 10 = 0.20 mmol
 2 Cu2+ -> 1 I2  => I2 = 0.10 mmol
 M(I2) = 2 x 127 = 254 g/mol
 mass = 1.0e-4 mol x 254 = 0.0254 g  <-- (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Halve the Cu moles for I2, then ×254. Trap (a) 0.051 g doubles the iodine (forgetting 2Cu → 1I2); (c) 0.102 g uses the atomic mass 127 times the Cu moles.
Answer: (b) 0.0254 g.
7 (iv)Passage Q. (i) to (v)
2.5 g sample of copper is dissolved in excess of H2SO4 to prepare 100 mL of 0.02 M CuSO4(aq). 10 mL of 0.02 M solution CuSO4(aq) is mixed with excess of KI to show the following changes.
CuSO4 + 2KI → K2SO4 + CuI2
2CuI2 → Cu2I2 + I2
The liberated iodine is titrated with hypo (Na2S2O3) and requires V mL of 0.1 M hypo solution for its complete reduction.
(iv) The colour of solution developed during addition of KI of CuSO4 solution is:
(a) violet due to dissolution of liberated I2   (b) brownish yellow due to dissolution of liberated I2   (c) red due to the formation of CuI2   (d) brown due to the formation of Cu2I2		Solution colour KI CuSO4IODOMETRIC TITRATIONS | REDOX REACTIONS | Class 11 Chemistry | JEE Main | NEET Chemistry🟡 Similar(b) brownish yellow due to dissolution of liberated I2
Concept When KI is added to CuSO4, two things form: a white precipitate of cuprous iodide (Cu2I2, i.e. CuI) and free I2. The colour of the solution is set by the dissolved iodine, which forms the brown tri-iodide (I3−) with excess I−.
Key relation 2Cu2+ + 4I− → Cu2I2(white) + I2; I2 + I− → I3− (brown) solution colour = dissolved iodine, not the ppt.
Solution
1. Cu2+ oxidises I−, liberating molecular I2.
2. With excess KI present, I2 dissolves as I3−, which is brown / brownish-yellow.
3. The copper ends as Cu2I2 (CuI), a white solid — it colours the precipitate, not the clear solution, so (d) is wrong about colour.
4. CuI2 is unstable and decomposes at once, so (c) “red CuI2” is not what is seen.
5. Iodine solutions are brown, not violet (violet is I2 vapour / non-polar solvents), so (a) is wrong.
6. The solution is therefore brownish yellow due to dissolved I2 — option (b).
 2Cu2+ + 4I- -> Cu2I2 (white ppt) + I2
 I2 + I- -> I3-   (brown, in solution)
 solution colour  = brownish yellow  (b)
 precipitate      = white Cu2I2
 violet = I2 vapour/CCl4 ; not aqueous
⚡ JEE Trick Separate the precipitate (white CuI) from the solution (brown I3−); the question asks the solution. Violet belongs to iodine vapour or a CCl4 layer, never the aqueous KI solution.
Answer: (b) brownish yellow due to dissolution of liberated I2.
7 (v)Passage Q. (i) to (v)
2.5 g sample of copper is dissolved in excess of H2SO4 to prepare 100 mL of 0.02 M CuSO4(aq). 10 mL of 0.02 M solution CuSO4(aq) is mixed with excess of KI to show the following changes.
CuSO4 + 2KI → K2SO4 + CuI2
2CuI2 → Cu2I2 + I2
The liberated iodine is titrated with hypo (Na2S2O3) and requires V mL of 0.1 M hypo solution for its complete reduction.
(v) Select the correct statements:
(1) The reaction belongs to iodometric titration   (2) The reaction belongs to iodimetric titration   (3) Cu2I2 formed during the reaction is white   (4) Starch is used as indicator in this titration
(a) 1, 3, 4   (b) 2, 3, 4   (c) 1, 3   (d) 2, 3		Iodometric titration statementsIODOMETRIC TITRATIONS | REDOX REACTIONS | Class 11 Chemistry | JEE Main | NEET Chemistry🟡 Similar(a) 1, 3, 4
Concept Iodometry vs iodimetry: iodometric = an oxidant first liberates I2 from excess I−, then I2 is titrated with hypo (indirect). Iodimetric = a reductant is titrated directly with a standard I2 solution. Here Cu2+ liberates I2 first, so it is iodometric.
Key relation oxidant + excess I− → I2; I2 + 2S2O32− → 2I− + S4O62− (starch endpoint) indirect liberation = iodometry.
Solution
1. Statement (1): I2 is liberated by Cu2+ and then titrated — that is iodometric. TRUE.
2. Statement (2): iodimetric means direct titration with I2; not the case here. FALSE.
3. Statement (3): Cu2I2 (cuprous iodide, CuI) is a white solid. TRUE.
4. Statement (4): starch is the standard indicator (blue→colourless at the endpoint). TRUE.
5. So the correct set is 1, 3 and 4. exactly matches option (a).
6. Options (c) 1,3 and (d) 2,3 drop the valid starch statement or wrongly pick iodimetric.
 (1) iodometric (I2 liberated then titrated) T
 (2) iodimetric (direct I2 titration)        F
 (3) Cu2I2 is white                          T
 (4) starch indicator                        T
 -----------------------------------------
 correct set = 1, 3, 4                 <-- (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Memory hook: iodO-metric = Oxidant liberates iodine (indirect); iodI-metric = dIrect with I2. Starch is added near the endpoint to avoid adsorbing too much iodine. Trap: dropping (4) and choosing (c).
Answer: (a) 1, 3, 4.
8 (i)Passage Q. (i) to (x)
K2Cr2O7 acts as a good oxidizing agent in acidic medium.
Cr2O72− (Orange) + 14H+ + 6e− → 2Cr3+ (Green) + 7H2O
In alkaline solution, orange colour of Cr2O72− changes to yellow colour due to formation of CrO42− and again yellow colour changes to orange colour on changing the solution to acidic medium.
Cr2O72− (Orange) + 2OH− → 2CrO42− (Yellow) + H2O
2CrO42− (Yellow) + 2H+ → Cr2O72− (Orange) + H2O
CrO42− and Cr2O72− exist in equilibrium at pH = 4 and are interconvertible by altering the pH of the solution. When heated with H2SO4 and metal chloride, K2Cr2O7 gives vapours of chromyl chloride (CrO2Cl2). Chromyl chloride (CrO2Cl2) when passed into aqueous NaOH solution, yellow colour solution of CrO42− is obtained. This on reaction with lead acetate gives yellow ppt. of PbCrO4.
When H2O2 is added to an acidified solution of dichromate ion, a complicated reaction occurs. The products obtained depends on the pH and concentration of Cr.
Cr2O72− + 2H+ + 4H2O2 → 2CrO(O2)2 + 5H2O
A deep blue-violet coloured peroxo compound, CrO(O2)2, called chromic peroxide is formed. This decomposes rapidly in aqueous solution into Cr3+ and oxygen.
(i) What happens when a solution of potassium chromate is treated with an excess of dilute nitric acid?
(a) Cr3+ and Cr2O72− are formed   (b) Cr2O72− and H2O are formed   (c) CrO42− is reduced to +3 state of Cr   (d) CrO42− is reduced to 0 state of Cr		Chromate acidificationImportant Reactions of Potassium Dichromate| Effect of pH, Heat| Chromyl Chloride Test| Structure|🟡 Similar(b) Cr2O72− and H2O are formed
Concept The chromate–dichromate equilibrium is pH-controlled and involves no change in oxidation state (Cr stays +6). Adding acid (H+ from HNO3) drives yellow CrO42− to orange Cr2O72−. Dilute HNO3 here is just the acid, not an oxidant/reductant of Cr.
Key relation 2CrO42− + 2H+ → Cr2O72− + H2O condensation, Cr remains +6 throughout.
Solution
1. Potassium chromate supplies CrO42− (Cr is +6).
2. Excess dilute HNO3 lowers the pH, supplying H+.
3. Two chromates condense: 2CrO42− + 2H+ → Cr2O72− + H2O. the yellow→orange colour change.
4. Oxidation number of Cr is +6 on both sides — no redox, so no Cr3+ (rules out a, c) and no Cr(0) (rules out d).
5. The products are dichromate ion and water.
6. Hence option (b): Cr2O72− and H2O are formed.
 CrO4^2- (yellow, Cr +6)  +  H+ (dil HNO3)
 2CrO4^2- + 2H+ -> Cr2O7^2- + H2O
 Cr: +6 -> +6   (NO redox)
 products = Cr2O7^2- + H2O          <-- (b)
⚡ JEE Trick “Dilute” HNO3 here only provides H+; it does not reduce Cr, so distractors naming Cr3+ or Cr(0) are traps. Any yellow↔orange chromate question is acid–base, not redox.
Answer: (b) Cr2O72− and H2O are formed.

(ii) Which of the following statements is wrong when a mixture of NaCl and K2Cr2O7 is gently warmed with conc. H2SO4?
(a) A deep red vapour is evolved   (b) The vapours obtained when passed through NaOH solution gives a yellow solution of Na2CrO4   (c) Chlorine gas is formed   (d) Chromyl chloride is formed		Chromyl chloride testJEE Main 2026 Chemistry | What is Chromyl Chloride Test | JEE Main Chemistry | Indrajeet Sir KGS🟡 Similar(c) Chlorine gas is formed
Concept The chromyl chloride test: a chloride + K2Cr2O7 + conc. H2SO4, gently warmed, gives deep-red CrO2Cl2 vapour. This is not an oxidation of Cl− to Cl2 — Cr stays +6 and Cl stays −1. The question asks which statement is WRONG.
Key relation K2Cr2O7 + 4NaCl + 6H2SO4 → 2CrO2Cl2↑ + ... ; no Cl2 Cr +6, Cl −1 on both sides = no redox.
Solution
1. (a) CrO2Cl2 is a deep-red vapour — TRUE.
2. (d) chromyl chloride is indeed the product — TRUE.
3. (b) passing the vapour into NaOH gives yellow Na2CrO4 (CrO2Cl2 + 4NaOH → Na2CrO4 + 2NaCl + 2H2O) — TRUE.
4. (c) claims Cl2 gas forms. In the chromyl chloride test Cl− keeps oxidation state −1; no Cl2 is evolved. Cl2 would appear only with a strong oxidant like MnO2/HCl, not this test.
5. So the one wrong statement is (c).
6. The red vapour is molecular CrO2Cl2, often mistaken for Cl2 — that is the trap (c) sets.
 Cr2O7^2- + 4Cl- + 6H+ -> 2CrO2Cl2 + 3H2O
 Cr: +6 -> +6 ;  Cl: -1 -> -1   (no redox)
 so NO Cl2 gas is evolved
 wrong statement = "Chlorine gas formed" (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Chromyl chloride test = molecular transfer of Cl, never Cl−→Cl2. If the question offered “Cl2 evolved” as a true option it would be wrong; here it is the one false statement asked for.
Answer: (c) Chlorine gas is formed (the wrong statement).

Chromyl chloride (CrO2Cl2) when passed into aqueous NaOH solution, yellow colour solution of CrO42− is obtained. This on reaction with lead acetate gives yellow ppt. of PbCrO4.
When H2O2 is added to an acidified solution of dichromate ion, a complicated reaction occurs. The products obtained depends on the pH and concentration of Cr.
Cr2O72− + 2H+ + 4H2O2 → 2CrO(O2)2 + 5H2O

(iii) The CrO3 on reaction with HCl and NaOH(aq) gives respectively:
(a) CrO2Cl2, CrO42−   (b) Cr(OH)2, CrO42−   (c) Cl2, Cr2O72−   (d) Cl2, Cr(OH)3		CrO3 reactionsd & f Block - 1 | Potassium Dichromate - K2Cr2O7 | Inorganic Chemistry | JEE Advanced🟡 Similar(a) CrO2Cl2, CrO42−
Concept CrO3 is the acidic anhydride of chromic acid with Cr at +6. Neither reaction with HCl nor with NaOH changes that oxidation state (both are acid–base/condensation, not redox). With HCl it gives chromyl chloride; with alkali it gives chromate.
Key relation CrO3 + 2HCl → CrO2Cl2 + H2O; CrO3 + 2NaOH → Na2CrO4 + H2O Cr stays +6 in both.
Solution
1. CrO3 has Cr in the +6 state (acidic oxide).
2. With HCl: CrO3 + 2HCl → CrO2Cl2 + H2O — chromyl chloride, Cr still +6. no Cl2, no reduction.
3. With aqueous NaOH: CrO3 + 2NaOH → Na2CrO4 + H2O, i.e. yellow CrO42−.
4. So HCl → CrO2Cl2 and NaOH → CrO42−, matching option (a).
5. (c)/(d) wrongly invoke Cl2 (would need Cr reduced); (b) names Cr(OH)2 with Cr +2, also a reduction — none occurs.
6. Therefore the pair is CrO2Cl2, CrO42−.
 CrO3 (Cr +6)
  + 2HCl  -> CrO2Cl2 + H2O   (Cr +6)
  + 2NaOH -> Na2CrO4 + H2O   (CrO4^2-)
 no redox -> no Cl2, no Cr(OH)2
 pair = CrO2Cl2 , CrO4^2-        <-- (a)
⚡ JEE Trick An acidic +6 oxide: HCl pulls out chromyl chloride, alkali gives chromate — both keep Cr at +6. Any option showing Cl2 or lower Cr needs a reductant that is not present, so reject it.
Answer: (a) CrO2Cl2, CrO42−.

2CrO42− (Yellow) + 2H+ → Cr2O72− (Orange) + H2O
CrO42− and Cr2O72− exist in equilibrium at pH = 4 and are interconvertible by altering the pH of the solution. When heated with H2SO4 and metal chloride, K2Cr2O7 gives vapours of chromyl chloride (CrO2Cl2). Chromyl chloride (CrO2Cl2) when passed into aqueous NaOH solution, yellow colour solution of CrO42− is obtained. This on reaction with lead acetate gives yellow ppt. of PbCrO4.
When H2O2 is added to an acidified solution of dichromate ion, a complicated reaction occurs. The products obtained depends on the pH and concentration of Cr.
Cr2O72− + 2H+ + 4H2O2 → 2CrO(O2)2 + 5H2O
A deep blue-violet coloured peroxo compound, CrO(O2)2, called chromic peroxide is formed. This decomposes rapidly in aqueous solution into Cr3+ and oxygen.
(iv) Number of mole of K2Cr2O7 reduced by one mole of Sn2+ ions in acidic medium is:
(a) 1/3   (b) 3   (c) 1/6   (d) 6		Dichromate tin n-factorn-factors Calculation of Oxidising and Reducing Agent | Redox Reaction | Easy Marks in JEE🟡 Similar(a) 1/3
Concept Electrons lost by the reductant equal electrons gained by the oxidant. Sn2+ → Sn4+ gives 2 electrons; each K2Cr2O7 (via Cr2O72− + 6e− → 2Cr3+) accepts 6. So one Sn2+ can reduce only a fraction of a dichromate.
Key relation mol K2Cr2O7 = (e− from Sn2+) / (e− per dichromate) = 2 / 6 electron balance.
Solution
1. Sn2+ → Sn4+ + 2e−: n-factor of Sn2+ = 2.
2. Cr2O72− + 14H+ + 6e− → 2Cr3+ + 7H2O: n-factor of dichromate = 6.
3. Equate electrons: 1 mol Sn2+ supplies 2 mol e−. eq(reductant) = eq(oxidant).
4. mol K2Cr2O7 reduced = 2 / 6 = 1/3.
5. Check by equivalents: eq(Sn2+) = eq(dichromate) ⇒ 1 × 2 = x × 6 → x = 1/3 mol of K2Cr2O7.
6. Put differently, one dichromate needs 3 Sn2+ (3 × 2 = 6 e−), so one Sn2+ handles only 1/3 of a dichromate. inverse of the n-factor ratio.
7. So the number of moles of K2Cr2O7 reduced is 1/3, option (a).
 Sn2+ -> Sn4+ + 2e-        (gives 2 e-)
 Cr2O7^2- + 6e- -> 2Cr3+   (takes 6 e-)
 mol K2Cr2O7 = 2 / 6 = 1/3        <-- (a)
 check: 1 x 2 = n x 6 -> n = 1/3
⚡ JEE Trick Moles react in the inverse ratio of their n-factors: Sn (2) vs dichromate (6) gives 2/6 = 1/3. Trap (b) 3 inverts the ratio; (d) 6 just quotes the dichromate n-factor.
Answer: (a) 1/3.
8 (v)Passage Q. (i) to (x)
K2Cr2O7 acts as a good oxidizing agent in acidic medium.
Cr2O72− (Orange) + 14H+ + 6e− → 2Cr3+ (Green) + 7H2O
In alkaline solution, orange colour of Cr2O72− changes to yellow colour due to formation of CrO42− and again yellow colour changes to orange colour on changing the solution to acidic medium.
Cr2O72− (Orange) + 2OH− → 2CrO42− (Yellow) + H2O
2CrO42− (Yellow) + 2H+ → Cr2O72− (Orange) + H2O
CrO42− and Cr2O72− exist in equilibrium at pH = 4 and are interconvertible by altering the pH of the solution. When heated with H2SO4 and metal chloride, K2Cr2O7 gives vapours of chromyl chloride (CrO2Cl2). Chromyl chloride (CrO2Cl2) when passed into aqueous NaOH solution, yellow colour solution of CrO42− is obtained. This on reaction with lead acetate gives yellow ppt. of PbCrO4.
When H2O2 is added to an acidified solution of dichromate ion, a complicated reaction occurs. The products obtained depends on the pH and concentration of Cr.
Cr2O72− + 2H+ + 4H2O2 → 2CrO(O2)2 + 5H2O
A deep blue-violet coloured peroxo compound, CrO(O2)2, called chromic peroxide is formed. This decomposes rapidly in aqueous solution into Cr3+ and oxygen.
(v) The equivalent mass of BaCrO4 used as an oxidizing agent in acidic medium is: (Atomic mass of Ba = 137.34 and Cr = 52)
(a) 137.34   (b) 84.44   (c) 114.45   (d) 68.67		Equivalent mass barium chromateEquivalent mass of oxidizing agent in the reaction| Equivalence weight | Equivalance master🟡 Similar(b) 84.44
Concept Equivalent mass = molar mass ÷ n-factor. For an oxidising agent the n-factor is the number of electrons gained per formula unit. BaCrO4 contains one chromium in the +6 state; in acidic medium it is reduced Cr6+ → Cr3+, i.e. 3 electrons per Cr. Barium (+2) and oxygen (−2) are spectators and keep their states.
Key relation Eq mass = M / n; n = 3 for Cr6+ → Cr3+ exactly one Cr per BaCrO4, so the salt’s n-factor is the Cr change.
Solution
1. Molar mass BaCrO4 = 137.34 (Ba) + 52 (Cr) + 4 × 16 (O) = 137.34 + 52 + 64 = 253.34.
2. As an oxidant in acid, Cr6+ → Cr3+ gains 3 e−, so the electrons gained per Cr = 3.
3. There is only one Cr per formula, so the whole BaCrO4 has n-factor = 3. Ba2+ and O2− do not change oxidation state.
4. Eq mass = M / n = 253.34 / 3 = 84.44.
5. This matches option (b) exactly.
6. (a) 137.34 is merely the atomic mass of Ba (a decoy); using n = 1 on the salt would give 253.34, also wrong.
7. (d) 68.67 and (c) 114.45 come from dividing by 2 or by other wrong n-factors; the correct divisor is 3.
 M(BaCrO4) = 137.34 + 52 + 4x16
           = 137.34 + 52 + 64 = 253.34
 Cr6+ -> Cr3+  : n-factor = 3
 Eq mass = 253.34 / 3 = 84.44     <-- (b)
⚡ JEE Trick One Cr per unit ⇒ the whole salt’s n-factor is just the Cr change (3), even though Ba is +2. Trap (a) treats the molar mass itself as the equivalent mass (n = 1).
Answer: (b) 84.44.

When heated with H2SO4 and metal chloride, K2Cr2O7 gives vapours of chromyl chloride (CrO2Cl2). Chromyl chloride (CrO2Cl2) when passed into aqueous NaOH solution, yellow colour solution of CrO42− is obtained. This on reaction with lead acetate gives yellow ppt. of PbCrO4.
When H2O2 is added to an acidified solution of dichromate ion, a complicated reaction occurs. The products obtained depends on the pH and concentration of Cr.
Cr2O72− + 2H+ + 4H2O2 → 2CrO(O2)2 + 5H2O

(vi) The equivalent mass of KIO3 in the reaction,
2Cr(OH)3 + OH− + KIO3 → 2CrO42− + KI + 5H2O:
(a) molar mass   (b) M/3   (c) M/6   (d) M/2		Equivalent mass iodateEquivalent mass of oxidizing agent in the reaction| Equivalence weight | Equivalance master🟡 Similar(c) M/6
Concept Equivalent mass is molar mass divided by the n-factor, and the n-factor of a species in a redox change is the number of electrons it gains or loses per formula unit. The trap is to use chromium’s change; the question asks about KIO3, so only iodine’s own oxidation-number change counts, regardless of how many chromium atoms are present.
Key relation Eq mass = M / n, where n = |ΔO.N. of I in KIO3| KIO3 is the species being reduced, so its own electron change fixes n.
Solution
1. Identify the role of KIO3: on the right it becomes KI, so iodine is reduced — KIO3 is the oxidising agent here.
2. Find iodine in the reactant: in IO3−, O is −2 × 3 = −6 and the ion carries −1, so I = +5.
3. Find iodine in the product KI: K is +1, so I = −1.
4. Electron gain per iodine = (+5) − (−1) = 6, so n = 6 for KIO3. Iodine is reduced, picking up 6 electrons.
5. Therefore Eq mass = M / n = M / 6, which is option (c).
6. Cross-check by electron balance: 2Cr(III)→2Cr(VI) releases 2×3 = 6 e, exactly matching the 6 e taken up by one KIO3, so n = 6 is consistent with the balanced equation.
 I in IO3- : -2x3 = -6, ion = -1  => I = +5
 I in KI   : -1
 n = (+5) - (-1) = 6
 -----------------------------------------
 Eq mass of KIO3 = M / 6          <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Iodate-to-iodide is always a 6-electron change (+5 to −1); that is why iodometric standards built on IO3− use M/6. The tempting M/2 comes from wrongly borrowing chromium’s per-atom change — but the asked species is KIO3.
Answer: (c) M/6.

(vii) When H2O2 is added to an acidified solution of K2Cr2O7, then:
(a) solution turns green due to formation of Cr2O3 and reduction of Cr takes place   (b) solution turns yellow due to formation of CrO(O2)2 and no redox change   (c) a deep blue-violet coloured compound CrO(O2)2 due to reduction of Cr   (d) solution gives green ppt. CrO(O2)2 due to oxidation of Cr		Chromium peroxide formationSynthesis of Chromium peroxide, Colour of CrO5 | Butterfly structure & Oxidation no. of Cr in CrO5🟡 Similar(b) formation of CrO(O₂)₂, no redox change
Concept Acidified dichromate plus H2O2 gives chromium peroxide CrO(O2)2 (CrO5). The teaching point is the oxidation state: chromium does not change, so this is a ligand/peroxide substitution, not a redox step for Cr.
Key relation O.N. of Cr in Cr2O72− = +6 and in CrO(O2)2 = +6 Equal oxidation numbers mean no net electron transfer at chromium.
Solution
1. Count Cr in CrO(O2)2: one oxo O is −2, two peroxo groups O22− are −2 each, total −6; neutral molecule → Cr = +6.
2. In Cr2O72− each Cr is also +6, so Cr = +6 → +6: no reduction and no oxidation of chromium.
3. The peroxide oxygen stays −1 (as in H2O2), so no element is redox-changed in forming the complex. It is an acid–base type peroxo-complex formation.
4. Hence option (a) (reduction to Cr2O3) and (c)/(d) (reduction/oxidation of Cr) are all wrong on the oxidation-state claim.
5. Only (b) states the correct chemistry: formation of CrO(O2)2 with no redox change.
 Cr in Cr2O7^2-     : +6
 Cr in CrO(O2)2     : +6   (no change)
 -----------------------------------------
 result: CrO(O2)2 forms, no redox  (b)
⚡ JEE Trick CrO5 is deep blue-violet (the stem says so); the option’s word “yellow” is a misprint, but the decisive, correct clause is “no redox change,” which only (b) has. Option (c) names the right colour yet falsely claims reduction — Cr never leaves +6.
Answer: (b) formation of CrO(O2)2 with no redox change (the printed “yellow” is a misprint for deep blue-violet).

(viii) The colour of Cu2Cr2O7 solution in water is:
(a) green   (b) blue   (c) orange   (d) black		Cupric dichromate colourWhy Transition Metal Ions Are Coloured | JEE 2027 Chemistry (English)🟡 Similar(a) green
Concept The colour of a dissolved salt is the visual sum of its coloured ions. Copper dichromate puts a blue copper cation alongside an orange dichromate anion in water, and the eye reads the overlap of the two absorptions as green.
Key relation observed colour = colour(Cu2+) + colour(Cr2O72−) = blue + orange → green Both ions absorb in complementary regions, so the transmitted light is green.
Solution
1. On dissolving, the salt dissociates to give copper cations and dichromate anions in solution.
2. The hydrated copper(II) ion [Cu(H2O)6]2+ is blue; any cuprous Cu+ is unstable in water and disproportionates to Cu2+ + Cu, so the blue Cu2+ colour dominates. Aqueous copper almost always appears as blue Cu2+.
3. The dichromate ion Cr2O72− is orange, as the passage states.
4. Blue light from the copper centre and orange light from the dichromate combine, and the eye perceives the mixture as green — the same reason copper chromate/dichromate baths look green.
5. Rule out the rest: pure blue (b) would need a colourless anion, orange (c) would need a colourless cation, and black (d) is not a dilute-solution colour for these ions.
6. Hence the aqueous solution is green, option (a).
 Cu2+        : blue
 Cr2O7^2-    : orange
 -----------------------------------------
 blue + orange seen as  GREEN        (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Remember the additive pair: a blue 3d cation with an orange/yellow oxo-anion reads green. The distractor “orange” (c) ignores the copper colour; “blue” (b) ignores the dichromate colour. Both coloured ions are present, so neither single colour survives.
Answer: (a) green.

Cr2O72− + 2H+ + 4H2O2 → 2CrO(O2)2 + 5H2O

(ix) Which statement about CrO5 is wrong?
(a) Oxidation number of Cr in CrO5 is +6.   (b) CrO5 has butterfly structure   (c) It has one oxygen atom attached with double bond with Cr and four oxygen atoms attached with single bond with chromium.   (d) It has four peroxide bond.		CrO5 structureoxidation number of Cr in CrO5 l Chromium pentoxide (CrO5) l butterfly structure l CrO5 structure l🟡 Similar(d) It has four peroxide bonds (WRONG statement)
Concept CrO5 (chromium peroxide) is a butterfly-shaped molecule: one chromium carries a single doubly-bonded oxo oxygen and two side-on peroxo (O22−) units. Counting the peroxide links correctly settles the question.
Key relation CrO5 = one Cr=O (oxo) + two O−O (peroxo) ⇒ 4 single Cr–O bonds + 2 peroxide bonds Each peroxo group contributes one O–O link and two Cr–O single bonds.
Solution
1. Oxidation state: one oxo O = −2, two peroxo groups = −2 each, sum −6, molecule neutral → Cr = +6. So (a) is correct.
2. Geometry is the well-known butterfly structure, so (b) is correct.
3. Bonding to Cr: the oxo oxygen is doubly bonded (Cr=O) and the four peroxo oxygens are each singly bonded to Cr, so (c) — one double-bonded O and four single-bonded O — is correct.
4. Count peroxide (O–O) bonds: the two side-on O2 units give exactly two O–O linkages, not four. Four peroxo oxygen atoms pair up into only two O–O bonds.
5. So every one of (a), (b) and (c) states a genuine fact about CrO5, leaving only (d) to be checked.
6. Therefore statement (d), “four peroxide bonds,” is the wrong one — the number of O–O bonds is two.
 oxo bonds  (Cr=O)       : 1
 Cr-O single bonds       : 4  (from 2 peroxo O2)
 peroxide O-O bonds      : 2  (not 4)
 -----------------------------------------
 wrong statement : (d) four peroxide bonds
⚡ JEE Trick Peroxo oxygens come in pairs: 4 peroxo O atoms = 2 O–O bonds. Mis-reading “four peroxide oxygens” as “four peroxide bonds” is the exact trap (d) sets. Statements (a)–(c) are all genuine facts about CrO5.
Answer: (d) — “four peroxide bonds” is the wrong statement (CrO5 has two).
8 (x)Passage Q. (i) to (x)
