12.5 g of a sample of AgNO3 is dissolved in 50 mL of water. It is titrated with 50 mL of KI solution. The AgI precipitate is filtered off. Excess of KI in the filterate is titrated with 50 mL M/10 KIO3 solution acidified with dilute H2SO4. 20 mL of the same stock solution of KI requires 30 mL of M/10 KIO3 under similar conditions. Calculate % of AgNO3 in the sample.AgNO3 iodometric assay10 TITRATION INVOLVING IODINE | IODIMETRIC AND IODOMETRIC TITATION | IIT ADVANCED | JEE MAIN🟡 Similar85%
Concept The silver is precipitated as AgI by excess KI; the KI left in the filtrate is then measured against KIO₃. To use that measurement you must first standardise the KI stock (because its molarity is not given), using the reaction IO₃⁻ + 5I⁻ + 6H⁺ → 3I₂. The KI that disappeared matched Ag⁺ one-to-one, which delivers the AgNO₃. The iodate is never consumed by silver; it serves only as a reagent to count free iodide.
Key relation KI(reacted) = KI(total) − KI(excess); n(AgNO₃) = KI(reacted) each IO₃⁻ consumes 5 I⁻, and Ag⁺ + I⁻ → AgI is a 1:1 precipitation.
Solution
1. Standardise the stock: 20 mL KI needs 30 mL M/10 KIO₃, so n(KIO₃) = 0.030 × 0.1 = 3.0 × 10⁻³ and n(KI) = 5 × 3.0 × 10⁻³ = 0.015 mol in 20 mL → [KI] = 0.75 M.
2. KI added to the AgNO₃ sample = 0.050 × 0.75 = 0.0375 mol.
3. The excess KI in the filtrate is titrated by 50 mL M/10 KIO₃: n(KIO₃) = 5.0 × 10⁻³, so KI(excess) = 5 × 5.0 × 10⁻³ = 0.025 mol.
4. KI used by Ag⁺ = 0.0375 − 0.025 = 0.0125 mol = n(AgNO₃).
5. mass AgNO₃ = 0.0125 × 170 = 2.125 g (M = 170).
6. % AgNO₃ = 2.125/2.5 × 100 = 85%, matching the key.
7. Cross-check: the 15% balance (0.375 g) is inert matter, and 0.0125 mol of AgI (M = 235) would weigh 2.94 g of precipitate, a sensible amount to filter — the numbers hang together.
 [KI]: 5*(0.030*0.1)=0.015 mol/20mL = 0.75 M
 KI total  = 0.050*0.75 = 0.0375 mol
 KI excess = 5*(0.050*0.1) = 0.025 mol
 KI -> Ag = 0.0375-0.025 = 0.0125 = AgNO3
 mass = 0.0125*170 = 2.125 g
 % = 2.125/2.5*100 = 85%              <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick One iodate eats five iodides, so standardise the stock before anything else — skipping that step is the usual error. The silver step itself is a plain 1:1 precipitation; the KIO₃ is only a ruler for how much KI survived.
Answer: 85% AgNO₃.
2Hydrogen peroxide solution (20 mL) reacts quantitatively with a solution of KMnO4 (20 mL) acidified with dilute sulphuric acid. The same volume of KMnO4 solution is just decolourized by 10 mL MnSO4 in neutral medium simultaneously forming a dark brown precipitate of hydrated MnO2. The brown precipitate is dissolved in 10 mL of 0.2 M sodium oxalate under boiling conditions in presence of dilute H2SO4. Write the balanced equations involved in the reactions and calculate the molarity of H2O2.
📝 PYQ: IIT-JEE 2001Solution ↗
H2O2 / KMnO4 molarityEXAMPLE OF H2O2 & KMnO4 TITRATION | JEE-ADVANCED LEVEL🟡 Similar0.10 M
Concept Three linked reactions let you back-chain from the oxalate to the peroxide. The brown MnO₂ is formed in neutral medium from MnO₄⁻ reacting with Mn²⁺; dissolving it in oxalate fixes the moles of MnO₂ (1:1), the neutral equation then fixes the moles of MnO₄⁻, and finally the acidic MnO₄⁻–H₂O₂ equation gives the peroxide. The key subtlety is that neutral permanganate is only a 3-electron oxidant, which changes every ratio downstream.
Key relation n(MnO₂) = n(oxalate); n(MnO₄⁻) = (2/5)·n(MnO₂); n(H₂O₂) = (5/2)·n(MnO₄⁻) the neutral 2MnO₄⁻ + 3Mn²⁺ → 5MnO₂ ratio is what gives the 2/5 step.
Solution
1. Acidic: 2MnO₄⁻ + 5H₂O₂ + 6H⁺ → 2Mn²⁺ + 5O₂ + 8H₂O.
2. Neutral: 2MnO₄⁻ + 3Mn²⁺ + 2H₂O → 5MnO₂ + 4H⁺.
3. Dissolving: MnO₂ + C₂O₄²⁻ + 4H⁺ → Mn²⁺ + 2CO₂ + 2H₂O, a 1:1 ratio of MnO₂ to oxalate.
4. n(oxalate) = 0.010 × 0.2 = 2.0 × 10⁻³ mol, so n(MnO₂) = 2.0 × 10⁻³ mol.
5. From the neutral step n(MnO₄⁻) = (2/5) × 2.0 × 10⁻³ = 8.0 × 10⁻⁴ mol.
6. From the acidic step n(H₂O₂) = (5/2) × 8.0 × 10⁻⁴ = 2.0 × 10⁻³ mol in 20 mL, so M(H₂O₂) = 2.0 × 10⁻³/0.020 = 0.10 M.
7. Cross-check: that same 8.0 × 10⁻⁴ mol MnO₄⁻ reacts with both 10 mL MnSO₄ (neutral) and 20 mL of the 0.10 M H₂O₂ (acidic), so one permanganate solution ties the two assays together consistently.
 n(oxalate) = 0.010*0.2 = 2.0e-3 = n(MnO2)
 neutral: MnO4- = (2/5)*MnO2 = 8.0e-4 mol
 acidic:  H2O2 = (5/2)*MnO4- = 2.0e-3 mol
 M(H2O2) = 2.0e-3/0.020 = 0.10 M      <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick In neutral medium permanganate falls only to Mn⁴⁺ (3 electrons), not all the way to Mn²⁺ (5); that is why MnO₄⁻:MnO₂ is 2:5, not 2:1. The oxalate 1:1 step is the fixed anchor that closes the whole chain back to H₂O₂.
Answer: 0.10 M H₂O₂.
3Silver present in a 1.0 g sample is determined by first precipitating Ag as Ag2CrO4. The precipitate is finally dissolved in acid solution and treated with excess of KI solution. The liberated iodine required 30 mL 0.095 M hypo solution. Determine mass percentage of Ag in the original sample. M(Ag)=108.Silver as Ag2CrO4 assayIodometry - Estimation of Potassium dichromate using Sodium thiosulphateDr.DDD🟡 Similar20.52%
Concept Silver is weighed as Ag₂CrO₄, which carries two silvers per chromate. After the precipitate is dissolved in acid and treated with excess KI, it is the chromate (Cr⁶⁺→Cr³⁺, a 3-electron reduction) that liberates iodine, which is then titrated with hypo. So the chain is 2 Ag ≡ 1 Cr ≡ 1.5 I₂ ≡ 3 S₂O₃²⁻, and the silver is linked to the titre through chromium, not directly. The silver itself is redox-inert here; it only sets the 2:1 ratio to chromate.
Key relation n(Cr) = n(thiosulphate)/3; n(Ag) = 2·n(Cr) = (2/3)·n(thio) the redox electrons belong to chromium, while two silvers ride on each chromate.
Solution
1. Ag₂CrO₄ → 2Ag⁺ + CrO₄²⁻, tying silver to chromate in a 2:1 ratio.
2. In acid 2CrO₄²⁻ → Cr₂O₇²⁻, then Cr₂O₇²⁻ + 6I⁻ + 14H⁺ → 2Cr³⁺ + 3I₂ + 7H₂O, so each Cr ≡ 3 electrons ≡ 3 thiosulphate.
3. n(thiosulphate) = 0.030 × 0.095 = 2.85 × 10⁻³ mol.
4. n(Cr) = 2.85 × 10⁻³/3 = 9.5 × 10⁻⁴ mol.
5. n(Ag) = 2 × 9.5 × 10⁻⁴ = 1.9 × 10⁻³ mol.
6. mass Ag = 1.9 × 10⁻³ × 108 = 0.2052 g, so % Ag = 0.2052/1.0 × 100 = 20.52%, matching the key.
7. Cross-check in equivalents: 2.85 × 10⁻³ eq of thiosulphate equals the electrons from chromium, and dividing by the 3-electron Cr factor gives 9.5 × 10⁻⁴ mol Cr, hence 1.9 × 10⁻³ mol Ag — the same route.
 n(thio) = 0.030*0.095 = 2.85e-3 mol
 Cr = thio/3 = 9.5e-4 mol
 Ag = 2*Cr = 1.9e-3 mol
 mass = 1.9e-3 * 108 = 0.2052 g
 % = 0.2052/1.0*100 = 20.52%          <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Two silvers ride on one chromate, but the three electrons are chromium’s, not silver’s. So n(Ag) = (2/3)·n(thio): tying the 3 electrons to silver, or forgetting the factor of 2 from Ag₂CrO₄, are the two standard slips.
Answer: 20.52% Ag.
4The chromium in a 1.87 g sample of chromite (FeOCr2O3) was oxidised to Cr6+ state by fusion with Na2O2. The fused mixture was treated with excess of water and boiled to destroy the excess of peroxide. After acidification, the sample was treated with 50 mL of 0.16 M FeSO4 solution. 2.97 mL of 0.05M Cr2O72− solution was required to oxidize the Fe2+ ion left unreacted. Determine the mass percentage of chromite in the original sample. [Cr = 52, Fe = 56]Chromite assayBack Titration and Purity Problems | JEE 2027 Chemistry (English)🟡 Similar14.20%
Concept This is the same back-titration logic as the FeCr₂O₄ problem. The ore chromium is fused with peroxide to Cr⁶⁺, which oxidises a measured excess of Fe²⁺ (3 electrons per Cr); the Fe²⁺ left unreacted is then titrated with standard dichromate, where one Cr₂O₇²⁻ takes 6 Fe²⁺. Subtracting the back-titre from the total gives the Fe²⁺ the ore consumed, which leads back to the mineral. The iron in the chromite is already oxidised and plays no part in the titration.
Key relation Fe(ore) = total Fe²⁺ − 6·n(dichromate); n(Cr) = Fe(ore)/3; n(chromite) = n(Cr)/2 FeOCr₂O₃ holds two chromiums, each a 3-electron oxidant.
Solution
1. Total Fe²⁺ = 0.050 × 0.16 = 8.0 × 10⁻³ mol.
2. n(dichromate) = 0.00297 × 0.05 = 1.485 × 10⁻⁴ mol, so excess Fe²⁺ = 6 × 1.485 × 10⁻⁴ = 8.91 × 10⁻⁴ mol.
3. Fe²⁺ used by the ore chromate = 8.0 × 10⁻³ − 8.91 × 10⁻⁴ = 7.109 × 10⁻³ mol.
4. n(Cr⁶⁺) = 7.109 × 10⁻³/3 = 2.370 × 10⁻³ mol, so n(chromite) = 2.370 × 10⁻³/2 = 1.185 × 10⁻³ mol.
5. M(FeOCr₂O₃) = 56 + 16 + 2×52 + 3×16 = 224, so mass = 1.185 × 10⁻³ × 224 = 0.2654 g.
6. % chromite = 0.2654/1.87 × 100 = 14.20%, matching the key.
7. Cross-check: the ore chromium is 2.370 × 10⁻³ mol = 0.1232 g of Cr, about 6.6% of the 1.87 g sample, a reasonable grade for a chromite ore.
 Fe tot = 0.050*0.16 = 8.0e-3 mol
 excess Fe = 6*(0.00297*0.05) = 8.91e-4 mol
 Fe by ore = 8.0e-3 - 8.91e-4 = 7.11e-3 mol
 Cr = Fe/3 = 2.37e-3 ; chromite = Cr/2 = 1.185e-3
 mass = 1.185e-3 * 224 = 0.2654 g
 % = 0.2654/1.87*100 = 14.20%         <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick FeOCr₂O₃ and FeCr₂O₄ are the same formula with M = 224. The iron atom is pre-oxidised to Fe³⁺ by the peroxide and never consumes the added FeSO₄, so subtract the back-titre first and route everything through chromium.
Answer: 14.20% chromite.
50.55 g of a sample containing KI is added to 50 mL 0.1 N KIO3 solution and acidified. The mixture is boiled to expel off all I2. The resulting solution is cooled down and excess of pure KI crystals are added. The solution required 21 mL of 0.0956 M thiosulphate solution to titrate and liberate I2. Determine mass percentage of KI in the original sample. [K = 39, I = 127]KI iodometric assay10 TITRATION INVOLVING IODINE | IODIMETRIC AND IODOMETRIC TITATION | IIT ADVANCED | JEE MAIN🟡 Similar90.3%
Concept The iodate first reacts with the sample’s iodide (IO₃⁻ + 5I⁻ + 6H⁺ → 3I₂) and that iodine is boiled off, so the KIO₃ consumed equals the sample KI. The KIO₃ that survives is then measured by adding fresh KI crystals and titrating the new I₂ with thiosulphate. Working throughout in equivalents keeps the 5-electron iodate factor hidden inside the normality and makes the difference method clean. The boiling step is essential: it removes the first batch of iodine so only the leftover iodate is measured.
Key relation eq(sample KI) = eq(KIO₃ total) − eq(KIO₃ left); eq(KIO₃ left) = eq(thiosulphate) the given 0.1 N already carries the 5-electron factor of IO₃⁻.
Solution
1. eq(KIO₃ total) = 0.1 N × 0.050 L = 5.0 × 10⁻³ eq.
2. In the second step n(thio) = 0.021 × 0.0956 = 2.008 × 10⁻³ mol = eq(thio) = eq(I₂) = eq(KIO₃ left).
3. eq(KIO₃ used by sample KI) = 5.0 × 10⁻³ − 2.008 × 10⁻³ = 2.992 × 10⁻³ eq.
4. For I⁻ → (1/2)I₂ each iodide loses one electron, so eq(KI) = n(KI) = 2.992 × 10⁻³ mol.
5. mass KI = 2.992 × 10⁻³ × 166 = 0.4967 g (M = 166).
6. % KI = 0.4967/0.55 × 100 = 90.3%, matching the key.
7. Cross-check: the leftover iodate (2.008 × 10⁻³ eq) plus the iodate consumed by the sample (2.992 × 10⁻³ eq) returns the full 5.0 × 10⁻³ eq taken, confirming the difference is self-consistent.
 eq(KIO3 total) = 0.1*0.050 = 5.0e-3 eq
 eq(KIO3 left) = thio = 0.021*0.0956 = 2.01e-3
 eq(KI sample) = 5.0e-3 - 2.01e-3 = 2.99e-3 eq
 n(KI) = 2.99e-3 mol ; M(KI) = 166
 mass = 2.99e-3 * 166 = 0.497 g
 % = 0.497/0.55*100 = 90.3%           <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Normality hides the IO₃⁻ + 5I⁻ balancing, so work in equivalents and the 5-electron factor never has to be written out. The "boil off the I₂" step is the whole trick: it lets the leftover iodate report the sample’s KI purely by difference.
Answer: 90.3% KI.
6A 0.3 g sample of an alloy of copper containing copper as CuO is dissolved in 50 mL dil sulphuric acid solution and to this solution was added 1.66 g of KI crystal. Copper is precipitated as CuI(s). The resulting solution is filtered off and filtrate was boiled till all iodine is evaporated off. To the resulting solution excess of KMnO4 solid was added and liberated iodine required 37 mL of 0.1 N Na2S2O3 solution to reach the equivalence point. Calculate the mass percentage of Cu in the alloy. (M : Cu = 63.5, I = 127 and K = 39)Copper iodometric assayiodometric titration of Copper using Na2S2O3🟡 SimilarCu = 66.15%
Concept This is an iodometric assay where Cu2+ oxidises iodide: 2Cu2+ + 4I− → 2CuI↓ + I2. That I2 is boiled off and thrown away, so the titration measures the left-over, unreacted iodide — not the copper’s iodine. Excess KMnO4 re-oxidises that leftover I− to I2, which thiosulphate then titrates.
Key relation meq(left I−) = meq(S2O32−); I− used by Cu = 2 × mol Cu each Cu buries one I in CuI and sends one I into the discarded I2.
Solution
1. KI taken = 1.66/166 = 0.01 mol = 10 mmol I− (K = 39, I = 127).
2. Thiosulphate = 37 × 0.1 = 3.7 meq; since I2 + 2S2O32− → 2I− + S4O62−, the leftover I− (n = 1) = 3.7 mmol.
3. In 2Cu2+ + 4I− → 2CuI + I2, each Cu consumes 2 I−; the I2 here is evaporated, so it is not in the titre.
4. I− used by Cu = 10 − 3.7 = 6.3 mmol ⇒ Cu = 6.3/2 = 3.15 mmol.
5. mass Cu = 3.15 × 63.5 = 200.0 mg ⇒ %Cu = 200.0/300 × 100 = 66.68%.
6. The key prints 66.15%, which needs 37.5 mL (leftover 3.75, Cu 3.125 mmol, 198.4 mg) — the stem’s “37 mL” is a misprint for 37.5 mL.
 KI            = 1.66/166 = 10.0 mmol I-
 thiosulphate  = 37 x 0.1 = 3.7 meq = left I-
 I- used by Cu = 10.0 - 3.7 = 6.3 mmol
 Cu            = 6.3/2   = 3.15 mmol
 mass Cu       = 3.15 x 63.5 = 200.0 mg
 %Cu           = 200.0/300 = 66.68%   (print)
 with 37.5 mL  -> 198.4 mg -> 66.15%  (key)
⚡ JEE Trick The trap is to titrate the copper’s own I2; here it is boiled off, so what KMnO4 liberates is the excess iodide. Cu = (total − excess)/2.
Answer: Cu ≈ 66.2% — key 66.15% (37.5 mL); the printed 37 mL gives 66.68%.
7A 5.0 g sample containing Pb3O4, PbO2 and some inert impurity is dissolved in 250 mL dil HNO3 solution and 2.68 g of Na2C2O4 was added, so that all lead converted into Pb2+. A 10 mL portion of this solution required 8.0 mL 0.02 M KMnO4 for titration of excess of oxalate. In an another experiment, 25 mL of solution was taken and excess oxalate was removed by extraction, this required 10 mL of a permanganate solution for oxidation of Pb2+ to Pb4+. 10 mL this permanganate solution is equivalent to 4.48 mL 5V H2O2 solution. Calculate mass percentage of PbO2 and Pb3O4 in the original sample. (At mass of Pb = 206)Pb3O4 / PbO2 mixture assayRedox Titration | Stoichiometry 2 | Reactions of KMnO4 and K2Cr2O7🟡 SimilarPb₃O₄ = 68.20%, PbO₂ = 23.80%
Concept Pb3O4 is 2PbO·PbO2, so both Pb3O4 and PbO2 carry exactly one Pb(IV) that oxidises oxalate. Known oxalate reduces all Pb(IV) to Pb2+; KMnO4 finds the leftover oxalate, and a permanganate (standardised vs 5V H2O2) re-oxidises Pb2+→Pb4+ to find total lead.
Key relation Pb(IV) equiv = oxalate consumed; a + b = Pb(IV); 3a + b = total Pb a = mmol Pb3O4, b = mmol PbO2.
Solution
1. Oxalate = 2.68/134 = 0.02 mol = 20 mmol = 40 meq (C2O42−, n = 2).
2. Excess oxalate: 10 mL needs 8.0 × 0.02 × 5 = 0.8 meq KMnO4; scale to 250 mL ⇒ 0.8 × 25 = 20 meq left.
3. Oxalate spent on Pb(IV) = 40 − 20 = 20 meq = Pb(IV) equiv (Pb4+ + 2e → Pb2+) ⇒ a + b = 20/2 = 10.
4. 5V H2O2: N = 5/5.6 = 0.893; 4.48 mL = 4.48 × 5/5.6 = 4.0 meq, so 10 mL permanganate = 4.0 meq.
5. In 25 mL, Pb2+→Pb4+ (n = 2) uses 4.0 meq ⇒ Pb = 2.0 mmol per 25 mL = 20 mmol in 250 mL ⇒ 3a + b = 20.
6. Solve 3a + b = 20 and a + b = 10 ⇒ a = 5, b = 5 mmol.
7. masses: Pb3O4 = 5 × 682 = 3410 mg = 68.2%; PbO2 = 5 × 238 = 1190 mg = 23.8%.
 oxalate total = 20 mmol = 40 meq
 excess oxalate= 0.8 x 25 = 20 meq
 Pb(IV): a+b   = (40-20)/2 = 10 mmol
 10 mL perm    = 4.48 x 5/5.6 = 4.0 meq
 total Pb: 3a+b= (4.0/2) x 10 = 20 mmol
 => a = 5 (Pb3O4), b = 5 (PbO2)
 Pb3O4 = 5x682 = 68.2% ; PbO2 = 5x238 = 23.8%
⚡ JEE Trick Count Pb(IV): one per Pb3O4 and one per PbO2, giving a + b; total Pb atoms give 3a + b. Two equations, two oxides — the H2O2 step is only to fix the permanganate strength.
Answer: Pb3O4 = 68.20%, PbO2 = 23.80%.
8A sample of Chrome-Vanadium steel weighing 2.0 g was dissolved in a mixture of sulphuric acid and just sufficient oxidant was added to raise the oxidation state of iron to Fe3+, the chromium to Cr2O72−, vanadium to VO3− and Mn to MnO4−. The solution was then treated with HCl and resulting solution still containing Fe3+, Cr2O72− and VO3− then treated with 25 mL of 1.0 M FeSO4. This resulted in reduction of dichromate and VO3− to Cr3+ and VO2+ in the solution respectively. Fe2+ and VO2+ in the solution was then titrated with 0.25 M KMnO4 and required 12.0 mL to reach the equivalence point. A small amount of Fe2+ was then added to again reduce the VO3− produced by KMnO4 back to VO2+ and this then titrated directly with 0.25 M KMnO4, a process requiring 1.00 mL to reach the equivalence point. Calculate the following quantities.
(a) (Moles of Fe2+ in 25 mL sample of standard FeSO4 solution.
(b) (Moles of Fe2+ titrated with 12.0 mL of standard KMnO4.
(c) (Moles of Fe2+ consumed by Cr2O72−. Percentage of V and Cr in the steel. [M of V = 51, Cr = 52]		Chrome-Vanadium steel analysisRedox Titration | Chemistry | JEE Advanced I JEE Main I Titration of KMnO4🟡 SimilarCr = 8.67%, V = 3.19%
Concept Fe2+ is the shuttle. Added Fe2+ reduces Cr2O72− → Cr3+ (6e) and VO3− → VO2+ (1e). The first KMnO4 titre oxidises (excess Fe2+ + VO2+); because VO2+ gives its electron back, only dichromate permanently drains Fe2+. A tiny second titre, after re-reducing just the vanadium, isolates V.
Key relation meq(12 mL KMnO4) = Fe2+(total) − 6·n(Cr2O72−) vanadium cancels in the first titre, so 25 − 15 is the dichromate’s Fe2+.
Solution
1. (a) Fe2+ added = 25 mL × 1.0 M = 25 mmol = 0.025 mol.
2. First titre = 12.0 × 0.25 × 5 = 15 meq = (excess Fe2+ + VO2+).
3. (c) Fe2+ drained by dichromate = 25 − 15 = 10 mmol = 0.010 mol (VO2+ returns its electron, so V cancels).
4. n(Cr2O72−) = 10/6 = 1.667 mmol ⇒ Cr = 2 × 1.667 = 3.333 mmol.
5. Second titre isolates V: 1.00 × 0.25 × 5 = 1.25 meq = VO2+ (n = 1) ⇒ V = 1.25 mmol.
6. (b) the 12 mL = 0.015 mol Fe-equivalents = excess Fe2+ 13.75 mmol + VO2+ 1.25 mmol.
7. %Cr = 3.333 × 52/2000 = 8.67%; %V = 1.25 × 51/2000 = 3.19%.
 (a) Fe2+       = 25 mL x 1.0 = 25 mmol
 12 mL KMnO4    = 12 x 0.25 x 5 = 15 meq
 (c) Fe2+->Cr   = 25 - 15 = 10 mmol
 Cr2O7 2-       = 10/6 = 1.667 mmol
 Cr             = 3.333 mmol -> 8.67%
 1 mL KMnO4     = 1.25 meq = V = 1.25 mmol
 V              = 1.25 mmol -> 3.19%
⚡ JEE Trick VO2+ is redox-reversible in the first titre, so it cancels and 25 − 15 lands straight on the dichromate’s Fe2+. Only the small 1 mL titre carries the vanadium.
Answer: (a) 0.025 mol; (b) 0.015 mol Fe-equiv; (c) 0.010 mol; Cr = 8.67%, V = 3.19%.
9A mineral containing tellurium was dissolved, so that all the tellurium was converted to TeO2. This was treated with a 50.00 mL portion of 0.0200 M potassium dichromate in an acidic medium. The reaction, as shown by the skeleton below, was expected.
TeO2 + Cr2O72− → H5TeO6− + Cr3+
Titration of the excess dichromate required 12.00 mL of 0.080 M FeSO4. How many mg of Te did the mineral sample contain? Molar mass of Te =177.6.		Tellurium dichromate assayBACK TITRATION |IIT JEE MAINS AND ADVANCE| |NEET| |CLASS 11|🟡 Similar447.55 mg
Concept A back-titration: a known excess of dichromate oxidises TeO2 (Te goes +4→+6 in H5TeO6−), and the dichromate left over is found with standard Fe2+. Te is got by difference. The trap is the Te n-factor: read it from the product’s oxidation state, not from the oxidant.
Key relation meq(Te) = meq(Cr2O72− total) − meq(Fe2+) everything compared as milli-equivalents, N × V.
Solution
1. Dichromate total = 50.00 × 0.0200 = 1.00 mmol = 6.00 meq (Cr2O72− + 6e → 2Cr3+).
2. Excess dichromate = Fe2+ = 12.00 × 0.080 = 0.96 mmol = 0.96 meq (Fe2+→Fe3+, n = 1).
3. Dichromate spent on Te = 6.00 − 0.96 = 5.04 meq (total minus the back-titrated excess).
4. Te n-factor: in H5TeO6−, O(−2)×6 + H(+1)×5 + charge(−1) ⇒ Te = +6; from TeO2 (Te = +4) the change is +4→+6, so n = 2.
5. Equivalents are conserved, so meq(Te) = 5.04 ⇒ mol Te = meq/n = 5.04/2 = 2.52 mmol.
6. mass Te = mol × M = 2.52 × 177.6 = 447.55 mg.
7. Sanity: Te uses 5.04 of the 6.00 meq (84%), so most of the dichromate reacted and only a small excess was back-titrated — consistent with the small 0.96 meq Fe2+ reading.
 Cr2O7 total = 50.00 x 0.0200 x 6 = 6.00 meq
 Fe2+ (excess)= 12.00 x 0.080 x 1 = 0.96 meq
 on Te        = 6.00 - 0.96 = 5.04 meq
 Te (+4 -> +6): n = 2
 mol Te       = 5.04/2 = 2.52 mmol
 mass Te      = 2.52 x 177.6 = 447.55 mg
⚡ JEE Trick Do not borrow the dichromate’s n = 6 for tellurium. Te’s n-factor is 2, fixed by +4→+6; copying n = 6 is the classic blunder that triples the mass.
Answer: 447.55 mg of Te.
10A new iron containing compound can have either of the two possible formulae K3[Fe(C2O4)3] or K2[Fe(C2O4)2(H2O)2]. To find, which is correct, you dissolve 1.356 g of the compound in acid, which converts oxalate to oxalic acid. The solution required 34.5 mL of 0.108 M KMnO4 to reach the equivalence point. What is the correct formula of compound?Iron oxalate formula determinationPreparation of Potassium trioxalatoferrate (III) with Calculation🟡 SimilarK₃[Fe(C₂O₄)₃]
Concept The two formulae differ in iron oxidation state: K3[Fe(C2O4)3] has Fe(III) (inert to MnO4−) and 3 oxalates, while K2[Fe(C2O4)2(H2O)2] has Fe(II) and 2 oxalates. Compute the KMnO4 each would demand per 1.356 g and match the measured 34.5 mL.
Key relation meq(KMnO4) = 2·n(oxalate) (+ 1·n(Fe2+) if iron is +2) oxalate n = 2, Fe2+ n = 1, MnO4− n = 5.
Solution
1. KMnO4 = 34.5 × 0.108 = 3.726 mmol = 18.63 meq (n = 5).
2. Formula A K3[Fe(C2O4)3]: M = 117 + 56 + 3×88 = 437; Fe is +3 (not oxidised).
3. mol A = 1.356/437 = 3.103 mmol ⇒ oxalate = 3 × 3.103 = 9.31 mmol ⇒ demand = 9.31 × 2 = 18.62 meq ≈ 18.63. Match.
4. Formula B K2[Fe(C2O4)2(H2O)2]: M = 78 + 56 + 2×88 + 2×18 = 346; Fe is +2.
5. mol B = 1.356/346 = 3.92 mmol ⇒ demand = oxalate(2×3.92×2 = 15.68) + Fe2+(3.92) = 19.6 meq — not 18.63.
6. Only A reproduces the titre: 18.62/(0.108×5) = 34.5 mL. So the compound is K3[Fe(C2O4)3].
 KMnO4 used = 34.5 x 0.108 x 5 = 18.63 meq
 A (M=437): mol = 1.356/437 = 3.103 mmol
   oxalate x2 = 3x3.103x2 = 18.62 meq  OK
 B (M=346): mol = 1.356/346 = 3.92 mmol
   oxalate 15.68 + Fe2+ 3.92 = 19.6 meq  NO
⚡ JEE Trick The oxidation state of iron decides whether it adds to the titre. The Fe(III) tris-oxalate (iron silent) matches 18.6 meq, so it is the formula.
Answer: K3[Fe(C2O4)3].

1Consider the following reactions:
NaCl + K2Cr2O7 + H2SO4 → A + KHSO4 + NaHSO4 + H2O
A + NaOH → B + NaCl + H2O
B + H2SO4 + H2O2 → C + Na2SO4 + H2O
In the product 'C', 'X' is the number of O22− units, 'Y' is the total number oxygen atoms present and 'Z' is the oxidation state of Cr. The value of X + Y + Z is ____ . [JEE Main 2026 (21 Jan. Shift 1)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2026 · 21 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Chromium peroxide productConsider the following reactions: NaCl+K2Cr2O7+H2SO4 A+KHSO4+NaHSO4+H2O A+NaOH to B+NaCl+H2O B+H2SO4+H2O2🟢 Exact13
Concept This is the chromyl-chloride chain. A = CrO2Cl2 (chromyl chloride, red vapour), B = Na2CrO4 (yellow chromate), and C = CrO5, the deep-blue perchromate formed by H2O2. The counting hinges on CrO5 keeping Cr at +6 because the peroxide oxygens are −1, not −2.
Key relation CrO5 = CrO(O2)2 one Cr=O oxo plus two side-on peroxide (O22−) units.
Solution
1. NaCl + K2Cr2O7 + H2SO4 → CrO2Cl2 (A).
2. A + NaOH → Na2CrO4 (B) + NaCl + H2O.
3. B + H2SO4 + H2O2 → CrO5 (C) + Na2SO4 + H2O.
4. X = peroxide units in CrO5 = 2 (two O22−).
5. Y = total O atoms = 1 (oxo) + 2×2 (peroxide) = 5.
6. Z = OS of Cr: two peroxides give −4, oxo gives −2, sum −6; neutral molecule ⇒ Cr = +6 (peroxide O is −1).
7. X + Y + Z = 2 + 5 + 6 = 13.
8. Cross-check: CrO5 is the deep-blue species of the chromium-peroxide test; its “butterfly” structure has one Cr=O oxo and two side-on peroxide O–O bridges, so O22− = 2, total O = 5, and Cr = +6 are mutually consistent for a neutral molecule.
 A = CrO2Cl2   B = Na2CrO4   C = CrO5
 CrO5 = CrO(O2)2
 X (O2^2- units)     = 2
 Y (total O atoms)   = 1 + 2x2 = 5
 Z (OS of Cr)        = +6
 X + Y + Z = 2 + 5 + 6 = 13
⚡ JEE Trick In CrO5 the four peroxide O are −1 each; treating all five O as −2 gives a wrong Cr = +10. Keep Cr at +6 — CrO5 is a Cr(VI) peroxide.
Answer: 13.
2500 mL of 1.2 M KI solution is mixed with 500 mL of 0.2 M KMnO4 solution in basic medium.
The liberated iodine was titrated with standard 0.1 M Na2S2O3 solution in the presence of starch indicator till the blue color disappeared. The volume (in L) of Na2S2O3 consumed is ____ . (Nearest integer) [JEE Main 2026 (28 Jan. Shift 1)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2026 · 28 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Iodometric titration KMnO4500 mL of 1.2 M KI solution is mixed with 500 mL of 0.2M KMnO4 solution in basic medium🟢 Exact3
Concept In basic (neutral) medium KMnO4 is reduced to MnO2, taking 3 electrons, and oxidises I− to I2. The liberated I2 is then titrated by thiosulphate. Chain the equivalents straight through; the whole answer turns on using n = 3 for permanganate, not 5.
Key relation meq(KMnO4) = meq(I2) = meq(S2O32−) electrons are conserved along the chain.
Solution
1. KI = 0.5 L × 1.2 M = 0.6 mol; KMnO4 = 0.5 × 0.2 = 0.1 mol.
2. Basic medium: MnO4− + 2H2O + 3e → MnO2 + 4OH−, so n = 3.
3. meq KMnO4 = 0.1 × 3 = 0.3 eq = 300 meq; iodide is in large excess, so permanganate limits.
4. I− → (1/2)I2 (n = 1): the I2 carries 300 meq ⇒ I2 = 150 mmol.
5. I2 + 2S2O32− → 2I− + S4O62−: meq S2O32− = 300 ⇒ 0.3 mol.
6. Check iodide is in excess: oxidising 300 meq of I− uses 300 mmol of the 600 mmol KI, so half the iodide is spare — KMnO4 is correctly the limiting reagent and the 150 mmol I2 figure is sound.
7. Volume of thiosulphate = moles / molarity = 0.3 mol / 0.1 M = 3 L (the nearest integer that the question asks for).
 KI    = 0.5 x 1.2 = 0.6 mol  (excess)
 KMnO4 = 0.5 x 0.2 = 0.1 mol
 basic: n = 3  -> 0.1 x 3 = 300 meq
 I2    = 300 meq / 2 = 150 mmol
 S2O3  = 300 meq = 0.3 mol
 V     = 0.3 / 0.1 = 3 L
⚡ JEE Trick The medium sets the n-factor: acidic KMnO4 is n = 5, but basic/neutral is n = 3 (→ MnO2). Using 5 here would wrongly give 5 L.
Answer: 3 L.
3X and Y are the number of electrons involved, respectively during the oxidation of I− to I2 and S2− to S by acidified K2Cr2O7. The value of X + Y is [JEE Main 2026 (24 Jan. Shift 1)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2026 · 24 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Electrons in oxidationX and Y are the number of electrons involved, respectively during the oxidation of I- to I2 and S2- to S by acidified K2Cr2O7🟢 Exact12
Concept “Electrons involved” here means the electrons transferred when each oxidation is balanced against one Cr2O72−, which accepts 6 electrons. So both half-reactions are scaled to 6 e, and X and Y each come out as 6.
Key relation Cr2O72− + 14H+ + 6e → 2Cr3+ + 7H2O the oxidant fixes 6 electrons per dichromate.
Solution
1. The dichromate half-reaction transfers 6 electrons per Cr2O72−.
2. I− → I2: 2I− → I2 + 2e; to match 6 e, 6I− → 3I2 + 6e ⇒ X = 6.
3. S2− → S: S2− → S + 2e; to match 6 e, 3S2− → 3S + 6e ⇒ Y = 6.
4. Full equations: Cr2O72− + 6I− + 14H+ → 2Cr3+ + 3I2 + 7H2O.
5. And Cr2O72− + 3S2− + 14H+ → 2Cr3+ + 3S + 7H2O.
6. Both balanced equations move 6 electrons ⇒ X + Y = 6 + 6 = 12.
7. The count is just the n-factor the oxidant imposes: one Cr2O72− accepts 6 e, so any reductant balanced against it must supply 6 e, whether the element releases 1 e (I, S) or more per atom. Hence X and Y are both 6, independent of the oxidised species.
 Cr2O7^2- + 6e -> 2Cr3+   (oxidant: 6 e)
 6 I-  -> 3 I2 + 6e       X = 6
 3 S2- -> 3 S  + 6e       Y = 6
 X + Y = 6 + 6 = 12
⚡ JEE Trick The electron count asked is what the oxidant accepts — 6 per dichromate — so each balanced oxidation carries 6, whatever the element. X = Y = 6.
Answer: 12.
4MnO42−, in acidic medium, disproportionates to : [JEE Main 2026 (21 Jan. Shift 1)]
(a) Mn2O7 and MnO2   (b) MnO4− and MnO   (c) MnO4− and MnO2   (d) Mn2O7 and MnO
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2026 · 21 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Manganate disproportionationMnO4^2-, in acidic medium, disproportionates to:🟢 Exact(c) MnO₄⁻ and MnO₂
Concept Manganate, MnO42−, has Mn at +6, an intermediate state stable only in strong alkali. In acid it disproportionates: part of the Mn is oxidised to +7 (MnO4−) and part reduced to +4 (MnO2).
Key relation 3MnO42− + 4H+ → 2MnO4− + MnO2 + 2H2O same element both oxidised and reduced.
Solution
1. Mn in MnO42− is +6.
2. Disproportionation needs the element to go both up and down from this one state.
3. Up: Mn+6 → Mn+7 (MnO4−), loses 1 e.
4. Down: Mn+6 → Mn+4 (MnO2), gains 2 e.
5. Balance electrons (2 up : 1 down): 3MnO42− + 4H+ → 2MnO4− + MnO2 + 2H2O.
6. Products are MnO4− and MnO2 ⇒ option (c).
7. Rule out the rest: Mn2O7 (in a and d) is an explosive covalent oxide not formed in aqueous acid, and MnO / MnO (b and d) would need Mn reduced to +2, far below the +4 that one-step disproportionation reaches. Only (c) pairs the correct +7 and +4 products.
 Mn(+6) up   -> Mn(+7) MnO4-   (-1 e) x2
 Mn(+6) down -> Mn(+4) MnO2    (+2 e) x1
 3 MnO4^2- + 4H+ ->
        2 MnO4- + MnO2 + 2 H2O
 products = MnO4- and MnO2  -> (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Green manganate survives only in strong base; acidify and it splits 2:1 into purple permanganate and brown MnO2. Mn2O7 (a) and MnO (b/d) never form in solution.
Answer: (c) MnO4− and MnO2.
5Consider the following reactions
K2Cr2O7 →[KOH, −H2O] A →[H2SO4, −H2O] B + K2SO4
The products A and B, respectively are : [JEE Main 2025 (23 Jan. Shift 2)]
(a) K2CrO4 and CrO   (b) K2CrO4 and Cr2O3   (c) K2CrO4 and K2Cr2O7   (d) K2Cr(OH)6 and Cr2O3
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2025 · 23 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
Dichromate chromate interconversionJEE mains 2025 PYQ Consider the following reactions. The products [A] and [B], respectively are K2Cr2O7 KOH H2SO4🟢 Exact(c) K₂CrO₄ and K₂Cr₂O₇
Concept Chromate and dichromate interconvert by pH, with Cr staying +6 throughout — this is acid–base, not redox. KOH gives yellow chromate (A); H2SO4 gives orange dichromate (B) plus K2SO4.
Key relation 2CrO42− + 2H+ ⇋ Cr2O72− + H2O base pushes left, acid pushes right; Cr is +6 on both sides.
Solution
1. K2Cr2O7 + 2KOH → 2K2CrO4 + H2O; so A = K2CrO4 (yellow).
2. Alkali drives the equilibrium to chromate.
3. 2K2CrO4 + H2SO4 → K2Cr2O7 + K2SO4 + H2O; so B = K2Cr2O7 (orange).
4. Acid drives it back to dichromate; K2SO4 is the named by-product.
5. Cr remains +6 the whole way — no electrons move.
6. A = K2CrO4, B = K2Cr2O7 ⇒ option (c).
7. The colour change confirms it: adding base to orange dichromate turns it yellow (chromate, A), and re-acidifying the yellow turns it orange again (dichromate, B). This reversible yellow↔orange swing is a classic indicator that no redox has occurred, only protonation equilibrium.
 K2Cr2O7 + 2KOH -> 2 K2CrO4 + H2O   (A)
 2 K2CrO4 + H2SO4 ->
        K2Cr2O7 + K2SO4 + H2O        (B)
 Cr stays +6 (acid-base, not redox)
 A = K2CrO4 , B = K2Cr2O7  -> (c)
⚡ JEE Trick No redox occurs, so reject any option with CrO (Cr2+) or Cr2O3 (Cr3+); Cr must stay +6, which only (c) keeps.
Answer: (c) K2CrO4 and K2Cr2O7.
6Preparation of potassium permanganate from MnO2 involves two step process in which the 1st step is a reaction with KOH and KNO3 to produce [JEE Main 2025 (24 Jan. Shift 1)]
(a) K3MnO4   (b) K4Mn(OH)6   (c) KMnO4   (d) K2MnO4
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2025 · 24 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
KMnO4 preparationPreparation of potassium permanganate from MnO2 involves two step process in which the 1st step is a reaction with KOH and KNO3🟢 Exact(d) K₂MnO₄
Concept KMnO4 is made from MnO2 in two steps. Step 1 is an oxidative alkali fusion: MnO2 is fused with KOH and an oxidant (KNO3 or air) to give green potassium manganate K2MnO4 (Mn goes +4→+6).
Key relation MnO2 + 2KOH + KNO3 → K2MnO4 + KNO2 + H2O KNO3 is the oxidiser (N: +5→+3).
Solution
1. Step 1 fuses MnO2 with KOH and KNO3 (or air/O2).
2. Balanced: MnO2 + 2KOH + KNO3 → K2MnO4 + KNO2 + H2O.
3. Mn: +4 (MnO2) → +6 (K2MnO4), losing 2 e to the nitrate.
4. The product of step 1 is K2MnO4, green potassium manganate.
5. Step 2 (not asked): K2MnO4 is oxidised (electrolytically, or by Cl2/O3) to KMnO4, +6→+7.
6. First-step product ⇒ option (d) K2MnO4.
7. Why an oxidant is needed: MnO2 alone with molten KOH would not change Mn’s state; KNO3 (or air) is there to pull Mn from +4 up to +6. Reject (a) K3MnO4 and (b) K4Mn(OH)6, which are not the manganate formed in this fusion.
 Step 1: MnO2 + 2KOH + KNO3 ->
         K2MnO4 + KNO2 + H2O   (Mn +4->+6)
 Step 2: K2MnO4 -> KMnO4       (Mn +6->+7)
 step-1 product = K2MnO4  -> (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Step 1 stops at Mn(+6) manganate; (c) KMnO4 (Mn +7) is the second-step product and the tempting wrong pick. The question asks only for the first step.
Answer: (d) K2MnO4.
7Which of the following oxidation reactions are carried out by both K2Cr2O7 and KMnO4 in acidic medium?
(A) I− → I2   (B) S2− → S   (C) Fe2+ → Fe3+   (D) I− → IO3−   (E) S2O32− → SO42−
Choose the correct answer from the options given below: [JEE Main 2025 (28 Jan. Shift 1)]
(a) C, D and E only   (b) B, C and D only   (c) A, D and E only   (d) A, B and C only
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2025 · 28 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Oxidising action comparisonWhich of following oxidation reactions are carried out by both K2Cr2O7 and KMnO4 in acidic medium?🟢 Exact(d) A, B and C only
Concept Both K2Cr2O7 and KMnO4 in acid are moderate oxidants. They share the mild oxidations — I−→I2, S2−→S, Fe2+→Fe3+ — but the deep “over-oxidations” to IO3− or SO42− are not common to both.
Key relation common set = mild oxidations only decide each listed change for both reagents.
Solution
1. (A) I−→I2: both oxidise iodide to iodine in acid. Common.
2. (B) S2−→S: both oxidise sulphide to sulphur. Common.
3. (C) Fe2+→Fe3+: the standard redox titre for both. Common.
4. (D) I−→IO3−: the usual product is only I2, so not shared.
5. (E) S2O32−→SO42−: dichromate, like iodine, tends to give S4O62−, so not shared.
6. Common to both = A, B, C ⇒ option (d).
7. Eliminate the wrong sets: (a), (b) and (c) each include D or E, which demand oxidation beyond the usual I2 / S4O62− endpoints, so no option but (d) keeps only the oxidations that both reagents reliably perform in acidic medium.
 A I- -> I2        both  : yes
 B S2- -> S        both  : yes
 C Fe2+ -> Fe3+    both  : yes
 D I- -> IO3-      no (stop at I2)
 E S2O3 -> SO4     no (dichromate -> S4O6)
 common = A, B, C  -> (d)
⚡ JEE Trick The shared, textbook oxidations stop at I2, S and Fe3+; the deep over-oxidation choices (D, E) are the distractors that break the “both” condition.
Answer: (d) A, B and C only.
8Given below are two statements:
Statement I: In the oxalic acid vs KMnO4 (in the presence of dil H2SO4) titration the solution needs to be heated initially to 60°C, but no heating is required in Ferrous ammonium sulphate (FAS) vs KMnO4 titration (in the presence of dil H2SO4)
Statement II: In oxalic acid vs KMnO4 titration, the initial formation of MnSO4 takes place at high temperature, which then acts as catalyst for further reaction. In the case of FAS vs KMnO4, heating oxidizes Fe2+ into Fe3+ by oxygen of air and error may be introduced in the experiment. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below [JEE Main 2025 (28 Jan. Shift 1)]
(a) Statement I is true but Statement II is false   (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are false   (c) Statement I is false but Statement II is true   (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are true
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2025 · 28 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Permanganate titration heatingStatement I: In the oxalic acid vs KMnO4 (in the presence of dil H2SO4) titration the solution needs to be heated to 60C🟢 Exact(b)Both Statements false ⚠
Concept These are standard volumetric-analysis facts. Oxalic acid vs KMnO4 is warmed (~60°C) because the reaction is sluggish cold and is autocatalysed by the Mn2+ it forms; FAS vs KMnO4 is run cold so that air does not oxidise Fe2+.
Key relation both statements are textbook-true and Statement II is the correct reason for Statement I.
Solution
1. Statement I, part 1: oxalic acid vs KMnO4 is heated to ~60°C. True — cold the reaction is too slow.
2. Statement I, part 2: FAS vs KMnO4 needs no heating. True — it is done at room temperature. So Statement I is TRUE.
3. Statement II, part 1: Mn2+ (MnSO4) formed autocatalyses the oxalate reaction. True.
4. Statement II, part 2: heating FAS lets O2 oxidise Fe2+→Fe3+, adding error. True. So Statement II is TRUE.
5. Both true and II explains I ⇒ the correct option is (d).
6. The key marks (b) “both false”, which contradicts both standard facts.
7. For (b) to hold, both procedures would have to be stated wrongly, yet every laboratory manual warms oxalic acid–KMnO4 to about 60°C and runs FAS–KMnO4 cold for exactly the reasons given, so the defensible answer is (d), both statements true with II explaining I.
 Oxalic acid vs KMnO4 : heat ~60 C  (slow cold)
   reason: Mn2+ autocatalysis
 FAS vs KMnO4 : no heat (room temp)
   reason: heat -> air oxidises Fe2+
 St I = TRUE , St II = TRUE -> (d)
 key says (b) both false    -> mismatch
⚡ JEE Trick FAS is titrated cold precisely to dodge aerial Fe2+ oxidation — that is Statement II explaining Statement I. Both stand, so the answer is (d), not (b).
Answer: (b) Both statements false — per the key; my working gives (d) Both statements true ⚠.
9Thiosulphate reacts differently with iodine and bromine in the reactions given below:
2 S2O32− + I2 → S4O62− + 2I−
S2O32− + 5Br2 + 5H2O → 2SO42− + 4Br− + 10H+
Which of the following statement justifies the above dual behaviour of thiosulphate? [JEE Main 2025 (8 April Shift 1)]
(a) Bromine is a stronger oxidant than iodine   (b) Thiosulphate undergoes oxidation by bromine and reduction by iodine in these reaction   (c) Bromine is a weaker oxidant than iodine   (d) Bromine undergoes oxidation and iodine undergoes reduction in these reactions
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 8 Apr · MorningSolution ↗
Thiosulphate dual behaviourThiosulphate reacts differently with iodine and bromine in the reactions given below🟢 Exact(a) Bromine is a stronger oxidant than iodine
Concept How far thiosulphate is oxidised depends on the oxidant’s strength. Weak I2 takes sulphur only from +2 to +2.5 (tetrathionate); strong Br2 drives it all the way to +6 (sulphate). In both, thiosulphate is the reductant.
Key relation oxidising power Br2 > I2 sets the depth of oxidation of S2O32−.
Solution
1. With I2: 2S2O32− + I2 → S4O62− + 2I−; S average +2 → +2.5 (mild).
2. With Br2: S2O32− + 5Br2 + 5H2O → 2SO42− + 4Br− + 10H+; S +2 → +6 (deep).
3. In both reactions thiosulphate is OXIDISED (it is the reductant), so (b) and (d) are wrong.
4. The only difference is the oxidant: Br2 (E° ≈ +1.07 V) is stronger than I2 (E° ≈ +0.54 V).
5. The stronger oxidant Br2 forces full oxidation to sulphate; the weaker I2 stops at tetrathionate.
6. Correct reason ⇒ (a) bromine is a stronger oxidant than iodine.
7. Note the electron bookkeeping: against I2 each thiosulphate loses 1 e (to +2.5), but against Br2 each loses 4 e (to +6, using 5 Br2 per two S). The deeper electron demand is met only because Br2 is the more powerful oxidant.
 I2  : 2 S2O3^2- -> S4O6^2-   (S +2 -> +2.5)
 Br2 : S2O3^2- -> 2 SO4^2-    (S +2 -> +6)
 S is oxidised in BOTH (reductant)
 E(Br2)=+1.07 V > E(I2)=+0.54 V
 stronger oxidant -> deeper oxidation -> (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Thiosulphate is always the reductant here; what changes is only how far it is pushed, and that is set purely by the oxidant’s strength — so (a).
Answer: (a) Bromine is a stronger oxidant than iodine.
10Iron (III) catalyses the reaction between iodide and persulphate ions, in which
(A) Fe3+ oxidises the iodide ion   (B) Fe3+ oxidises the persulphate ion   (C) Fe2+ reduces the iodide ion   (D) Fe2+ reduces the persulphate ion
Choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below: [JEE Main 2025 (8 April Shift 1)]
(a) B only   (b) A only   (c) B and C only   (d) A and D only
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 8 Apr · MorningSolution ↗
Iron catalysed persulphateIron (III) catalyses the reaction between iodide and persulphate ions, in which🟢 Exact(d) A and D only
Concept Fe(III) catalyses the slow I−/S2O82− reaction by splitting it into two fast steps and cycling between Fe3+ and Fe2+. The two repelling anions never have to meet directly.
Key relation Fe3+ oxidises I− (A); Fe2+ reduces S2O82− (D) catalyst regenerated each cycle.
Solution
1. Uncatalysed 2I− + S2O82− → I2 + 2SO42− is slow (two anions repel).
2. Step 1: 2Fe3+ + 2I− → 2Fe2+ + I2 — Fe3+ oxidises iodide (A).
3. Step 2: 2Fe2+ + S2O82− → 2Fe3+ + 2SO42− — Fe2+ reduces persulphate (D).
4. Fe3+ is regenerated, so iron is a true catalyst.
5. (B) and (C) reverse the roles (Fe3+ cannot reduce, Fe2+ cannot reduce iodide here), so they are wrong.
6. Operative statements = A and D ⇒ option (d).
7. Why catalysis speeds it up: the direct I−/S2O82− collision is slow because both are negative; the Fe3+/Fe2+ couple offers an ion-cation pathway of lower activation energy, and because iron is regenerated it is needed only in trace amounts.
 slow: 2 I- + S2O8^2- -> I2 + 2 SO4^2-
 (A) 2 Fe3+ + 2 I-  -> 2 Fe2+ + I2
 (D) 2 Fe2+ + S2O8^2- -> 2 Fe3+ + 2 SO4^2-
 Fe3+ regenerated -> catalyst
 A and D  -> (d)
⚡ JEE Trick A redox catalyst works by shuttling oxidation states: pick the two steps where Fe3+ oxidises iodide and Fe2+ reduces persulphate — A and D.
Answer: (d) A and D only.
11One of the products formed from the reaction of permanganate ion with iodide ion in neutral aqueous medium is [JEE Adv. 2025 (Paper-1)]
(a) I2   (b) IO3−   (c) IO4−   (d) IO2−
📝 PYQ: JEE Advanced 2025 · Paper 1Solution ↗
Permanganate iodide neutralJEE Advanced 2025 Paper-1 d-Block One of the products formed from the reaction of permanganate ion with iodide ion in neutral medium🟢 Exact(b) IO3−
Concept The oxidising power of permanganate depends on the medium. In acid it is reduced all the way to Mn2+, but in neutral or faintly alkaline solution it stops at MnO2 (Mn goes +7→+4, a 3-electron drop). The iodide it oxidises therefore goes further than I2: it reaches iodate.
Key relation 2MnO4− + I− + H2O → 2MnO2 + IO3− + 2OH− Electrons gained by Mn (6) match those lost by I (I− +5 → IO3−).
Solution
1. Neutral MnO4− half-reaction: MnO4− + 2H2O + 3e− → MnO2 + 4OH−.
2. Two MnO4− accept 6 electrons in all.
3. Those 6 electrons must come from iodine. If I− only reached I2 (loss of 1e per I), it could not absorb 6e from a single iodine atom.
4. Oxidising I− (−1) to iodate IO3− (+5) releases exactly 6 electrons per iodine — a clean match. That is why one I goes to IO3−, not I2.
5. Charge check: left −2−1 = −3; right −1(iodate) −2(OH) = −3. Balanced.
6. So a product is iodate, IO3− — option (b).
 MnO4- + 2H2O + 3e- -> MnO2 + 4OH-   (x2)
 I- -> IO3- + 6e-   (oxid. no. -1 -> +5)
 -----------------------------------------
 2MnO4- + I- + H2O -> 2MnO2 + IO3- + 2OH-
 product asked              <-- IO3-
⚡ JEE Trick Match the electron bookkeeping: 2 MnO4− need 6e, and only I−→IO3− supplies 6e per atom. I2 (a) is the acidic/weak-oxidant answer, not the neutral one.
Answer: (b) IO3−.
12Among the following halogens F2, Cl2, Br2 and I2 Which can undergo disproportionation reactions? [JEE Main 2025 (8 April Shift 1)]
(a) F2, Cl2 and Br2   (b) F2 and Cl2   (c) Only I2   (d) Cl2, Br2 and I2
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 8 Apr · MorningSolution ↗
Halogen disproportionationAmong the following halogens F2, Cl2, Br2 and I2, which can undergo disproportionation reactions?🟢 Exact(d) Cl2, Br2 and I2
Concept Disproportionation needs one element in an intermediate oxidation state so it can go both up and down. A halogen as X2 is at 0; it can rise to positive states (ClO−) and fall to −1. Fluorine is the exception: being the most electronegative element it has no positive oxidation state.
Key relation X2 + 2OH− → X− + XO− + H2O (X = Cl, Br, I) Requires a stable positive state XO−, which F cannot form.
Solution
1. Cl2 in cold alkali: Cl2 + 2OH− → Cl− + ClO− + H2O. Cl: 0→−1 and 0→+1. Disproportionates.
2. Br2 behaves the same → Br− + BrO− (or BrO3− when hot). Disproportionates.
3. I2 likewise → I− + IO3− in hot alkali. Disproportionates.
4. F2: F only ever shows 0 (as F2) and −1. No positive state exists, so it cannot go both ways.
5. With alkali F2 instead oxidises water: 2F2 + 2OH− → 2F− + OF2 + H2O — not a disproportionation of F.
6. So the set that disproportionates is Cl2, Br2, I2 — option (d).
 element   positive O.S.?   disproportionates?
 F2        NO               no
 Cl2       yes (+1,+5..)    yes
 Br2       yes              yes
 I2        yes              yes
 -----------------------------------------
 answer : Cl2, Br2 and I2        <-- (d)
⚡ JEE Trick The single fact that fluorine has no positive oxidation state kills any option containing F2. That eliminates (a) and (b) at a glance and leaves (d).
Answer: (d) Cl2, Br2 and I2.
13Given below are two statements :
Statement (I) : Fusion of MnO2 with KOH and an oxidising agent gives dark green K2MnO4.
Statement (II) : Manganate ion on electrolytic oxidation in alkaline medium gives permanganate ion. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below : [JEE Main 2025 (8 April Shift 2)]
(a) Statement I is true but Statement II is false   (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are false   (c) Statement I is false but Statement II is true   (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are true
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 8 Apr · EveningSolution ↗
Manganate permanganate preparationGiven below are two statements: Statement (I): Fusion of MnO2 with KOH and an oxidising agent gives dark green K2MnO4🟢 Exact(d) Both Statement I and Statement II are true
Concept This is the standard two-step laboratory route to KMnO4. First MnO2 is fused with KOH and an oxidant to the green manganate K2MnO4 (Mn +6); then the manganate is oxidised to purple permanganate KMnO4 (Mn +7), industrially by electrolysis.
Key relation 2MnO2 + 4KOH + O2 → 2K2MnO4 + 2H2O; then MnO42− → MnO4− + e− Mn climbs +4 → +6 → +7.
Solution
1. Statement I: fusing MnO2 with KOH and an oxidising agent (O2, KNO3 or KClO3) gives dark-green K2MnO4.
2. Oxidation-state check: Mn in MnO2 is +4, in K2MnO4 is +6 — an oxidation, so an oxidant is genuinely required. Statement I is true.
3. Statement II: the green manganate is then oxidised MnO42− → MnO4− + e−.
4. The industrial method is exactly electrolytic oxidation in alkaline medium, raising Mn +6 → +7. Matches the preparation given in the NCERT.
5. So Statement II is also true, and no causal link between them is claimed.
6. Both statements true → option (d).
 step 1 : MnO2 (+4) -fuse KOH/O2-> K2MnO4 (+6)
          dark green manganate       (Stmt I)
 step 2 : MnO4^2- (+6) -electrolysis->
          MnO4^- (+7) permanganate   (Stmt II)
 -----------------------------------------
 both true                 <-- (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Remember the colour/charge ladder: green MnO42− (+6) → purple MnO4− (+7). Both the fusion and the electrolytic step are textbook preparation of KMnO4, so both statements stand.
Answer: (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are true.
14The species which does not undergo disproportionation reaction is : [JEE Main 2025 (22 Jan. Shift 2)]
(a) ClO3−   (b) ClO−   (c) ClO2−   (d) ClO4−
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2025 · 22 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
Oxochlorine disproportionationThe species which does not undergo disproportionation reaction is: ClO4- ClO3- ClO2- ClO- | JEE 2025🟢 Exact(d) ClO4−
Concept An ion disproportionates only if its central atom is in an intermediate oxidation state — able to be both oxidised and reduced. For chlorine the maximum state is +7; once there it cannot climb higher, so it cannot disproportionate.
Key relation disproportionation needs 0 < O.S.(Cl) < +7 ClO4− has Cl at the ceiling (+7), so it is ruled out.
Solution
1. Assign Cl oxidation states: ClO− +1, ClO2− +3, ClO3− +5, ClO4− +7.
2. ClO− (+1) → Cl− (−1) + ClO3− (+5): disproportionates.
3. ClO2− (+3) → Cl− and ClO3−: disproportionates.
4. ClO3− (+5) → Cl− and ClO4−: disproportionates.
5. ClO4− (+7) is already the highest state of Cl; it can only be reduced, never oxidised. No upward path → no disproportionation.
6. Hence the species that does not disproportionate is ClO4− — option (d).
 ion        O.S. Cl   up possible?  dispropn?
 ClO-       +1        yes           yes
 ClO2-      +3        yes           yes
 ClO3-      +5        yes           yes
 ClO4-      +7        NO (max)      NO
 -----------------------------------------
 answer : ClO4-                <-- (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Spot the highest oxidation state in the list: an atom at its group-maximum (+7 for Cl) cannot be oxidised, so it can never disproportionate. One glance at ClO4− settles it.
Answer: (d) ClO4−.
152MnO4− + bI− + cH2O → xI2 + yMnO2 + zOH−
If the above equation is balanced with integer coefficients, the value of z is ____ . [JEE Main 2024 (30 Jan Shift 1)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 30 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Balancing permanganate iodideQ24 JEE Main Chemistry PYQ Jan 30 2024 Shift 1 | 2MnO4- + bI- + cH2O -> xI2 + yMnO2 + zOH-🟢 Exact8
Concept In neutral/alkaline medium permanganate is reduced to MnO2 (Mn +7→+4, 3e each) and iodide is oxidised to I2 (2I− → I2, 2e). Balancing electrons fixes the iodide coefficient, then charge and atoms give the hydroxide coefficient z.
Key relation electrons lost = electrons gained; then charge(left) = charge(right) 2 MnO4− accept 6e, so 6 I− are oxidised.
Solution
1. Reduction: MnO4− + 2H2O + 3e− → MnO2 + 4OH−. Two of these absorb 6e.
2. Oxidation: 2I− → I2 + 2e−. To release 6e need 6 I− → 3 I2, so b = 6, x = 3, y = 2.
3. Skeleton: 2MnO4− + 6I− + cH2O → 3I2 + 2MnO2 + zOH−.
4. Charge balance: left = (−2) + (−6) = −8; right = −z. So z = 8.
5. Hydrogen fixes c: 2c = z = 8 → c = 4. All H come from H2O and go into OH−.
6. Oxygen check: left 8 + 4 = 12; right 4(MnO2) + 8(OH) = 12. Balanced, so z = 8.
 MnO4- +2H2O +3e- -> MnO2 +4OH-   (x2)
 2I- -> I2 +2e-                   (x3)
 -----------------------------------------
 2MnO4- +6I- +4H2O -> 3I2 +2MnO2 +8OH-
 charge: -8 = -z  ->  z = 8       <-- z=8
⚡ JEE Trick z is just the net negative charge balance: the two reactant anions carry −8, and MnO2/I2 are neutral, so all −8 must sit on OH−. z = 8 without finishing the full equation.
Answer: z = 8.
16Consider the following redox reaction:
MnO4− + H+ + H2C2O4 ⇄ Mn2+ + H2O + CO2
The standard reduction potentials are given as below
E°MnO4−/Mn2+ = +1.51 V; E°CO2/H2C2O4 = −0.49 V
If the equilibrium constant of the above reaction is given as Keq = 10X, then the value of X = ____ (nearest integer) [JEE Main 2024 (01 Feb Shift 2)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 1 Feb · EveningSolution ↗
Equilibrium constant from EMFConsider the following redox reaction MnO4^- + H^+ + H2C2O4 → Mn^+2 + H2O + CO2🟢 Exact338
Concept Link the cell potential to the equilibrium constant through ΔG°. The permanganate couple is the cathode (reduction, +1.51 V) and the oxalic-acid/CO2 couple is the anode (oxidation). The number of electrons n comes from the balanced redox equation.
Key relation log K = nE°cell / 0.0591, with E°cell = E°cathode − E°anode From ΔG° = −nFE° = −2.303RT log K.
Solution
1. E°cell = E°(MnO4−/Mn2+) − E°(CO2/H2C2O4) = 1.51 − (−0.49) = 2.00 V.
2. Electrons per species: MnO4− → Mn2+ gains 5e; H2C2O4 → 2CO2 loses 2e.
3. Balance to equal electrons: 2MnO4− (10e) with 5H2C2O4 (10e), so n = 10. n is the moles of electrons exchanged in the balanced reaction.
4. log K = nE°cell/0.0591 = (10 × 2.00)/0.0591 = 20/0.0591.
5. 20/0.0591 = 338.4, so X = 338 to the nearest integer.
6. (With 0.059 one gets 339; the book’s key uses 0.0591/0.0592 → 338.)
 E_cell = 1.51 - (-0.49) = 2.00 V
 n = 10  (2 MnO4- x5e = 5 H2C2O4 x2e)
 log K = nE/0.0591 = 20/0.0591 = 338.4
 -----------------------------------------
 X = 338                       <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick A big E°cell (2 V) times n = 10 forces a huge log K — the reaction is essentially complete, which is why KMnO4/oxalate titration goes to completion. Watch n: it is 10, not 5.
Answer: X = 338.
17The difference in the 'spin-only' magnetic moment values of KMnO4 and the manganese product formed during titration of KMnO4 against oxalic acid in acidic medium is ____ BM. (nearest integer) [JEE Main 2024-6-Apr-Shift-1]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 6 Apr · MorningSolution ↗
Spin-only magnetic momentThe difference in the 'spin-only' magnetic moment values of KMnO4 and the manganese product....🟢 Exact6
Concept The spin-only magnetic moment depends only on the number of unpaired electrons n through μ = √(n(n+2)) BM. So the task reduces to finding the d-electron count of manganese in each species. In an acidic titration against oxalic acid, purple KMnO4 (Mn +7) is reduced to the almost-colourless Mn2+, and these two oxidation states have very different d-configurations.
Key relation μ = √(n(n+2)) BM, n = number of unpaired d-electrons Mn7+ is 3d0 (n=0); Mn2+ is 3d5 high-spin (n=5).
Solution
1. Manganese is in group 7, so neutral Mn is [Ar]3d54s2; stripping electrons gives the ion configurations below.
2. In KMnO4 manganese is +7: all 3d and 4s electrons are removed, giving [Ar]3d0, so n = 0 unpaired electrons.
3. μ(KMnO4) = √(0×(0+2)) = 0 BM — diamagnetic.
4. The acidic-medium titration product is Mn2+: [Ar]3d5, with all five d-electrons singly occupied, so n = 5. Oxalate reduces Mn +7 → +2 in H+ medium; in alkaline medium it would stop at MnO2 instead.
5. μ(Mn2+) = √(5×(5+2)) = √35 = 5.92 BM.
6. Difference = 5.92 − 0 = 5.92 BM, which rounds to 6 BM.
 KMnO4 : Mn(+7) 3d0  n=0  mu=0 BM
 product: Mn2+  3d5  n=5  mu=sqrt(5x7)
          = sqrt(35) = 5.92 BM
 -----------------------------------------
 diff = 5.92 - 0 = 5.92 ~ 6 BM  <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick KMnO4 is diamagnetic (d0, μ=0) despite its intense colour — the purple is charge-transfer, not d–d. So the whole difference is just the Mn2+ moment, √35 ≈ 6.
Answer: 6 BM.
18Chlorine undergoes disproportionation in alkaline medium as shown below :
aCl2(g) + bOH−(aq) → cClO−(aq) + dCl−(aq) + eH2O(l)
The values of a,b,c and d in a balanced redox reaction are respectively : [JEE Main 2024 (29 Jan Shift 1)]
(a) 1, 2, 1 and 1   (b) 2, 2, 1 and 3   (c) 3, 4, 4 and 2   (d) 2, 4, 1 and 3
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 29 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Chlorine disproportionation balancingChlorine undergoes disproportionation in alkaline medium ad shown below: aCl2(g) + bOH-(aq) = cClO-🟢 Exact(a) 1, 2, 1 and 1
Concept In cold dilute alkali chlorine disproportionates: one Cl goes up to +1 (ClO−) and one goes down to −1 (Cl−). Balancing one Cl2 directly gives the simplest integer coefficients.
Key relation Cl2 + 2OH− → ClO− + Cl− + H2O 1 electron transferred within one Cl2; OH− supplies O and H.
Solution
1. Chlorine is 0 in Cl2; it splits to +1 in ClO− and −1 in Cl− — a 1-electron up/down transfer.
2. Take one Cl2 (a = 1): it gives one ClO− (c = 1) and one Cl− (d = 1).
3. Balance oxygen: ClO− needs 1 O, available only from OH−.
4. Balance hydrogen: 2 OH− give 2 H → one H2O (e = 1), so b = 2. Two hydroxides, one water.
5. Charge check: left 2×(−1) = −2; right −1(ClO) −1(Cl) = −2. Balanced.
6. So a, b, c, d = 1, 2, 1, 1 — option (a).
 Cl2  +  2OH-  ->  ClO-  +  Cl-  +  H2O
 a=1    b=2       c=1      d=1     e=1
 Cl: 0 -> +1 (ClO-) and 0 -> -1 (Cl-)
 -----------------------------------------
 a,b,c,d = 1,2,1,1             <-- (a)
⚡ JEE Trick The cold-alkali chlorine reaction Cl2+2OH−→ClO−+Cl−+H2O is worth memorising; its coefficients 1,2,1,1 drop straight out. (Hot alkali gives ClO3− with different numbers.)
Answer: (a) 1, 2, 1 and 1.
19Total number of species from the following which can undergo disproportionation reaction ____ .
H2O2, ClO3−, P4, Cl2, Ag, Cu+1, F2, NO2, K+ [JEE Main 2024 (30 Jan Shift 2)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 30 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
Count disproportionation speciesTotal number of species from the following which can undergo disproportionation reaction H2O2 ClO3-🟢 Exact6
Concept A species disproportionates only if its key element sits in an intermediate oxidation state — one that can both rise and fall. Elements already at their lowest state (metals, F) or at a fixed state cannot.
Key relation disproportionation ⇔ intermediate oxidation state available both ways Test each of the nine species.
Solution
1. H2O2: O is −1 → O2(0) + H2O(−2). Yes. ClO3−: Cl +5 → Cl− + ClO4−. Yes.
2. P4: P 0 → PH3(−3) + H2PO2−(+1) in alkali. Yes.
3. Cl2: 0 → Cl− + ClO−. Yes.
4. Cu+: +1 → Cu(0) + Cu2+. Yes. NO2: N +4 → NO2−(+3) + NO3−(+5). Yes.
5. Ag (0, lowest state), F2 (no positive state), K+ (fixed +1): cannot disproportionate. No intermediate route exists for these.
6. Count of “yes” = H2O2, ClO3−, P4, Cl2, Cu+, NO2 = 6.
 H2O2  ClO3-  P4   Cl2   Cu+   NO2   -> YES (6)
 Ag    F2     K+                     -> NO
 -----------------------------------------
 total disproportionating = 6  <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Reject the three “dead-ends” fast: a free metal at 0 (Ag), fluorine (no +state), and a fixed alkali-metal ion (K+). Everything left is at an intermediate state — count = 6.
Answer: 6.
20Given below are two statements :
Statement I: S8 solid undergoes disproportionation reaction under alkaline conditions to form S2− and S2O32−.
Statement II: ClO4− can undergo disproportionation reaction under acidic condition.
In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below: [JEE Main 2024 (31 Jan Shift 2)]
(a) Statement I is correct but statement II is incorrect   (b) Statement I is incorrect but statement II is correct   (c) Both statement I and statement II are incorrect   (d) Both statement I and statement II are correct
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 31 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
Sulphur perchlorate disproportionationStatement I: S8 solid undergoes disproportionation reaction under alkaline conditions to form S2- an🟢 Exact(a) Statement I is correct but statement II is incorrect
Concept Both statements are disproportionation tests. Sulphur in S8 is at 0 — an intermediate state — so it can split both ways. Chlorine in ClO4− is at +7, its maximum, so it cannot be oxidised further and cannot disproportionate.
Key relation disproportionation needs an intermediate O.S.; +7 (ClO4−) is the ceiling for Cl S(0) qualifies, Cl(+7) does not.
Solution
1. Statement I: in hot alkali, S8 (S = 0) → S2− (−2) + S2O32− (avg +2).
2. Sulphur goes both down (to −2) and up (to +2): a genuine disproportionation. Statement I is correct.
3. Statement II: in ClO4− chlorine is +7, the highest state chlorine can reach.
4. With no higher state available, Cl cannot be oxidised, so it cannot disproportionate. It can only be reduced, which is plain reduction, not disproportionation.
5. So Statement II is incorrect.
6. Statement I correct, II incorrect → option (a).
 Stmt I : S8 (0) -alkali-> S2- + S2O3^2-
          S: 0 -> -2 and 0 -> +2   TRUE
 Stmt II: ClO4- has Cl at +7 (max)
          cannot be oxidised       FALSE
 -----------------------------------------
 I true, II false             <-- (a)
⚡ JEE Trick The perchlorate trap recurs every year: Cl at +7 is maxed out, so ClO4− never disproportionates. Pair that with the real S8/alkali reaction and only (a) fits.
Answer: (a) Statement I correct, Statement II incorrect.
21The number of ions from the following that are expected to behave as oxidising agent is:
Sn4+, Sn2+, Pb2+, Tl3+, Pb4+, Tl+ [JEE Main 2024-6-Apr-Shift-2]
(a) 3   (b) 2   (c) 1   (d) 4
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 6 Apr · EveningSolution ↗
Ions as oxidising agentsThe number of ions from the following that… | JEE Main 2024 Redox Reactions and Electrochemistry🟢 Exact(b) 2
Concept An ion acts as an oxidising agent if it is in a higher, less stable oxidation state and tends to drop to a lower one. For heavy p-block elements the inert-pair effect makes the lower state (Pb2+, Tl+) stable and the higher state (Pb4+, Tl3+) strongly oxidising.
Key relation higher unstable O.S. → oxidising agent (gets reduced) Inert-pair effect favours +2 for Pb and +1 for Tl.
Solution
1. Sn4+: +4 is the stable state of tin, so Sn4+ is only a weak oxidant — not counted.
2. Sn2+: lower state of tin, it is a reducing agent (Sn2+→Sn4+), not oxidising.
3. Pb2+ and Tl+: these are the inert-pair-stabilised lower states; stable, so not oxidising.
4. Tl3+: higher state, unstable by inert-pair effect → readily reduced to Tl+. Oxidising agent. Tl3+ + 2e → Tl+.
5. Pb4+: higher state, unstable → reduced to Pb2+. Oxidising agent.
6. Oxidising ions = Tl3+, Pb4+ = 2 — option (b).
 ion    state   inert-pair verdict
 Sn4+   stable  weak  (not counted)
 Sn2+   lower   reducing
 Pb2+/Tl+  lower stable  no
 Tl3+   higher  OXIDISING
 Pb4+   higher  OXIDISING
 -----------------------------------------
 count = 2                     <-- (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Down groups 13-14 the inert-pair effect makes the top oxidation state the oxidant: Tl3+ and Pb4+ both snap back to Tl+/Pb2+. Sn4+ is stable, so it does not count — just 2.
Answer: (b) 2.
22At room temperature, disproportionation of an aqueous solution of in situ generated nitrous acid (HNO2) gives the species [JEE Adv. 2024 (Paper-1)]
(a) H3O+, NO3− and NO   (b) H3O+, NO3− and NO2   (c) H3O+, NO− and NO2   (d) H3O+, NO3− and N2O
📝 PYQ: JEE Advanced 2024 · Paper 1Solution ↗
Nitrous acid disproportionationAt room temperature, disproportionation of an aqueous solution of in situ generated nitrous acid…🟢 Exact(a) H3O+, NO3− and NO
Concept Nitrous acid has nitrogen at the intermediate oxidation state +3, so in water it is unstable and disproportionates: part of the nitrogen is oxidised up to +5 (nitrate) and part reduced down to +2 (nitric oxide, NO). The acidic aqueous medium carries the released protons as the hydronium ion H3O+, so all three of these species coexist.
Key relation 3HNO2 → HNO3 + 2NO + H2O N: +3 → +5 (1 atom, loses 2e) and +3 → +2 (2 atoms, each gains 1e); electrons balance 2 = 2.
Solution
1. N in HNO2 is +3 — an intermediate state between NO3− (+5) and NO (+2), so disproportionation is possible.
2. Oxidation half: N +3 → +5 in NO3−, losing 2e per nitrogen.
3. Reduction half: N +3 → +2 in NO, gaining 1e per nitrogen. So two NO form per one nitrate to balance electrons (1 oxidised loses 2e = 2 reduced gain 2e).
4. Combine in the ratio 1 : 2 : overall 3HNO2 → HNO3 + 2NO + H2O.
5. In aqueous acidic solution the strong acid HNO3 is fully ionised: HNO3 + H2O → H3O+ + NO3−; NO is evolved as a gas.
6. So the species present are H3O+, NO3− and NO — option (a).
 3HNO2 -> HNO3 + 2NO + H2O
 N: +3 -> +5 (NO3-, -2e)
    +3 -> +2 (NO,   +1e) x2
 HNO3 -(aq)-> H3O+ + NO3-
 -----------------------------------------
 species: H3O+, NO3-, NO     <-- (a)
⚡ JEE Trick The disproportionation partners of +3 nitrogen are +5 (NO3−) and +2 (NO) — the two common stable neighbours. Options with NO2 (+4) or NO− are distractors.
Answer: (a) H3O+, NO3− and NO.
231 mole of PbS is oxidised by X moles of O3 to get Y moles of O2. X + Y = [JEE Main 2024 (27 Jan Shift 2)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 27 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
Ozone oxidation of PbS1 mole of PbS is oxidised by “X” moles of O3 to get “Y” moles of O2. X + Y =🟢 Exact8
Concept Ozone is a powerful oxidant. Here it oxidises the sulphide ion S(−2) in insoluble PbS all the way up to sulphate S(+6) in PbSO4, while every O3 molecule that acts is itself converted to ordinary O2. The number of ozone molecules needed, X, is fixed purely by electron balance; the number of O2 released, Y, then follows from the same molecules.
Key relation PbS + 4O3 → PbSO4 + 4O2 S loses 8e (−2→+6); each O3→O2 accepts 2e, so 8/2 = 4 O3.
Solution
1. Oxidation states: S in PbS is −2; S in PbSO4 is +6. The change is +8, so each sulphur loses 8 electrons.
2. Lead stays +2 throughout (Pb2+ in both PbS and PbSO4), so only sulphur is oxidised.
3. Ozone as oxidant: O3 + 2e− → O2 + O2−; one O of each O3 is reduced and enters the sulphate, a net gain of 2e per O3.
4. To absorb the 8 electrons released by one sulphur we need 8/2 = 4 O3, so X = 4. Electron demand 8 divided by 2 per ozone.
5. Each of those 4 O3 yields one O2, so 4 O2 are released; hence Y = 4. Atom check: O left 12 = O right (4 in SO4) + 8 (4 O2).
6. Therefore X + Y = 4 + 4 = 8.
 S: -2 -> +6  (loses 8 e-)
 O3 -> O2  accepts 2 e- each
 O3 needed = 8/2 = 4   (X = 4)
 PbS + 4O3 -> PbSO4 + 4O2  (Y = 4)
 -----------------------------------------
 X + Y = 4 + 4 = 8           <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick The clean stoichiometry PbS + 4O3 → PbSO4 + 4O2 comes straight from the 8-electron jump of sulphide to sulphate — 4 ozone in, 4 oxygen out, sum 8.
Answer: X + Y = 8.
24Number of moles of H+ ions required by 1 mole of MnO4− to oxidise oxalate ion to CO2 is ____ . [JEE Main 2024 (31 Jan Shift 2)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 31 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
H+ in permanganate oxalateNumber of moles of H+ ions required by 1 mole of MnO4- to oxidise oxalate ion to CO2 is🟢 Exact8
Concept In acidic permanganate–oxalate titration, MnO4− (Mn +7) is reduced to Mn2+ and oxalate C2O42− is oxidised to CO2. The H+ appears to supply the oxygen of MnO4− as water; count it per mole of MnO4−.
Key relation 2MnO4− + 16H+ + 5C2O42− → 2Mn2+ + 8H2O + 10CO2 Each MnO4− consumes 8 H+.
Solution
1. Reduction half: MnO4− + 8H+ + 5e− → Mn2+ + 4H2O.
2. The 4 oxygens of MnO4− leave as 4 H2O, requiring 8 H+ per MnO4−.
3. Oxidation half: C2O42− → 2CO2 + 2e−. Needed to balance electrons but does not change the H+-per-permanganate count.
4. Full equation (x2 and x5): 2MnO4− + 16H+ + 5C2O42− → 2Mn2+ + 8H2O + 10CO2.
5. H+ used = 16 for 2 MnO4−.
6. Per 1 mole MnO4− = 16/2 = 8 moles H+.
 MnO4- + 8H+ + 5e- -> Mn2+ + 4H2O
 (the 4 O leave as 4 H2O -> 8 H+)
 full: 2MnO4- +16H+ +5C2O4^2- ->
       2Mn2+ + 8H2O + 10CO2
 -----------------------------------------
 per 1 MnO4- : 16/2 = 8 H+    <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Read it off the reduction half-reaction alone: MnO4− + 8H+ + 5e → Mn2+ + 4H2O. The oxalate side is irrelevant to the H+-per-permanganate figure, which is 8.
Answer: 8 moles of H+.
25Only 2 mL of KMnO4 solution of unknown molarity is required to reach the end point of a titration of 20 mL of oxalic acid (2 M) in acidic medium. The molarity of KMnO4 solution should be M. [JEE Main 2024-4 Apr-Shift-1]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 4 Apr · MorningSolution ↗
Permanganate molarity titrationOnly 2 mL of KMnO4 solution of unknown molarity is required to reach the end point of a titration of🟢 Exact8
Concept At the end point of a redox titration the milli-equivalents of oxidant and reductant are exactly equal. Working in equivalents lets us skip the 2:5 mole ratio entirely. For KMnO4 in acid the n-factor is 5 (Mn +7→+2, gains 5e); for oxalic acid H2C2O4 it is 2 (two carbons each +3→+4, loses 2e total). Use meq = Molarity × n-factor × volume(mL).
Key relation MKMnO4×5×VKMnO4 = Mox×2×Vox Equivalents of oxidant = equivalents of reductant at the end point.
Solution
1. n-factor of KMnO4 in acidic medium = 5 (Mn+7 → Mn2+); n-factor of oxalic acid = 2.
2. Data: oxalic acid 20 mL of 2 M; KMnO4 only 2 mL, molarity M unknown.
3. meq of oxalic acid = 2 M × 2 × 20 mL = 80 meq.
4. meq of KMnO4 = M × 5 × 2 mL = 10M. Only 2 mL of titrant was used.
5. Equate at the end point: 10M = 80, so M = 80/10 = 8 M.
6. So the KMnO4 solution is 8 M — a very concentrated titrant, consistent with only 2 mL being needed against 40 meq of acid.
 end point:  eq(KMnO4) = eq(oxalic)
 M x 5 x 2  =  2 x 2 x 20
 10 M = 80
 M = 80/10
 -----------------------------------------
 M(KMnO4) = 8 M              <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Use n-factors (5 and 2), not mole ratios, so you never juggle the 2:5 stoichiometry. The tiny 2 mL titrant against 20 mL of 2 M acid signals a high molarity — 8 M.
Answer: 8 M.
26Reduction potential of ions are given below:
ClO4−    IO4−    BrO4−
E° = 1.19 V;   E° = 1.65 V;   E° = 1.74 V
The correct order of their oxidising power is: [JEE Main 2024 (30 Jan Shift 2)]
(a) ClO4− > IO4− > BrO4−   (b) BrO4− > IO4− > ClO4−   (c) BrO4− > ClO4− > IO4−   (d) IO4− > BrO4− > ClO4−
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 30 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
Oxidising power orderReduction potential of ions are given below: The correct order of their oxidising power is:🟢 Exact(b) BrO₄⁻ > IO₄⁻ > ClO₄⁻
Concept Oxidising power is the tendency to be reduced, i.e. to gain electrons. The more positive the standard reduction potential E°, the stronger the oxidising agent. The trap is to reason from electronegativity of the central halogen and reverse the order; the numbers decide it directly.
Key relation oxidising power ∝ E°red The species with the largest E° most readily accepts electrons, so it is the best oxidant.
Solution
1. Read the three reduction potentials: ClO4− = 1.19 V, IO4− = 1.65 V, BrO4− = 1.74 V.
2. Rank by E°: 1.74 > 1.65 > 1.19, so the oxidising-power order is BrO4− > IO4− > ClO4−. largest E° = strongest oxidant
3. BrO4− (perbromate) is the strongest; its high E° reflects the instability of Br in the +7 state, so it is reduced very readily.
4. Note the anomaly: ClO4− is the weakest here even though Cl is the most electronegative — a periodic irregularity, so you cannot guess from the group alone.
5. This matches option (b).
 species      E°(V)    oxidising power
 --------------------------------------
 BrO4-        1.74       strongest
 IO4-         1.65       middle
 ClO4-        1.19       weakest
 --------------------------------------
 order: BrO4- > IO4- > ClO4-     (b)
⚡ JEE Trick When reduction potentials are given, ignore electronegativity and group trends — just sort the E° values; the biggest number is the best oxidant. The distractor (a) is the tempting “Cl is most electronegative” guess, which the data overrules.
Answer: (b) BrO4− > IO4− > ClO4−.
27In the reaction of potassium dichromate, potassium chloride and sulfuric acid (conc.), the oxidation state of the chromium in the product is + ____ . [JEE Main 2024 (31 Jan Shift 2)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 31 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
Chromyl chloride oxidation stateIn the reaction of potassium dichromate, potassium chloride and sulfuric acid (conc.), the oxidation🟢 Exact6
Concept K2Cr2O7 warmed with a chloride and hot concentrated H2SO4 gives the orange-red vapour chromyl chloride, CrO2Cl2 (the chromyl chloride test for Cl−). This is a metathesis (double decomposition), not a redox step for chromium, so Cr keeps the same oxidation state it held in dichromate. The trap is to assume the dramatic colour and the strong acid mean chromium has been reduced.
Key relation x + 2(−2) + 2(−1) = 0 ⇒ x = +6 In CrO2Cl2 each O is −2 and each Cl is −1, and the molecule is neutral.
Solution
1. Identify the product: K2Cr2O7 + 4KCl + 6H2SO4(conc) → 2CrO2Cl2 + 6KHSO4 + 3H2O.
2. The coloured product asked about is chromyl chloride, CrO2Cl2.
3. Assign known states: O = −2, Cl = −1. standard for oxide and chloride
4. Let Cr = x and set the sum to zero: x + 2(−2) + 2(−1) = 0 ⇒ x − 4 − 2 = 0 ⇒ x = +6.
5. So chromium is in the +6 state — unchanged from +6 in the dichromate, confirming no redox of Cr.
6. The product is thus asked at +6; the colour change (orange-red fumes) is a physical sign of CrO2Cl2, not of any oxidation-number change.
 CrO2Cl2 :  O = -2 (x2),  Cl = -1 (x2)
 -------------------------------------
 x + 2(-2) + 2(-1) = 0
 x - 4 - 2 = 0
 x = +6                     <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick The chromyl chloride test transfers Cr from K2Cr2O7 (Cr = +6) to CrO2Cl2 with no change in oxidation number — it is a double-decomposition, not an oxidation. Do not be tempted to drop Cr to +3.
Answer: +6.
28Which of the following reactions are disproportionation reactions?
1 Cu+ → Cu2+ + Cu
2 3MnO42− + 4H+ → 2MnO4− + MnO2 + 2H2O
3 2KMnO4 → K2MnO4 + MnO2 + O2
4 2MnO4− + 3Mn2+ + 2H2O → 5MnO2 + 4H+
Choose the correct answer from the options given below: [JEE Main 2024 (01 Feb Shift 1)]
(a) 1, 2   (b) 2,3,4   (c) 1,2,4   (d) 1,4
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 1 Feb · MorningSolution ↗
Identify disproportionation reactionsQ4 JEE Main, Chemistry PYQ Feb 01, 2024, Shift 1 | Which of the following reactions are disproportio🟢 Exact(a) 1, 2
Concept In a disproportionation reaction one element in a single intermediate oxidation state is simultaneously oxidised and reduced, splitting into a higher and a lower state. Check every reaction for one element going both up and down; a second element carrying the change, or two different feed states merging into one, disqualifies it as disproportionation.
Key relation disproportionation: one element, one reactant state → both a higher and a lower state If only one element changes but moves in a single direction, or if two feed states converge, it is not disproportionation.
Solution
1. Cu+ → Cu2+ + Cu: Cu(+1) → +2 and 0. Same element up and down → disproportionation. (1 ✓)
2. 3MnO42− → 2MnO4− + MnO2: Mn(+6) → +7 and +4 → disproportionation. (2 ✓)
3. 2KMnO4 → K2MnO4 + MnO2 + O2: Mn(+7) only goes down (+6, +4) while O(−2)→0 goes up. Two different elements change → not disproportionation. (3 ✗)
4. 2MnO4− + 3Mn2+ → 5MnO2: Mn(+7) and Mn(+2) merge to +4 — that is comproportionation, the reverse. (4 ✗)
5. Only reactions 1 and 2 have a single element splitting both ways → option (a).
 rxn  element   change            verdict
 ----------------------------------------
 1    Cu   +1 -> +2 and 0        YES
 2    Mn   +6 -> +7 and +4       YES
 3    Mn down only; O up         NO (decomp)
 4    Mn +7,+2 -> +4             NO (compro)
⚡ JEE Trick Reaction 4 is the classic trap: it looks redox-ish but two states converge to one — comproportionation, not disproportionation. Reaction 3 fails because Mn moves only downward; the O atoms carry the oxidation.
Answer: (a) 1, 2.
29In acidic medium, K2Cr2O7 shows oxidising action as represented in the half reaction
Cr2O72− + XH+ + Ye− → 2A + ZH2O X,Y,Z and A are respectively are: [JEE Main 2024 (01 Feb Shift 1)]
(a) 8,6,4 and Cr2O3   (b) 14,7,6 and Cr3+   (c) 8,4,6 and Cr2O3   (d) 14,6,7 and Cr3+
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 1 Feb · MorningSolution ↗
Dichromate half reactionQ3 JEE Main, Chemistry PYQ Feb 01, 2024, Shift 1 | In acidic medium, K2Cr2O7 shows oxidising ...🟢 Exact(d) 14, 6, 7 and Cr³⁺
Concept In acidic medium dichromate is reduced from Cr(+6) to Cr(+3). Balance the half reaction in the fixed order: match the metal atoms, then oxygen with H2O, then hydrogen with H+, and finally charge with electrons. A is the dissolved chromium product, Cr3+ — not an oxide, because the medium is aqueous acid.
Key relation Cr2O72− + 14H+ + 6e− → 2Cr3+ + 7H2O Each Cr falls from +6 to +3, so 2 Cr absorb 2 × 3 = 6 electrons.
Solution
1. Product A = Cr3+ (not Cr2O3, since the medium is acidic solution), with 2 Cr on each side.
2. Balance O: 7 oxygens on the left need Z = 7 H2O on the right.
3. Balance H: 7 H2O carry 14 H, so X = 14 H+.
4. Balance charge (electrons Y): left = (−2) + 14(+1) + Y(−1) = 12 − Y; right = 2(+3) = +6; set 12 − Y = 6 ⇒ Y = 6.
5. Cross-check with oxidation number: 2 Cr × (6−3) = 6 electrons — matches Y = 6.
6. Matching the half reaction to the stem (2A = 2Cr3+), So X,Y,Z,A = 14, 6, 7, Cr3+ → option (d).
 Cr2O7(2-) + 14H+ + 6e- -> 2Cr3+ + 7H2O
 ---------------------------------------
 O: 7 left  = 7 H2O     -> Z = 7
 H: 7 H2O   = 14 H+     -> X = 14
 e: 2Cr, +6->+3 each    -> Y = 6
⚡ JEE Trick Options with Cr2O3 are traps: an oxide forms in the solid/alkaline route, not in acidic solution. Also 14 H+ and 6 e− are the dichromate numbers worth memorising for titration work.
Answer: (d) 14, 6, 7 and Cr3+.
30Which of the following cannot function as an oxidising agent? [JEE Main 2024 (27 Jan Shift 2)]
(a) N3−   (b) SO42−   (c) BrO3   (d) MnO4
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 27 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
Cannot act as oxidantWhich of the following cannot function as an oxidising agent?(1) N3- (2)SO42-(3) BrO3-(4) MnO4-🟢 Exact(a) N³⁻
Concept A species can act as an oxidising agent only if its key atom can still be reduced, i.e. it is not already at its lowest oxidation state. An element pinned at its minimum oxidation number has no lower state to drop to, so it can only be oxidised — it is a reducing agent, never an oxidant. So scan the central-atom oxidation state of each option and ask whether a lower state exists.
Key relation oxidant must gain e− ⇒ central atom must sit above its minimum oxidation state Nitrogen’s minimum state is −3; it cannot go lower, so N3− cannot be reduced.
Solution
1. N3−: nitrogen is at −3, its lowest possible state → cannot be reduced → cannot act as oxidant.
2. SO42−: S = +6 (its maximum), can be reduced to +4 / 0 / −2 → can oxidise.
3. BrO3−: Br = +5, reducible to Br2 or Br− → can oxidise.
4. MnO4−: Mn = +7 (maximum), strongly reducible to +2/+4 → can oxidise.
5. Only N3− has nowhere lower to go, so it alone cannot be an oxidant → the answer is (a).
 species    central OS   lower state?
 ------------------------------------
 N3-        -3 (min)     none  -> NOT oxidant
 SO4(2-)    S +6         yes
 BrO3-      Br +5        yes
 MnO4-      Mn +7        yes
⚡ JEE Trick “Cannot be an oxidant” = element already at its minimum oxidation state (here N at −3). The oxo-anions all hold their atom at a high state, so each is a ready electron acceptor — they are the distractors.
Answer: (a) N3−.
31In alkaline medium MnO4− oxidises I− to [JEE Main 2024 (29 Jan Shift 1)]
(a) IO4−   (b) IO−   (c) I2   (d) IO3−
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 29 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Permanganate iodide alkalineIn alkaline medium, MnO4 −, oxidises I– to II 29TH JANUARY ,2024 I JEE MAINS I MORNING SHIFT II🟢 Exact(d) IO₃⁻
Concept The product of iodide oxidation by permanganate depends on the medium. In acidic solution I− is oxidised only to I2, but in alkaline medium the oxidation goes further, taking iodine to the +5 iodate ion, IO3−.
Key relation I− (−1) → IO3− (+5), a 6-electron oxidation Alkaline MnO4− is a strong enough oxidant to push iodine to its +5 oxo-anion.
Solution
1. Oxidation number of I in I− is −1; the candidate products have I at: IO4− (+7), IO3− (+5), IO− (+1), I2 (0).
2. In alkaline medium permanganate oxidises iodide to iodate, IO3− (I: −1 → +5). the standard alkaline product
3. Balanced: 2MnO4− + I− + H2O → 2MnO2 + IO3− + 2OH−.
4. I2 (c) is the acidic-medium product, so it is wrong here.
5. IO4− (+7) is over-oxidation that MnO4− does not reach, and IO− is not the stable product → answer (d).
 medium       I- oxidised to     I state
 --------------------------------------
 acidic       I2                 0
 alkaline     IO3-               +5
 --------------------------------------
 alkaline product = IO3-          (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Tie the product to the medium: acidic → I2, alkaline → IO3−. The tempting distractor I2 is the acidic answer; the stem explicitly says alkaline, which sends iodine to +5.
