32Match List - I with List - II.
List-I (Reaction): (A) N2(g) + O2(g) → 2NO(g)   (B) 2 Pb(NO3)2(s) → 2PbO(s) + 4NO2(g) + O2(g)   (C) 2Na(s) + 2H2O(l) → 2NaOH(aq) + H2(g)   (D) 2NO2(g) + 2−OH(aq) → NO2−(aq) + NO3−(aq) + H2O(l)
List-II (Type of redox reaction): (I) Decomposition   (II) Displacement   (III) Disproportionation   (IV) Combination
correct answer from the options given below : [JEE Main 2024-6-Apr-Shift-2]
(a) (A)-(II), (B)-(III), (C)-(IV), (D)-(I)   (b) (A)-(III), (B)-(II), (C)-(I), (D)-(IV)   (c) (A)-(IV), (B)-(I), (C)-(II), (D)-(III)   (d) (A)-(I), (B)-(II), (C)-(III), (D)-(IV)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 6 Apr · EveningSolution ↗
Match reaction typeMatch List–I (Reaction) with List–II (Type of redox reaction)🟢 Exact(c) (A)-(IV), (B)-(I), (C)-(II), (D)-(III)
Concept Classify each redox reaction by its skeleton: two species joining into one product (combination), one species splitting into several (decomposition), a free element ousting another from a compound (displacement), or a single element going both up and down from one state (disproportionation). Read the equation shape first, then confirm with oxidation numbers.
Key relation type = shape of the equation + direction of oxidation-number change Each List-I reaction fits exactly one pattern, which fixes the match uniquely.
Solution
1. (A) N2 + O2 → 2NO: two elements combine into one product → Combination (IV).
2. (B) 2Pb(NO3)2 → 2PbO + 4NO2 + O2: one solid breaks into several products → Decomposition (I).
3. (C) 2Na + 2H2O → 2NaOH + H2: Na displaces H from water (H+ → H2) → Displacement (II).
4. (D) 2NO2 + 2OH− → NO2− + NO3− + H2O: N(+4) → +3 and +5 → Disproportionation (III).
5. Collecting the four matches: A-IV, B-I, C-II, D-III → option (c).
 A  N2 + O2 -> 2NO          combination   IV
 B  Pb(NO3)2 -> many        decomposition I
 C  Na + H2O -> H2          displacement  II
 D  NO2 -> NO2- + NO3-      dispropn      III
⚡ JEE Trick Spot (D) first: a single reactant (NO2, N at +4) giving both a lower (+3, in NO2−) and a higher (+5, in NO3−) nitrogen is the unmistakable disproportionation, D-III, which alone eliminates every option except (c) — you need not even verify A, B and C.
Answer: (c) (A)-(IV), (B)-(I), (C)-(II), (D)-(III).
33Which one among the following metals is the weakest reducing agent? [JEE Main 2023 (25 Jan. Shift 2)]
(a) K   (b) Rb   (c) Na   (d) Li
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 25 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
Weakest reducing metalWhich one among the following metals is the weakest reducing agent?🟢 Exact(c) Na
Concept Reducing power of a metal in aqueous solution is set by its standard reduction potential: the more negative E°red, the more readily the metal is oxidised and the stronger the reductant. The weakest reducing agent is therefore the metal with the least negative (highest) E°red. In water the order is not the clean group trend, because ionisation energy and hydration energy together decide it — and that makes Na, not Li, the anomaly.
Key relation reducing power ∝ (−E°red); weakest ⇒ highest E°red In water the overall energetics (ionisation + hydration) make Na the least easily oxidised of these alkali metals.
Solution
1. List standard reduction potentials: Li −3.04 V, Rb −2.98 V, K −2.93 V, Na −2.71 V.
2. The most negative, Li (−3.04 V), is the strongest reducing agent in water.
3. The least negative, Na (−2.71 V), is the weakest reducing agent. highest E°red → hardest to oxidise
4. The aqueous reducing order is Li > Rb > K > Na, with Na at the bottom.
5. All four are alkali metals, yet Na sits highest in E°red, so it loses electrons least willingly → the answer is (c) Na.
 metal   E°red (V)    reducing power
 -------------------------------------
 Li      -3.04        strongest
 Rb      -2.98
 K       -2.93
 Na      -2.71        weakest   (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Do not pick Li as “weakest” by its small size — Li’s huge hydration energy makes it the strongest reductant in water. The weakest is Na, the alkali metal with the least negative E°red.
Answer: (c) Na.
34During the qualitative analysis of SO32− using dilute H2SO4, SO2 gas is evolved which turns K2Cr2O7 solution (acidified with dilute H2SO4): [JEE Main 2023 (30 Jan. Shift 1)]
(a) Black   (b) Red   (c) Green   (d) Blue
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 30 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Sulphite dichromate colourDuring the qualitative analysis of SO32− using dilute H2SO4, SO2 gas is evolved which turns K2Cr2O7🟢 Exact(c) Green
Concept SO2 (released from a sulphite with dilute acid) is a reducing gas because sulphur sits at the intermediate +4 state. It reduces acidified orange dichromate, Cr(+6), to the green chromium(III) ion, Cr(+3), while it is itself oxidised to sulphate, S(+6). The orange → green colour change is the confirmatory observation used in the qualitative test for sulphite.
Key relation Cr2O72− + 3SO2 + 2H+ → 2Cr3+ + 3SO42− + H2O Cr falls +6 → +3 (green); S rises +4 → +6.
Solution
1. SO32− + dilute H2SO4 releases SO2 gas (S = +4), a good reductant.
2. SO2 reduces dichromate: Cr(+6) → Cr(+3). each Cr gains 3 electrons
3. Electron balance: 2 Cr need 6 e−; each SO2 (+4→+6) gives 2 e−, so 3 SO2 are consumed.
4. The chromium(III) ion Cr3+ imparts a green colour to the solution.
5. The orange dichromate colour is therefore replaced by the green of Cr3+ → option (c).
 orange Cr2O7(2-)  +  SO2(reducing)
 ----------------------------------
 Cr: +6 -> +3   (green Cr3+)
 S : +4 -> +6   (sulphate)
 ----------------------------------
 colour: orange --> green        (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Dichromate reduction always lands on green Cr3+ — the same green seen when SO2, H2S, Fe2+ or any reductant acts on acidified K2Cr2O7. “Blue” and “red” are colour-trap distractors with no chromium species to back them, and “black” would need a sulphide, not a sulphite.
Answer: (c) Green.
35See the following chemical reaction:
Cr2O72− + XH+ + 6Fe2+ → YCr3+ + 6Fe3+ + ZH2O
The sum of X, Y and Z is [JEE Main 2023 (13 April Shift 2)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 13 Apr · EveningSolution ↗
Dichromate ferrous balancingSee the following chemical reaction: Cr2O72-+XH++6Fe2+→YCr3++6Fe3++ZH2O The sum of X, Y ....🟢 Exact23
Concept Balance the acidic dichromate–ferrous redox equation, then add the three coefficients. Dichromate is reduced, Cr(+6)→Cr(+3), while ferrous is oxidised, Fe(+2)→Fe(+3). The electron count (6 Fe give 6 e−, 2 Cr take 6 e−) together with oxygen and hydrogen balance fixes X, Y and Z, and a charge check confirms the set.
Key relation Cr2O72− + 14H+ + 6Fe2+ → 2Cr3+ + 6Fe3+ + 7H2O 6 Fe each give 1 e−, matching the 6 e− taken by 2 Cr.
Solution
1. Electrons: 6Fe2+ → 6Fe3+ release 6 e−; 2 Cr (+6→+3) absorb 6 e− → Y = 2 Cr3+.
2. Oxygen: the 7 O of Cr2O72− go to Z = 7 H2O.
3. Hydrogen: 7 H2O need 14 H, so X = 14 H+.
4. Charge check: left = (−2) + 14 + 6(+2) = +24; right = 2(+3) + 6(+3) = +24. balanced
5. The stem already prints 6Fe2+ and 6Fe3+, so only X, Y, Z are unknown, and all three now follow.
6. Sum X + Y + Z = 14 + 2 + 7 = 23.
 Cr2O7(2-)+14H+ +6Fe2+ -> 2Cr3+ +6Fe3+ +7H2O
 -------------------------------------------
 X = 14 (H+)
 Y = 2  (Cr3+)
 Z = 7  (H2O)
 X + Y + Z = 23            <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the dichromate skeleton: 14 H+, 6 e−, 2 Cr3+, 7 H2O. With 6 Fe2+ supplying exactly the 6 electrons, the coefficients drop out instantly and X+Y+Z = 23.
Answer: 23.
36In the scheme given below, X and Y, respectively, are
Metal halide →[aq. NaOH] White precipitate (P) + Filtrate (Q)
P →[aq. H2SO4, PbO2 (excess), heat] X (a coloured species in solution)
Q →[MnO(OH)2, Conc. H2SO4, warm] Y (gives blue – coloration with KI – starch paper) [JEE Adv. 2023 (Paper-1)]
(a) CrO42− and Br2   (b) MnO42− and Cl2   (c) MnO4− and Cl2   (d) MnSO4 and HOCl		Coloured species identificationIIT JEE Advanced 2023 P-1, Sec-2, Q-4 [Qualitative Analysis - MnCl2, KMnO4, Cl2, KI-Starch]🟢 Exact(c) MnO₄⁻ and Cl₂
Concept Trace the scheme. A metal halide with aq. NaOH gives a white hydroxide (P) plus a halide-bearing filtrate (Q). P oxidised by PbO2 in acid gives a coloured ion X; Q oxidised by MnO(OH)2/conc. H2SO4 gives a gas Y that blues KI–starch paper.
Key relation Mn2+ —PbO2/H+→ MnO4−; Cl− —oxidn→ Cl2 The white hydroxide and the oxidisable halide together pin the salt as MnCl2.
Solution
1. White precipitate P with NaOH → Mn(OH)2; so the metal is Mn and P = Mn(OH)2, filtrate Q holds the halide Cl−.
2. P + PbO2 + conc. H2SO4, heat: Mn(+2) is oxidised to MnO4− (+7), the purple permanganate — X. PbO2 is a strong oxidant
3. So the salt is MnCl2; Q contains chloride ions.
4. Q + MnO(OH)2 + conc. H2SO4, warm: Cl− is oxidised to Cl2 gas — Y.
5. Cl2 liberates I2 from KI, giving the blue starch–iodine colour — confirming Y = Cl2 → option (c).
 MnCl2 + NaOH -> Mn(OH)2 (P,white) + Cl- (Q)
 P + PbO2/H2SO4  -> MnO4- (X, purple)
 Q + MnO(OH)2/H2SO4 -> Cl2 (Y)
 Cl2 + KI-starch -> blue             (c)
⚡ JEE Trick “White hydroxide + oxidised by PbO2 to a purple ion” is the Mn2+ → MnO4− fingerprint; the blue KI–starch test then flags a halogen (Cl2). CrO42−/MnO42− options miss that PbO2 drives Mn all the way to +7.
Answer: (c) MnO4− and Cl2.
37Which of the following options are correct for the reaction?
2Au(CN)2−(aq) + Zn(s) → 2Au(s) + Zn(CN)42−(aq)
(A) Redox reaction   (B) Displacement reaction   (C) Decomposition reaction   (D) Combination reaction
Choose the correct answer from the options given below: [JEE Main 2023 (06 Apr Shift 1)]
(a) A only   (b) A and D only   (c) A and B only   (d) C and D only
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 6 Apr · MorningSolution ↗
Reaction type classificationWhich of the following options are correct for the reaction 2Au(CN)2(aq)-+Zn(s)→2Au(s)....🟢 Exact(c) A and B only
Concept Decide the reaction type from the oxidation-number changes and the equation skeleton. A free metal (Zn) pushing another metal (Au) out of its complex ion, with electrons transferred between them, is a redox reaction that is specifically a displacement. Nothing breaks into fragments and nothing fuses into a single product, so it is neither a decomposition nor a combination.
Key relation Au(+1) → Au(0); Zn(0) → Zn(+2) Electrons move from Zn to Au, so the process is redox; Zn taking Au’s place makes it a displacement.
Solution
1. In Au(CN)2−, with CN = −1, gold is +1; in the product Au(s) it is 0 → gold is reduced.
2. Zinc goes from 0 in Zn(s) to +2 in Zn(CN)42− → zinc is oxidised.
3. Electron transfer occurs, so it is a redox reaction → (A) correct.
4. The free metal Zn ejects Au from its cyanide complex — a displacement → (B) correct. this is the MacArthur–Forrest gold-recovery step
5. The only correct labels are therefore redox and displacement, so C and D fail → option (c).
 Au: +1 -> 0     reduced
 Zn:  0 -> +2    oxidised
 --------------------------------
 redox  = YES (A)
 displacement = YES (B)
 decomposition/combination = NO
⚡ JEE Trick A single free element replacing another element in a compound is always both redox and displacement — the two labels travel together, so once you spot the metal-for-metal swap you know A and B both hold. There is no fragmentation or fusion in the equation, so decomposition and combination are automatic distractors to reject on sight.
Answer: (c) A and B only.
38During the reaction of permanganate with thiosulphate, the change in oxidation of manganese occurs by value of 3. Identify which of the below medium will favour the reaction. [JEE Main 2023 (06 April Shift 2)]
(a) Both aqueous acidic and neutral   (b) Aqueous neutral   (c) Both aqueous acidic and faintly alkaline   (d) Aqueous acidic
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 6 Apr · EveningSolution ↗
Permanganate thiosulphate mediumDuring the reaction of permanganate with thiosulphate, the change in oxidation of manganese occurs b🟢 Exact(b) Aqueous neutral
Concept The product of permanganate — and hence the oxidation-number drop of Mn — depends entirely on the medium. The stem fixes the Mn change at 3 units, i.e. Mn(+7) → Mn(+4) deposited as brown MnO2. That specific 3-electron drop is the signature of a neutral (or faintly alkaline) medium, not an acidic one, where permanganate would instead go all the way to colourless Mn(+2).
Key relation Δ(ox. no.) = 7 − 4 = 3 ⇒ MnO4− → MnO2 Mn → MnO2 (a 3-electron reduction) is the neutral-medium behaviour of permanganate.
Solution
1. Acidic medium: MnO4− → Mn2+, a change of 7 − 2 = 5 — too big.
2. Strongly alkaline: MnO4− → MnO42−, a change of 7 − 6 = 1 — too small.
3. Neutral / faintly alkaline: MnO4− → MnO2, a change of 7 − 4 = 3 — matches. this is the required drop of 3
4. Permanganate oxidising thiosulphate to sulphate while forming MnO2 is exactly the neutral-medium reaction.
5. Only the neutral route gives the stated 3-unit change, so the favouring medium is aqueous neutral → option (b).
 medium        Mn product   Δ ox. no.
 ------------------------------------
 acidic        Mn2+         5
 neutral       MnO2         3   <-- wanted
 strong alk.   MnO4(2-)     1
⚡ JEE Trick Pin the medium from the electron count: Δ5 → acidic, Δ3 → neutral, Δ1 → strongly alkaline. The “both acidic and ...” options are traps, because acidic forces the full +7 → +2 drop of 5, not 3.
Answer: (b) Aqueous neutral.
392IO3− + xI− + 12H+ → 6I2 + 6H2 . What is the value of x ? [JEE Main 2023 (08 April Shift 1)]
(a) 2   (b) 12   (c) 10   (d) 6
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 8 Apr · MorningSolution ↗
Iodate iodide balancing2IO3- + xI- + 12H+ → 6I2 + 6H2O. What is the value of x?🟢 Exact(c) 10
Concept In 2IO3− + xI− + 12H+ → 6I2 + 6H2O, iodate (I = +5) and iodide (I = −1) comproportionate to I2 (I = 0). The coefficient x follows from a simple iodine atom balance, which the electron balance confirms. (Note: the printed “6H2” is a misprint for 6H2O.)
Key relation I atoms: 2 + x = 2 × 6 = 12 ⇒ x = 10 All iodine on both sides must balance, giving x directly.
Solution
1. Iodine atoms, left: 2 (from IO3−) + x (from I−); right: 6I2 = 12.
2. Set equal: 2 + x = 12 ⇒ x = 10.
3. Electron check: 2 IO3− gain 2 × 5 = 10 e− (+5→0); 10 I− lose 10 × 1 = 10 e− (−1→0) — balanced. confirms x = 10
4. Charge check: left = 2(−1) + 10(−1) + 12(+1) = 0; right = 0. neutral both sides
5. H and O also balance (12 H+ → 6 H2O carrying 12 H; 6 O from 2 IO3− → 6 O in water), so every count is consistent with x = 10 → option (c).
 2IO3- + 10 I- + 12H+ -> 6 I2 + 6 H2O
 ------------------------------------
 I atoms: 2 + x = 12   -> x = 10
 e- : 2x5 gained = 10x1 lost  (OK)
 charge: -2 -10 +12 = 0       (OK)
⚡ JEE Trick For comproportionation just balance the element that changes state — here iodine — and x falls out in one line. The distractor 12 (b) wrongly counts all 12 product I atoms as coming from I−, ignoring the 2 from iodate.
Answer: (c) 10.
40Given below are two statements:
Statement I: In redox titration, the indicators used are sensitive to change in pH of the solution.
Statement II: In acid-base titration, the indicators used are sensitive to change in oxidation potential. In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below [JEE Main 2023 (08 April Shift 2)]
(a) Statement I is correct but Statement II is incorrect   (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are incorrect   (c) Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct   (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are correct
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 8 Apr · EveningSolution ↗
Redox titration indicatorsStatement I : In redox titration, the indicators used are sensitive to change in pH of the solution.🟢 Exact(a)Statement I correct, II incorrect ⚠
Concept The two indicator classes respond to different quantities. A redox indicator changes colour with the oxidation (electrode) potential of the solution; an acid-base indicator changes colour with the pH (hydrogen-ion concentration). The statements swap these, so test each against the correct rule.
Key relation redox indicator ↔ E (oxidation potential); acid-base indicator ↔ pH Each indicator is sensitive to the variable that drives its own titration’s endpoint.
Solution
1. Statement I: “redox-titration indicators are sensitive to pH.” Redox indicators (e.g. ferroin, diphenylamine) switch with the oxidation potential, not pH → incorrect.
2. Statement II: “acid-base indicators are sensitive to oxidation potential.” Acid-base indicators (e.g. phenolphthalein) switch with pH, not potential → incorrect.
3. The two statements have simply exchanged the correct sensitivities, so both are false.
4. “Both incorrect” is option (b); the key instead keeps Statement I as correct → option (a).
5. Re-checking: a redox indicator by definition responds to the electrode potential — there is no reading on which Statement I is true, so my working is (b). key appears defective here
 indicator type   sensitive to    stmt says
 ------------------------------------------
 redox            ox. potential   pH   (I: FALSE)
 acid-base        pH              Eox  (II:FALSE)
 ------------------------------------------
 both false -> (b);  key prints (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Match each indicator to its own endpoint variable: redox → potential, acid-base → pH. Both statements cross-wire these, so the clean answer is “both incorrect.” Treat the key’s (a) with caution.
Answer: (a) Statement I is correct but Statement II is incorrect, per the key — my working gives (b) both statements are incorrect ⚠
41Given below are two statements:
Statement I : Aqueous solution of K2Cr2O7 is preferred as a primary standard in volumetric analysis over Na2Cr2O7 aqueous solution.
Statement II : K2Cr2O7 has a higher solubility in water than Na2Cr2O7. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below: [JEE Main 2023 (10 April Shift 1)]
(a) Statement I is false but Statement II is true   (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are true   (c) Statement I is true but Statement II is false   (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are false
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 10 Apr · MorningSolution ↗
Dichromate primary standardAqueous solution of K2Cr2O7 is preferred as a primary standard in volumetric analysis over Na2Cr2O7 aqueous solution🟢 Exact(c) Statement I true, Statement II false
Concept A primary standard must be obtainable in high purity, be non-hygroscopic, stable to air and heat, and have a definite formula so that a single weighing fixes an exact molarity. K2Cr2O7 satisfies all of these; the trap is the solubility claim in Statement II.
Key relation purity + non-hygroscopic + fixed formula → primary standard K2Cr2O7 crystallises pure and dry, whereas Na2Cr2O7 is deliquescent.
Solution
1. K2Cr2O7 can be recrystallised to high purity and dried without decomposing, so a weighed mass gives an exact amount. Statement I is true.
2. Na2Cr2O7 is deliquescent (hygroscopic): it picks up atmospheric moisture, so its weighed mass is uncertain. That is why it cannot serve as a primary standard.
3. Hence the potassium salt is preferred for direct weighing in volumetric work.
4. Solubility: Na2Cr2O7 is in fact far more soluble than K2Cr2O7 — its high solubility is why industry uses the sodium salt.
5. So Statement II (K2Cr2O7 more soluble) is false.
6. Statement I true and Statement II false → option (c).
 primary standard wants:
   pure, dry, non-hygroscopic, fixed formula
 K2Cr2O7 : yes        -> Statement I TRUE
 Na2Cr2O7: deliquescent AND more soluble
 Statement II (K2Cr2O7 more soluble) FALSE
 ------------------------------------------
 I true, II false                 <-- (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Na2Cr2O7 is the cheap, more soluble but hygroscopic salt; K2Cr2O7 is the lab primary standard. Any option that calls K2Cr2O7 “more soluble” is the planted trap.
Answer: (c) Statement I is true but Statement II is false.
42In alkaline medium, the reduction of permanganate anion involves a gain of ____ electrons. [JEE Main 2023 (10 April Shift 2)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 10 Apr · EveningSolution ↗
Permanganate reduction electronsIn alkaline medium, the reduction of permanganate anion involves a gain of ________ electrons.🟢 Exact3
Concept Permanganate's reduction product depends entirely on the medium: acidic gives Mn2+ (a 5-electron gain), neutral or faintly alkaline gives MnO2 (3 electrons), and strongly alkaline gives the manganate MnO42− (1 electron). The standard NCERT “alkaline” permanganate reaction, which this JEE question uses, is the one forming MnO2.
Key relation MnO4− + 2H2O + 3e− → MnO2 + 4OH− electrons gained = fall in the oxidation number of Mn.
Solution
1. Oxidation state of Mn in MnO4−: O is −2 (×4 = −8), overall charge −1, so Mn = +7.
2. In neutral or faintly alkaline medium the product is MnO2, in which O is −2 (×2 = −4) on a neutral molecule, so Mn = +4.
3. Electrons gained per Mn = 7 − 4 = 3.
4. Balance the half-reaction in base: MnO4− + 2H2O + 3e− → MnO2 + 4OH−. Charge is −1−3 = −4 on the left and 4×(−1) = −4 on the right; O and H also balance.
5. So the gain is 3 electrons, matching the key.
6. Aside: in strongly alkaline medium only the one-electron step MnO4− → MnO42− (Mn +6) occurs; the question's “alkaline medium” is taken as the usual faintly-alkaline route to MnO2.
 Mn in MnO4-  = +7
 alkaline product MnO2 : Mn = +4
 electrons = 7 - 4 = 3
 MnO4- + 2H2O + 3e- -> MnO2 + 4OH-
 charge check : -1-3 = -4 = 4(-1)
 ------------------------------------------
 electrons gained = 3             <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Three media, three products: Mn2+ (5e, acid), MnO2 (3e, neutral/faint alkali), MnO42− (1e, strong alkali). The “faintly alkaline → MnO2” route is the 3-electron default the examiner expects here.
Answer: 3 electrons.
43KMnO4 is titrated with ferrous ammonium sulphate hexahydrate in presence of dilute H2SO4. Number of water molecules produced for 2 molecules of KMnO4 is ____ [JEE Main 2023 (13 April Shift 1)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 13 Apr · MorningSolution ↗
Water from FAS titrationKMnO4 is titrated with ferrous ammonium sulphate hexahydrate in presence of dilute H2SO4. Number of water molecules produced for 2 molecules of KMnO4🟢 Exact68
Concept Water comes from two sources here: the redox reaction itself and the six waters of crystallisation carried by each Mohr's salt unit, FeSO4·(NH4)2SO4·6H2O. The word hexahydrate is the whole question — counting only redox water gives the wrong answer.
Key relation 2MnO4− + 10Fe2+ + 16H+ → 2Mn2+ + 10Fe3+ + 8H2O 2 KMnO4 oxidise 10 Fe2+, i.e. 10 FAS units, each bringing 6 H2O.
Solution
1. Each MnO4− gains 5 electrons and oxidises 5 Fe2+; so 2 KMnO4 need 10 Fe2+.
2. Molecular equation: 2KMnO4 + 10FAS + 8H2SO4 → K2SO4 + 2MnSO4 + 5Fe2(SO4)3 + 8H2O.
3. The redox part produces 8 water molecules per 2 KMnO4.
4. FAS (ferrous ammonium sulphate hexahydrate) is FeSO4·(NH4)2SO4·6H2O: 6 crystal waters each.
5. 10 FAS × 6 = 60 water molecules released on dissolution.
6. Crystal water comes from the 10 FAS units needed to reduce the 2 KMnO4, not from a separate reagent — so it is legitimately “produced” when the salt dissolves.
7. Total = 8 (redox) + 60 (crystal water) = 68 = 8 + 60 = 68.
 2 KMnO4 oxidise 10 Fe2+   (5 e- each)
 redox water          : 8
 FAS = FeSO4.(NH4)2SO4.6H2O
 crystal water : 10 x 6 = 60
 ------------------------------------------
 total H2O = 8 + 60 = 68          <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick “Hexahydrate” is doing the heavy lifting: 60 of the 68 waters are crystal water. Miss them and you answer 8, the exact distractor the examiner plants; the tie between 10 FAS and the 6 waters each is the only arithmetic that matters.
Answer: 68 water molecules.
44The total change in the oxidation state of manganese involved in the reaction of KMnO4 and potassium iodide in the acidic medium is ____ . [JEE Main 2023 (15 April Shift 1)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 15 Apr · MorningSolution ↗
Manganese oxidation changeThe total change in the oxidation state of manganese involved in the reaction of KMnO4 and potassium iodide in acidic medium🟢 Exact5
Concept In acidic medium permanganate is reduced all the way to Mn2+. The “total change in the oxidation state of manganese” is just the magnitude of its oxidation-number drop, which equals the electrons gained per Mn.
Key relation MnO4− (+7) + 8H+ + 5e− → Mn2+ (+2) + 4H2O +7 down to +2 is a change of 5.
Solution
1. KMnO4 in acidic medium is reduced to Mn2+; overall 2MnO4− + 10I− + 16H+ → 2Mn2+ + 5I2 + 8H2O.
2. Mn oxidation state in MnO4−: 4(−2) + Mn = −1, so Mn = +7.
3. Mn oxidation state in the free Mn2+ ion = +2.
4. Total change = 7 − 2 = 5. This equals the 5 electrons taken up by each Mn.
5. KI supplies those electrons (2I− → I2 + 2e−), but the quantity asked is only the change on Mn.
6. Hence the total change in the oxidation state of manganese = 5.
 acidic MnO4- -> Mn2+
 Mn : +7  ->  +2
 change = 7 - 2 = 5
 ( = 5 electrons gained per Mn )
 2MnO4- + 10I- + 16H+ -> 2Mn2+ + 5I2 + 8H2O
 ------------------------------------------
 total change on Mn = 5           <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Acidic permanganate always lands on Mn2+, so the Mn change is a fixed 5 (7→2). Do not add the iodine side — the question asks only about manganese, and “total change” here means the single Mn jump, not a sum over atoms.
Answer: 5.
458 H2S (5 moles) reacts completely with acidified aqueous potassium permanganate solution. In this reaction, the number of moles of water produced is x, and the number of moles of electrons involved is y. The value of (x + y) is ____ . [JEE Adv. 2023 (Paper-2)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Advanced 2023 · Paper 2Solution ↗
H2S permanganate x plus yH2S (5 moles) reacts completely with acidified aqueous potassium permanganate solution — moles of water x and electrons y (JEE Adv 2023)🟢 Exact18
Concept Acidified KMnO4 oxidises H2S to sulphur (S: −2 → 0, 2e each) while MnO4− is reduced to Mn2+ (5e). Balance the equation, then read off water (x) and electrons (y) for 5 mol H2S.
Key relation 2MnO4− + 5H2S + 6H+ → 2Mn2+ + 5S + 8H2O electrons balance: 2×5 = 5×2 = 10.
Solution
1. Oxidation half: H2S → S + 2H+ + 2e−.
2. Reduction half: MnO4− + 8H+ + 5e− → Mn2+ + 4H2O.
3. LCM of electrons = 10: 2MnO4− + 5H2S + 6H+ → 2Mn2+ + 5S + 8H2O. H: 10+6 = 16 = 8×2; O: 8 = 8; charge: −2+6 = +4 = 2×(+2).
4. The equation is written for exactly 5 mol H2S, giving 8 mol H2O, so x = 8.
5. Electrons transferred for 5 mol H2S = 5 × 2 = 10, so y = 10 (equivalently, the 2 MnO4− each gain 5, also 10).
6. Both x and y are read for the whole 5 mol H2S, so no further scaling is needed.
7. x + y = 8 + 10 = 18.
 H2S -> S + 2H+ + 2e-         (x2.5 scaled)
 MnO4- + 8H+ + 5e- -> Mn2+ + 4H2O
 2MnO4- + 5H2S + 6H+ -> 2Mn2+ + 5S + 8H2O
 x (water, 5 mol H2S) = 8
 y (electrons = 5 x 2) = 10
 ------------------------------------------
 x + y = 8 + 10 = 18              <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Balance for 5 H2S and both answers fall out: water is the 8 in the equation, electrons are just 2 per H2S. No need to track the 5 moles separately — the stem already fixed them.
Answer: 18.
46The number of electrons involved in the reduction of permanganate to manganese dioxide in acidic medium is ____ . [JEE Main 2023 (30 Jan. Shift 1)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 30 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Permanganate to MnO2 electronsThe number of electrons involved in the reduction of permanganate to manganese dioxide in acidic medium🟢 Exact3
Concept Reduction of permanganate to manganese dioxide means manganese changes from +7 to +4, so the number of electrons involved is just that difference in oxidation number. The phrase “in acidic medium” only fixes how H and O are balanced with H+ and H2O; it does not alter the electron count, which depends solely on the two Mn oxidation states.
Key relation MnO4− + 4H+ + 3e− → MnO2 + 2H2O electrons = change in the oxidation state of Mn.
Solution
1. Mn in MnO4−: 4(−2) + Mn = −1 gives Mn = +7.
2. Mn in MnO2: 2(−2) + Mn = 0 gives Mn = +4.
3. Electrons gained per Mn = 7 − 4 = 3.
4. Balance the half-reaction in acid: add 2H2O on the right for the two spare oxygens, then 4H+ on the left; MnO4− + 4H+ + 3e− → MnO2 + 2H2O. Charge: −1+4−3 = 0, matching neutral MnO2.
5. Both sides now balance in mass and charge, confirming 3 electrons.
6. So the reduction of permanganate to MnO2 involves 3 electrons.
 Mn in MnO4- = +7
 Mn in MnO2  = +4
 electrons = 7 - 4 = 3
 MnO4- + 4H+ + 3e- -> MnO2 + 2H2O
 charge : -1 + 4 - 3 = 0  (= MnO2)
 ------------------------------------------
 electrons = 3                    <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Electron count = drop in oxidation number, nothing else. Mn2+ (acidic, 5e) and MnO2 (neutral/weak-base, 3e) are the two to keep apart — here the product is explicitly MnO2, so the answer is 3, not 5.
Answer: 3.
47KMnO4 oxidises I− in acidic and neutral/faintly alkaline solution, respectively to [JEE Main 2023 (30 Jan. Shift 2)]
(a) I2 and IO3−   (b) IO3− and I2   (c) IO3− and IO3−   (d) I2 and I2
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 30 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
Permanganate iodide productsKMnO4 oxidises I- in acidic and neutral/faintly alkaline solution, respectively, to🟢 Exact(d)I₂ and I₂ ⚠
Concept What KMnO4 oxidises iodide to depends on the medium. In acid the product is iodine, I2; in neutral or faintly alkaline solution the stronger relative oxidising environment carries iodide all the way to iodate, IO3−. The two products are different.
Key relation acidic: I− → I2;  neutral/faint alkali: I− → IO3− standard NCERT reactions of permanganate with iodide.
Solution
1. Acidic medium: 2MnO4− + 16H+ + 10I− → 2Mn2+ + 5I2 + 8H2O. Product = I2.
2. Neutral/faintly alkaline: 2MnO4− + H2O + I− → 2MnO2 + 2OH− + IO3−. Product = IO3−.
3. So, respectively, the products are I2 and IO3− — that is option (a).
4. Option (d) I2 and I2 would require iodate to be reduced back to iodine in faintly alkaline medium, which does not happen here.
5. The working therefore gives (a), while the printed key gives (d).
 acidic : I- -> I2
   2MnO4- + 16H+ + 10I- -> 2Mn2+ + 5I2 + 8H2O
 neutral/faint alkali : I- -> IO3-
   2MnO4- + H2O + I- -> 2MnO2 + 2OH- + IO3-
 ------------------------------------------
 I2 and IO3-  = (a)    key prints (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Remember the ladder: acid stops at I2, neutral/weakly basic pushes iodide up to iodate IO3−. If both entries in an option are identical, be suspicious — the two media rarely give the same product.
Answer: (d) I2 and I2 per the key — my working gives (a) I2 and IO3− ⚠
48Acidified potassium permanganate solution oxidises oxalic acid. The spin-only magnetic moment of the manganese product formed from the above reaction is B.M. ____ (Nearest Integer) [JEE Main 2022 (27 Jun Shift 1)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 27 Jun · MorningSolution ↗
Spin-only moment Mn productAcidified potassium permanganate solution oxidises oxalic acid. The spin-only magnetic moment of the manganese product🟢 Exact6
Concept When acidified KMnO4 oxidises oxalic acid, the manganese ends up as Mn2+; the spin-only magnetic moment depends only on the number of unpaired d-electrons in that product. So the whole problem is: identify the Mn product, count its unpaired electrons, and apply the spin-only formula. The trap is to use the moment of MnO4− (d0, diamagnetic) instead of the reduced ion.
Key relation μ = √(n(n+2)) BM n = number of unpaired electrons in the ion.
Solution
1. 2MnO4− + 5C2O42− + 16H+ → 2Mn2+ + 10CO2 + 8H2O, so the Mn product is Mn2+.
2. Atomic Mn is [Ar]3d54s2; forming Mn2+ removes the two 4s electrons, leaving [Ar]3d5.
3. The 3d5 set is exactly half-filled, so by Hund's rule all 5 electrons are unpaired: n = 5.
4. Apply the formula: μ = √(5(5+2)) = √(5×7) = √35 = 5.92 BM.
5. Rounding 5.92 to the nearest integer gives 6.
6. So the spin-only moment of the manganese product is 6 BM (nearest integer).
 acid KMnO4 + oxalic -> Mn2+
 Mn2+ : [Ar] 3d5  (half-filled)
 unpaired n = 5
 mu = sqrt(5(5+2)) = sqrt(35) = 5.92 BM
 nearest integer = 6
 ------------------------------------------
 spin-only moment = 6 BM          <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Mn2+ (d5) is the classic 5-unpaired, 5.92 BM ion — the single most-asked magnetic-moment value. Rounding 5.92 gives 6, not 5; and never take the moment of the purple MnO4−, which is d0 and diamagnetic.
Answer: 6.
49A 2.0 g sample containing MnO2 is treated with HCl liberating Cl2. The Cl2 gas is passed into a solution of KI and 60.0 mL of 0.1M Na2S2O3 is required to titrate the liberated iodine. The percentage of MnO2 in the sample is ____ Nearest integer) [Atomic masses (in u) Mn=55; Cl=35.5: O=16, I=127, Na=23, K=39, S=32] [JEE Main 2022 (28 Jun Shift 1)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 28 Jun · MorningSolution ↗
Percentage MnO2 iodometryA 2.0 g sample containing MnO2 is treated with HCl liberating Cl2 — percentage of MnO2 by iodometry🟢 Exact13
Concept A chain of 1:1 redox equivalences links the sample to the titre: one MnO2 liberates one Cl2, which liberates one I2, which consumes two thiosulphate. Work back from the measured thiosulphate to moles of MnO2.
Key relation MnO2 ≡ Cl2 ≡ I2 ≡ 2 S2O32− each step transfers 2 electrons; M(MnO2) = 55 + 32 = 87.
Solution
1. MnO2 + 4HCl → MnCl2 + Cl2 + 2H2O (Mn4+→Mn2+, 1 Cl2 per MnO2).
2. Cl2 + 2I− → 2Cl− + I2; then I2 + 2S2O32− → 2I− + S4O62−.
3. Moles S2O32− = 0.0600 L × 0.1 M = 6.0×10−3.
4. Moles I2 = (6.0×10−3)/2 = 3.0×10−3 = moles Cl2 = moles MnO2.
5. Mass MnO2 = moles × molar mass = 3.0×10−3 × 87 = 0.261 g.
6. The whole 2.0 g sample contains this 0.261 g of MnO2, the rest being inert.
7. % MnO2 = (0.261/2.0) × 100 = 13.05 ≈ 13.
 n(S2O3 2-) = 0.0600 x 0.1 = 6.0e-3
 n(I2)  = 6.0e-3 / 2 = 3.0e-3
 n(Cl2) = n(MnO2) = 3.0e-3
 mass MnO2 = 3.0e-3 x 87 = 0.261 g
 % = 0.261 / 2.0 x 100 = 13.05
 ------------------------------------------
 % MnO2 = 13 (nearest int)        <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick The whole chain is 1:1 except I2:thiosulphate = 1:2. Halve the thiosulphate moles once and you have moles of MnO2 directly — molarity and 87 g/mol finish it.
Answer: 13.
5020 mL of 0.02M K2Cr2O7 solution is used for the titration of 10 mL of Fe2+ solution in the acidic medium. The molarity of Fe2+ solution is ____ ×10−2 M [JEE Main 2022-27 July-Shift-1]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 27 Jul · MorningSolution ↗
Ferrous molarity dichromate20mL of 0.02M K2Cr2O7 solution is used for the titration of 10mL of Fe2+ solution in acidic medium — molarity of Fe2+🟢 Exact24
Concept At the end point the milli-equivalents of oxidant and reductant are equal. Dichromate has an n-factor of 6 (each of the 2 Cr goes +6→+3, so 2×3 = 6 electrons), while Fe2+ has n-factor 1. Equate meq and solve for the ferrous molarity; the only trap is using moles instead of equivalents and dropping the factor of 6.
Key relation n1M1V1 = n2M2V2 meq(Cr2O72−) = meq(Fe2+) at equivalence.
Solution
1. Cr2O72− + 14H+ + 6e− → 2Cr3+ + 7H2O, so the n-factor of dichromate = 6.
2. Fe2+ → Fe3+ + e−, so the n-factor of ferrous = 1.
3. Set meq equal: n1M1V1 = n2M2V2, i.e. 6 × 0.02 × 20 = 1 × M × 10.
4. Left side = 6 × 0.02 × 20 = 2.4 meq of dichromate.
5. So 10M = 2.4 ⇒ M = 2.4/10 = 0.24 M.
6. Check in moles: one Cr2O72− oxidises 6 Fe2+, and 0.02×20 = 0.4 mmol dichromate × 6 = 2.4 mmol Fe2+ in 10 mL = 0.24 M. Same answer.
7. 0.24 M = 24 × 10−2 M, so the blank is 24.
 6 x 0.02 x 20 = 1 x M x 10
 2.4 = 10 M
 M = 2.4 / 10 = 0.24 M
 0.24 M = 24 x 10^-2 M
 ------------------------------------------
 answer blank = 24                <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick The 6 electrons of dichromate are the whole game — forget the factor (use n = 1) and you get 4, the planted trap. Remember: one Cr2O72− oxidises six Fe2+.
Answer: 24 (i.e. 0.24 M).
51Manganese (VI) has ability to disproportionate in acidic solution. The difference in oxidation states of two ions it forms in acidic solution is [JEE Main 2022 (24 Jun Shift 2)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 24 Jun · EveningSolution ↗
Manganate disproportionation statesManganese (VI) has ability to disproportionate in acidic solution. The difference in oxidation states of two ions🟢 Exact3
Concept Manganese(VI) is the manganate ion MnO42−. In acid it is unstable and disproportionates — the same +6 species is simultaneously oxidised and reduced, giving one product with Mn above +6 and one below. The quantity asked for is the gap between those two oxidation states, not between the products and the start.
Key relation 3MnO42− + 4H+ → 2MnO4− + MnO2 + 2H2O +6 splits into +7 and +4.
Solution
1. Mn(VI) is the manganate ion MnO42−: 4(−2) + Mn = −2 gives Mn = +6.
2. Disproportionation: part of it is oxidised to permanganate MnO4− (Mn = +7).
3. The rest is reduced to manganese dioxide MnO2 (Mn = +4).
4. Balanced: 3MnO42− + 4H+ → 2MnO4− + MnO2 + 2H2O. 2 electrons lost (two 6→7) balance 2 gained (one 6→4).
5. So the two manganese-containing ions/products have Mn in the +7 and +4 states.
6. Difference in oxidation states = 7 − 4 = 3.
 Mn(VI) = MnO4 2-  (Mn = +6)
 oxidised -> MnO4-  (Mn = +7)
 reduced  -> MnO2   (Mn = +4)
 3MnO4 2- + 4H+ -> 2MnO4- + MnO2 + 2H2O
 difference = 7 - 4 = 3
 ------------------------------------------
 difference = 3                   <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Disproportionation always straddles the starting state: +6 goes up to +7 and down to +4, so the spread is 3. The middle state (+6) is the giveaway that it is a disproportionation.
Answer: 3.
52Which one of the following reactions indicates the reducing ability of hydrogen peroxide in basic medium? [JEE Main 2022 (29 Jun Shift 1)]
(a) HOCl + H2O2 → H3O+ + Cl− + O2   (b) PbS + 4H2O2 → PbSO4 + 4H2O   (c) 2MnO4− + 3H2O2 → 2MnO2 + 3O2 + 2H2O   (d) O4n2+ + H2O2 → Mn4+ + 2OH−
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 29 Jun · MorningSolution ↗
H2O2 reducing ability basicWhich one of the following reactions indicates the reducing ability of hydrogen peroxide in basic medium🟢 Exact(c) 2MnO₄⁻ + 3H₂O₂ → 2MnO₂ + 3O₂ + 2H₂O
Concept H2O2 acts as a reducing agent when its oxygen (−1) is oxidised to O2 (0) while it hands electrons to something else. We need that happening in basic medium — scan the options for H2O2 producing O2 and reducing the other species.
Key relation H2O2 → O2 + 2H+ + 2e− (reducing) if O2 is released and the partner is reduced, H2O2 is the reductant.
Solution
1. (c) 2MnO4− + 3H2O2 → 2MnO2 + 3O2 + 2H2O: Mn +7→+4 (reduced), H2O2 → O2 (oxidised). H2O2 is the reducing agent, and this is the basic-medium reaction. Correct.
2. (a) HOCl + H2O2 → H3O+ + Cl− + O2: H2O2 is reducing but this is acidic (H3O+ formed), not basic.
3. (b) PbS + 4H2O2 → PbSO4 + 4H2O: S2−→S6+, so H2O2 is oxidising — wrong role.
4. (d) Mn2+ + H2O2 → Mn4+ + 2OH−: Mn2+→Mn4+, H2O2 is again oxidising.
5. Only (c) shows H2O2 reducing in a basic medium.
 reducing H2O2 : O(-1) -> O(0), gives O2
 (a) acidic (H3O+)            reducing, wrong medium
 (b) PbS->PbSO4               H2O2 oxidising
 (c) MnO4- -> MnO2 + O2       reducing, basic  OK
 (d) Mn2+ -> Mn4+             H2O2 oxidising
 ------------------------------------------
 answer = (c)                     <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Two filters: H2O2 must release O2 (reducing) and the medium must be basic (no H+/H3O+ on the product side). That knocks out (a) at once even though it also has H2O2 reducing.
Answer: (c).
53The dark purple colour of KMnO4 disappears in the titration with oxalic acid in acidic medium. The overall change in the oxidation number of manganese in the reaction is [JEE Main 2022-26 Jul-Shift-1]
(a) 5   (b) 1   (c) 7   (d) 2
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 26 Jul · MorningSolution ↗
Manganese oxidation change oxalicThe dark purple colour of KMnO4 disappears in titration with oxalic acid in acidic medium — overall change in oxidation number of manganese🟢 Exact(a) 5
Concept In this titration the dark purple colour of KMnO4 fades because MnO4− (intensely purple) is reduced to almost colourless Mn2+ in acidic medium while oxalic acid is oxidised to CO2. The “overall change in oxidation number of manganese” is just the size of that drop, which equals the electrons gained per Mn.
Key relation 2MnO4− + 5C2O42− + 16H+ → 2Mn2+ + 10CO2 + 8H2O Mn goes +7 → +2.
Solution
1. Oxalic acid reduces KMnO4 in acidic medium; the manganese product is Mn2+.
2. Oxidation state of Mn in MnO4−: 4(−2) + Mn = −1 gives Mn = +7.
3. Oxidation state of Mn in the free Mn2+ ion = +2.
4. Overall change = 7 − 2 = 5. This equals the 5 electrons gained by each Mn.
5. The disappearance of the dark purple colour (purple → nearly colourless) confirms the complete reduction to Mn2+.
6. A change of 5 matches option (a).
 purple MnO4- -> colourless Mn2+
 Mn : +7 -> +2
 change = 7 - 2 = 5
 ------------------------------------------
 overall change = 5  = (a)        <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Loss of the purple colour is the visual cue for MnO4−→Mn2+, always a change of 5. Option (c) 7 is the oxidation number of Mn itself, not the change — the classic distractor planted for a quick misread.
Answer: (a) 5.
54The products obtained from a reaction of hydrogen peroxide and acidified potassium permanganate are [JEE Main 2022-26Jul-Shift-2]
(a) Mn4+, H2O only   (b) Mn2+, H2O only   (c) Mn4+, H2O, O2 only   (d) Mn2+, H2O, O2 only
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 26 Jul · EveningSolution ↗
H2O2 permanganate productsThe products obtained from a reaction of hydrogen peroxide and acidified potassium permanganate🟢 Exact(d) Mn²⁺, H₂O, O₂ only
Concept With acidified KMnO4, H2O2 is the reducing agent: permanganate drops to Mn2+ and H2O2 is oxidised to O2. So three products appear — Mn2+, water and oxygen.
Key relation 2MnO4− + 5H2O2 + 6H+ → 2Mn2+ + 5O2 + 8H2O Mn +7→+2; O2O2 → O2.
Solution
1. Acidic permanganate reduces to Mn2+ (not Mn4+), so options (a) and (c) are out.
2. H2O2 here is oxidised, releasing O2; so O2 must be a product.
3. Option (b) lists Mn2+ and H2O only — it omits the O2, so it is incomplete.
4. Balanced: 2MnO4− + 5H2O2 + 6H+ → 2Mn2+ + 5O2 + 8H2O. electrons: 2×5 (Mn) = 5×2 (O2) = 10.
5. Here H2O2 is the reducing agent (O: −1 → 0), opposite to its oxidising role against, say, PbS.
6. Products = Mn2+, H2O and O2 → option (d).
 acidic : MnO4- -> Mn2+  (5 e-)
 H2O2 -> O2 + 2H+ + 2e-   (reducing)
 2MnO4- + 5H2O2 + 6H+ -> 2Mn2+ + 5O2 + 8H2O
 products: Mn2+, H2O, O2
 ------------------------------------------
 answer = (d)                     <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Two checks pick (d): acidic permanganate → Mn2+ (kills Mn4+ options), and H2O2 as reductant must give off O2 (kills the “H2O only” option b).
Answer: (d) Mn2+, H2O, O2 only.
55The oxidation state of manganese in the product obtained in a reaction of potassium permanganate and hydrogen peroxide in basic medium is [JEE Main 2022-27 Jul-Shift-2]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 27 Jul · EveningSolution ↗
Mn oxidation state basic productThe oxidation state of manganese in the product obtained in a reaction of potassium permanganate and hydrogen peroxide in basic medium🟢 Exact4
Concept In basic medium hydrogen peroxide reduces permanganate, but not all the way to Mn2+ as it would in acid; the stopping point in neutral or basic medium is manganese dioxide, MnO2. The quantity asked for is simply the oxidation state of Mn in that MnO2 product, so the key skill is reading the medium correctly.
Key relation 2MnO4− + 3H2O2 → 2MnO2 + 3O2 + 2H2O + 2OH− basic medium stops the reduction at MnO2.
Solution
1. KMnO4 + H2O2 in basic medium: H2O2 acts as the reductant, releasing O2 (O: −1 → 0).
2. Permanganate in neutral or basic medium is reduced only to MnO2, not to Mn2+.
3. In MnO2 each O is −2, total −4 on a neutral molecule, so Mn = +4.
4. Balanced: 2MnO4− + 3H2O2 → 2MnO2 + 3O2 + 2H2O + 2OH−. Mn: +7→+4 is a 3-electron gain; each H2O2 donates 2 electrons, 2×3 = 3×2 = 6.
5. The presence of OH− on the product side confirms the medium is basic.
6. So the oxidation state of Mn in the product MnO2 is +4.
 basic : MnO4- + H2O2 -> MnO2 + O2
 in MnO2 : O = -2 (x2) = -4, so Mn = +4
 2MnO4- + 3H2O2 -> 2MnO2 + 3O2 + 2H2O + 2OH-
 ------------------------------------------
 Mn oxidation state = +4          <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Medium sets the stopping point: acid → Mn2+ (+2), but neutral/basic → MnO2 (+4). The “basic” in the stem is the signal for +4, not +2.
Answer: +4.
56Given below are two statements: One is labelled as Assertion A and the other is labelled as Reason R
Assertion A : Permanganate titration are not performed in presence of hydrochloric acid.
Reason R : Chlorine is formed as a consequence of oxidation of hydrochloric acid. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below [JEE Main 2022-28 July-Shift-2]
(a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A   (b) Both A and R are true but R is NOT the correct explanation of A   (c) A is true but R is false   (d) A is false but R is true
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 28 Jul · EveningSolution ↗
Permanganate HCl assertion reasonAssertion: Permanganate titrations are not performed in presence of hydrochloric acid. Reason: chlorine formed🟢 Exact(a) Both A and R true and R is the correct explanation of A
Concept Permanganate titrations need a non-oxidisable acid, dilute H2SO4. HCl fails because its chloride ion is itself oxidised by MnO4−: some permanganate is then spent making Cl2 instead of reacting with the analyte, so the measured titre is too high. Assertion–Reason questions turn on whether R is not only true but the cause of A, so both facts and their link must be checked.
Key relation 2MnO4− + 10Cl− + 16H+ → 2Mn2+ + 5Cl2 + 8H2O E°(MnO4−/Mn2+) = 1.51 V > E°(Cl2/Cl−) = 1.36 V, so oxidation of Cl− is spontaneous.
Solution
1. Assertion A: KMnO4 titrations are indeed not performed in the presence of HCl — this is a true statement.
2. Reason R: the chloride of HCl is oxidised to chlorine, Cl− → (1/2)Cl2 + e− — also true.
3. This side reaction drains MnO4−, so more titrant is consumed than the analyte demands and the end point is falsified. That is exactly why A holds.
4. Therefore R is not merely true but is the correct explanation of A.
5. H2SO4 is used instead: sulphate is not oxidised, and HNO3 is avoided because it is itself an oxidiser.
 E(MnO4-/Mn2+) = 1.51 V > E(Cl2/Cl-) = 1.36 V
 => MnO4- oxidises Cl-  : extra titrant used
 A true, R true, R explains A    -> (a)
⚡ JEE Trick “Why dilute H2SO4, not HCl or HNO3?” is the recurring hook: HCl is oxidised to Cl2, HNO3 is itself an oxidant, only H2SO4 is inert both ways.
Answer: (a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A.
570.01 MKMnO4 solution was added to 20.0 mL of 0.05 M Mohr 's salt solution through a burette. The initial reading of 50 mL burette is zero. The volume of KMnO4 solution left in the burette after the end point is ____ mL. (nearest integer) [JEE Main 2022 (28 Jun Shift 2)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 28 Jun · EveningSolution ↗
Burette volume left titration0.01M KMnO4 solution added to 20.0mL of 0.05M Mohr's salt — volume of KMnO4 left in burette after end point🟢 Exact30
Concept Mohr’s salt supplies Fe2+, which permanganate oxidises 5:1. Find the volume of titrant actually delivered, then subtract from the 50 mL burette (initial reading zero) to get the volume still left inside.
Key relation MnO4− + 5Fe2+ + 8H+ → Mn2+ + 5Fe3+ + 4H2O One permanganate (5 electrons) oxidises five Fe2+, so n(MnO4−) = n(Fe2+)/5.
Solution
1. mmol Fe2+ = 20.0 mL × 0.05 M = 1.0 mmol.
2. From the 5:1 ratio, mmol MnO4− needed = 1.0/5 = 0.20 mmol.
3. Volume of 0.01 M KMnO4 = 0.20 mmol / 0.01 M = 20 mL delivered.
4. Burette capacity 50 mL, initial reading 0, so liquid remaining = 50 − 20 = 30 mL. The end point mark sits at 20 mL.
5. A quick cross-check: n-factor of Fe2+ is 1 and of MnO4− is 5, so milli-equivalents match (1.0 = 0.20 × 5).
6. The end point itself is self-indicating: the first excess drop of purple MnO4− gives a lasting faint pink, which is why no external indicator is added and the reading is read off directly as 20 mL.
 n(Fe2+)   = 20.0 x 0.05 = 1.0 mmol
 n(MnO4-)  = 1.0 / 5     = 0.20 mmol
 V used    = 0.20 / 0.01 = 20 mL
 V left    = 50 - 20     = 30 mL  <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick The twist is “left in the burette”, not “used”. Compute the 20 mL consumed, then subtract from the full 50 mL. Forgetting the subtraction gives the tempting wrong answer 20.
Answer: 30 mL.
58Which of the given reactions is not an example of disproportionation reaction? [JEE Main 2022-26 Jul-Shift-1]
(a) 2H2O2 → 2H2O + O2   (b) 2NO2 + H2O → HNO3 + HNO2   (c) MnO4− + 4H+ + 3e− → MnO2 + 2H2O   (d) 3MnO42− + 4H+ → 2MnO4− + MnO2 + 2H2O
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 26 Jul · MorningSolution ↗
Not a disproportionationWhich of the given reactions is not an example of disproportionation reaction?🟢 Exact(c) MnO4- + 4H+ + 3e- -> MnO2 + 2H2O
Concept Disproportionation requires one element in a single species to be both oxidised and reduced, its atoms splitting into a higher and a lower oxidation state. A half-reaction that merely shows electrons is a plain reduction, not a disproportionation.
Key relation disproportionation: 2Xn → Xn+a + Xn−b Same element, same starting state, two different product states — no electrons written explicitly.
Solution
1. (a) 2H2O2 → 2H2O + O2: oxygen (−1) goes to −2 and 0 — disproportionation.
2. (b) 2NO2 + H2O → HNO3 + HNO2: nitrogen (+4) goes to +5 and +3 — disproportionation.
3. (d) 3MnO42− + 4H+ → 2MnO4− + MnO2 + 2H2O: Mn(+6) goes to +7 and +4 — disproportionation.
4. (c) MnO4− + 4H+ + 3e− → MnO2 + 2H2O: Mn only falls +7 → +4, electrons supplied from outside. Pure reduction, no element is simultaneously oxidised.
5. Hence the one that is not a disproportionation is (c); it is one half of the acidic MnO4−/MnO2 couple written with its electrons.
6. Note the contrast with (d): there Mn(+6) splits into +7 and +4 with no free electrons written, which is the authentic disproportionation of manganate.
 (a) O: -1 -> -2 and 0      disprop.
 (b) N: +4 -> +5 and +3     disprop.
 (d) Mn:+6 -> +7 and +4     disprop.
 (c) Mn:+7 -> +4 (gains e-) reduction  <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick If free electrons appear in the equation, it is a half-reaction (oxidation or reduction), never a complete disproportionation. Spotting the “3e−” in (c) settles it at a glance.
Answer: (c) MnO4− + 4H+ + 3e− → MnO2 + 2H2O.
59In the titration of KMnO4 and oxalic acid in acidic medium, the change in oxidation number of carbon at the end point is [JEE Main 2022-27 july-Shift-1]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 27 Jul · MorningSolution ↗
Carbon oxidation change titrationIn the titration of KMnO4 and oxalic acid in acidic medium, the change in oxidation number of carbon at end point🟢 Exact1
Concept In the permanganate–oxalic acid titration the oxalate ion is the reductant. The question asks only how much the oxidation number of carbon rises as oxalate converts to carbon dioxide at the end point. The word “carbon” signals a per-atom change, so the total electrons lost by the two carbons is not the answer.
Key relation C2O42− → 2CO2 + 2e− Each carbon loses one electron; the two carbons together release the two electrons MnO4− needs.
Solution
1. In oxalate C2O42−, let carbon be x: 2x + 4(−2) = −2, so 2x = +6 and x = +3.
2. In the product CO2, carbon = +4 (O is −2, molecule neutral).
3. Change per carbon = (+4) − (+3) = +1. That single-unit rise is what is asked.
4. Both carbon atoms rise by 1, so the ion loses 2 electrons overall, matching C2O42− → 2CO2 + 2e−.
5. This dovetails with the balanced redox: 2MnO4− + 5C2O42− + 16H+ → 2Mn2+ + 10CO2 + 8H2O.
6. There each oxalate hands over 2 electrons (n-factor 2), ten oxalate carbons feed ten CO2, and the 10 electrons released exactly reduce the 2 MnO4− (5 each) — a neat consistency check on the per-carbon change of 1.
 oxalate C : 2x + 4(-2) = -2  ->  x = +3
 CO2     C : +4
 change  = (+4) - (+3) = 1     <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick The question says “of carbon”, i.e. per atom, so the answer is 1, not 2. The 2 is the total electrons from the two carbons, a classic distractor.
Answer: 1.
60In neutral or alkaline solution, MnO4− oxidises thiosulphate to [JEE Main 2022-27 Jul-Shift-2]
(a) S2O72−   (b) S2O82−   (c) SO32−   (d) SO42−
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 27 Jul · EveningSolution ↗
Permanganate thiosulphate productIn neutral or alkaline solution, MnO4- oxidises thiosulphate to🟢 Exact(d) SO4^2-
Concept Permanganate is a powerful oxidant. In neutral or alkaline solution it oxidises thiosulphate completely, driving sulphur all the way up to its highest common state. The product is sulphate, not an intermediate sulphur-oxoanion.
Key relation 8MnO4− + 3S2O32− + H2O → 8MnO2 + 6SO42− + 2OH− In alkaline medium Mn drops +7 → +4 while S climbs +2 → +6.
Solution
1. In thiosulphate S2O32− the average oxidation state of sulphur is +2.
2. “Quantitative” oxidation by a strong oxidant takes sulphur to its maximum stable state, +6.
3. Sulphur at +6 with oxygen is the sulphate ion, SO42−. Each S loses 4 electrons.
4. The alternatives are wrong: S2O72− and S2O82− are not permanganate products, and SO32− (S = +4) is only partial oxidation.
5. Balancing confirms 6 SO42− per 3 S2O32−, with Mn ending as MnO2 in the alkaline medium.
6. Electron bookkeeping: each S2O32− loses 8 electrons (two S, +2 → +6), so 3 thiosulphate give up 24 electrons, matching 8 MnO4− each gaining 3 — a clean 24-electron balance that pins the product as sulphate.
 S in S2O3^2- : average +2
 strong oxidant -> S raised to +6
 S(+6) + O  =>  SO4^2-        <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Iodine takes thiosulphate only to tetrathionate (S = +2.5), but permanganate is far stronger and pushes it fully to +6 sulphate. Match oxidant strength to the final state.
Answer: (d) SO42−.
61The reaction of H2O2 with potassium permanganate in acidic medium leads to the formation of mainly [JEE-Main-2022-25Jul-Shift-1]
(a) Mn2+   (b) Mn4+   (c) Mn3+   (d) Mn6+
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 25 Jul · MorningSolution ↗
H2O2 permanganate acidic productThe reaction of H2O2 with potassium permanganate in acidic medium leads to the formation of mainly🟢 Exact(a) Mn^2+
Concept The product of permanganate reduction depends on the medium. In acidic solution MnO4− accepts five electrons and is reduced all the way to colourless Mn2+; H2O2 here acts as the reducing agent and is oxidised to O2.
Key relation 2MnO4− + 5H2O2 + 6H+ → 2Mn2+ + 5O2 + 8H2O Acidic medium: Mn goes +7 → +2, a five-electron reduction.
Solution
1. In acidic medium the standard half-reaction is MnO4− + 8H+ + 5e− → Mn2+ + 4H2O.
2. H2O2 supplies those electrons, its oxygen rising −1 → 0: H2O2 → O2 + 2H+ + 2e−.
3. So manganese ends at the +2 state, i.e. Mn2+. The pink colour of MnO4− fades to nearly colourless.
4. The other states are wrong for acid: Mn4+ (MnO2) forms in neutral/alkaline media, Mn6+ (manganate) in strong alkali, and Mn3+ is unstable.
5. Balancing 2:5 electrons gives the molecular equation above, confirming Mn2+.
6. Here H2O2 is the reducing agent (unusual for a species that is often an oxidiser): the stronger oxidant MnO4− wins, oxidising peroxide to O2 while being itself pulled down to Mn2+.
 acidic : MnO4- + 8H+ + 5e- -> Mn2+ + 4H2O
 H2O2 -> O2 + 2H+ + 2e-  (reductant)
 product Mn : +7 -> +2    -> Mn2+  (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the medium map: acid → Mn2+, neutral/weakly basic → MnO2 (Mn4+), strong base → MnO42− (Mn6+). The word “acidic” fixes the answer.
Answer: (a) Mn2+.
62On reaction with stronger oxidizing agent like KIO4, hydrogen peroxide oxidizes with the evolution of O2. The oxidation number of I in KIO4 changes to [JEE Main 2022-28 July-Shift-2]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 28 Jul · EveningSolution ↗
Iodine oxidation number changeOn reaction with stronger oxidizing agent like KIO4, hydrogen peroxide oxidizes with evolution of O2 — oxidation number of I in KIO4 changes to🟢 Exact5
Concept Hydrogen peroxide is amphoteric in redox: against a weak oxidant it oxidises, but against a stronger oxidiser such as KIO4 it is forced to act as a reducing agent, its oxygen (−1) rising to O2 (0). The electrons released reduce iodine in periodate to a lower oxidation state, and the clue is the stated evolution of O2.
Key relation KIO4 + H2O2 → KIO3 + H2O + O2 Periodate (I = +7) gains electrons and becomes iodate (I = +5).
Solution
1. Oxidation number of I in KIO4: +1 + x + 4(−2) = 0, so x = +7.
2. H2O2 acts as reductant here, so iodine must be reduced (its state falls).
3. The peroxide gives up 2 electrons per molecule (O: −1 → 0), reducing IO4− to IO3−, where I = +5. A two-electron drop, +7 → +5.
4. So the iodine ends in iodate, KIO3, with oxidation number +5.
5. The evolution of O2 confirms H2O2 is the electron donor and iodine the acceptor.
6. Balancing one electron pair: KIO4 + H2O2 → KIO3 + H2O + O2, where the 2 electrons from one peroxide exactly cover the +7 → +5 drop of a single iodine, so no other reductant or extra acid is needed.
7. The answer wanted is just the new oxidation number of iodine, which is +5, not the size of the change (2).
 KIO4 : +1 + x + 4(-2) = 0  ->  x = +7
 H2O2 -> O2 + 2H+ + 2e-   (reductant)
 I : +7 + 2e- -> +5  (in KIO3)  <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick “H2O2 evolves O2” is the tell-tale that peroxide is the reducer, so the partner must be reduced. Periodate (+7) drops exactly two to iodate (+5), not to iodide.
Answer: 5.
63Which one of the following is an example of disproportionation reaction? [JEE Main 2022 (26 Jun Shift 2)]
(a) 3MnO42− + 4H+ → 2MnO4− + MnO2 + 2H2O   (b) MnO4− + 4H+ + 4e− → MnO2 + 2H2O   (c) 10I− + 2MnO4− + 16H+ → 2Mn2+ + 8H2O + (5H2)   (d) 8MnO4− + 3 S2O32− + H2O → 8MnO2 + 6SO42− + 2OF
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 26 Jun · EveningSolution ↗
Example disproportionation reactionWhich one of the following is an example of disproportionation reaction? (a) 3MnO4^2- + 4H+🟢 Exact(a) 3MnO4^2- + 4H+ -> 2MnO4- + MnO2 + 2H2O
Concept Disproportionation means one element in a single reactant ends up in both a higher and a lower oxidation state. Scan the four equations for an element that splits that way; the scan garble in the distractors does not touch option (a).
Key relation 3MnO42− + 4H+ → 2MnO4− + MnO2 + 2H2O Manganate Mn(+6) goes to permanganate (+7) and to manganese dioxide (+4).
Solution
1. (a) In MnO42− Mn is +6; products are MnO4− (Mn +7) and MnO2 (Mn +4). Same element up and down — disproportionation.
2. (b) MnO4− + 4H+ + 4e− → MnO2: electrons shown, Mn only reduced +7 → +4 — a half-reaction, not disproportionation.
3. (c) I− + MnO4− type: iodide is oxidised and Mn is reduced — two different elements change, so inter-redox, not disproportionation.
4. (d) MnO4− + S2O32− type: Mn reduced, sulphur oxidised — again two elements, ordinary redox.
5. Only (a) has a single element, manganese, moving to two states, so (a) is the disproportionation.
6. This is the standard instability of manganate in acid: the +6 state is squeezed between the stabler +7 (MnO4−) and +4 (MnO2), so it splits spontaneously on acidification.
 (a) Mn: +6 -> +7 and +4   disprop.  <-- ans
 (b) Mn: +7 -> +4 (4e-)    reduction
 (c) I oxid., Mn reduced   inter-redox
 (d) S oxid., Mn reduced   inter-redox
⚡ JEE Trick One element, one reactant, two product states — that signature is unique to (a). Options with electrons written out, or with two different elements changing, are automatically ruled out.
Answer: (a) 3MnO42− + 4H+ → 2MnO4− + MnO2 + 2H2O.
64An acidified manganate solution undergoes disproportionation reaction. The spin-only magnetic moment value of the product having manganese in higher oxidation state is ____ B.M. (Nearest integer) [JEE Main 2022 (29 Jun Shift 1)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 29 Jun · MorningSolution ↗
Spin-only moment higher MnAn acidified manganate solution undergoes disproportionation reaction. Spin-only magnetic moment of product with Mn in higher oxidation state🟢 Exact0
Concept Acidified manganate MnO42− (Mn = +6) disproportionates into permanganate (+7) and MnO2 (+4). The product with manganese in the higher state is MnO4−; its spin-only moment follows from the d-electron count of Mn7+.
Key relation μ = √(n(n+2)) B.M., where n = unpaired electrons n is read off the 3d configuration of the metal centre.
Solution
1. Disproportionation: 3MnO42− + 4H+ → 2MnO4− + MnO2 + 2H2O.
2. Higher oxidation state product = MnO4−, with Mn at +7.
3. Mn is [Ar]3d54s2; removing 7 electrons empties both 4s and all 3d, giving a 3d0 centre. No d electrons remain.
4. Unpaired electrons n = 0, so μ = √(0 × 2) = 0 B.M.
5. This matches the fact that MnO4− is diamagnetic; its intense purple colour is a charge-transfer, not a d–d, transition.
6. For contrast, the other product MnO2 has Mn4+ (3d3, n = 3, μ = √15 ≈ 3.87 B.M.), but the question asks for the higher state, so the diamagnetic permanganate value of 0 is what counts.
 higher-state product : MnO4-  (Mn = +7)
 Mn7+ : [Ar] 3d0  ->  n = 0
 mu = sqrt(0(0+2)) = 0 B.M.   <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Mn7+ is d0, so it must be diamagnetic (μ = 0). The deep purple of permanganate is ligand-to-metal charge transfer, a classic trap that tempts you to expect unpaired electrons.
Answer: 0 B.M.
65In neutral or faintly alkaline medium, KMnO4 being a powerful oxidant can oxidise, thiosulphate almost quantitatively, to sulphate. In this reaction overall change in oxidation state of manganese will be [JEE Main 2022-29 Jul-Shift-1]
(a) 5   (b) 1   (c) 0   (d) 3
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 29 Jul · MorningSolution ↗
Manganese change thiosulphate sulphateIn neutral or faintly alkaline medium, KMnO4 oxidises thiosulphate to sulphate — overall change in oxidation state of manganese🟢 Exact(d) 3
Concept The oxidation state change of manganese in a permanganate reaction is set by the medium. In neutral or faintly alkaline solution MnO4− is reduced to MnO2, a three-electron change, independent of what the reductant (here thiosulphate) becomes.
Key relation MnO4− + 2H2O + 3e− → MnO2 + 4OH− Neutral/weakly alkaline medium: Mn goes +7 → +4.
Solution
1. In MnO4− manganese is at +7.
2. In neutral or faintly alkaline medium the reduction product is MnO2, where Mn = +4.
3. Overall change in oxidation state = 7 − 4 = 3. Each Mn accepts three electrons.
4. The balanced reaction 8MnO4− + 3S2O32− + H2O → 8MnO2 + 6SO42− + 2OH− confirms Mn ending as MnO2.
5. The decoy 5 is the acidic-medium change (+7 → +2); the stem is alkaline, so 5 is wrong and 3 is correct.
6. The thiosulphate half (S2O32− → 2SO42−) only balances the electrons; it does not alter how far manganese itself moves, which is fixed at 3 by the MnO4− → MnO2 step.
 neutral/alkaline product : MnO2
 Mn : +7 -> +4
 change = 7 - 4 = 3        <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Read the medium first. “Neutral or faintly alkaline” always means MnO2 and a change of 3; only acidic medium gives Mn2+ and a change of 5. The sulphate product is a red herring for this part.
Answer: (d) 3.
66Passage (Q.66 & 67): A sample (5.6 g) containing iron is completely dissolved in cold dilute HCl to prepare a 250 mL of solution. Titration of 25.0 mL of this solution requires 12.5 mL of 0.03 M KMnO4 solution to reach the end point. Number of moles of Fe2+ present in 250 mL solution is x×10−2 (consider complete dissolution of FeCl2). The amount of iron present in the sample is y% by weight. (Assume: KMnO4 reacts only with Fe2+ in the solution Use: Molar mass of iron as 56 g mol−1)
The value of x is ____ . [JEE Adv. 2021 (Paper-2)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Advanced 2021 · Paper 2Solution ↗
Moles Fe2+ in solutionA sample (5.6 g) containing iron is completely dissolved in cold dilute HCl to prepare 250 mL — moles of Fe2+ (JEE Adv 2021)🟢 Exact1.875
Concept Permanganate oxidises Fe2+ in a 1:5 ratio. Find moles of KMnO4 used on the 25 mL aliquot, scale to Fe2+, then multiply by 10 to reach the full 250 mL. The moles are written as x×10−2, so x is the answer.
Key relation MnO4− + 5Fe2+ + 8H+ → Mn2+ + 5Fe3+ + 4H2O n(Fe2+) = 5 × n(MnO4−).
Solution
1. n(KMnO4) = 12.5 mL × 0.03 M = 0.0125 L × 0.03 = 3.75×10−4 mol.
2. Fe2+ in the 25 mL aliquot = 5 × 3.75×10−4 = 1.875×10−3 mol.
3. The 25 mL is one-tenth of 250 mL, so Fe2+ in 250 mL = 10 × 1.875×10−3 = 1.875×10−2 mol. Scale by the dilution factor 250/25 = 10.
4. Writing this as x×10−2 gives x = 1.875.
5. Sanity: 0.03 M × 12.5 mL is a small titre, and the five-fold and ten-fold factors land the answer near 2×10−2 mol, as expected.
6. The assumption “KMnO4 reacts only with Fe2+” and “complete dissolution of FeCl2” means every mole of titrated Fe2+ stands for one mole of iron in the sample, so this x directly feeds the mass calculation in part y.
 n(MnO4-) = 0.0125 x 0.03 = 3.75e-4 mol
 Fe2+ (25 mL) = 5 x 3.75e-4 = 1.875e-3 mol
 Fe2+ (250 mL)= 10 x 1.875e-3 = 1.875e-2 mol
 x = 1.875                     <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Two multipliers matter: ×5 for the Fe:Mn ratio and ×10 for the aliquot-to-flask dilution. Miss either and you land on 1.875×10−3 or 3.75×10−3 by mistake.
Answer: x = 1.875.
67Passage (Q.66 & 67): A sample (5.6 g) containing iron is completely dissolved in cold dilute HCl to prepare a 250 mL of solution. Titration of 25.0 mL of this solution requires 12.5 mL of 0.03 M KMnO4 solution to reach the end point. Number of moles of Fe2+ present in 250 mL solution is x×10−2 (consider complete dissolution of FeCl2). The amount of iron present in the sample is y% by weight. (Assume: KMnO4 reacts only with Fe2+ in the solution Use: Molar mass of iron as 56 g mol−1)
The value of y is ____ . [JEE Adv. 2021 (Paper-2)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Advanced 2021 · Paper 2Solution ↗
Percent iron in sampleA sample (5.6 g) containing iron is completely dissolved in cold dilute HCl to prepare 250 mL — percent iron by weight (JEE Adv 2021)🟢 Exact18.75
Concept Having found the moles of Fe2+ in the whole 250 mL solution (part x), convert them to mass of iron and express that as a percentage of the 5.6 g sample. All the iron in the sample is taken as Fe2+ (complete dissolution of FeCl2), so the titrated moles equal the total iron, and the only new work is a mass-percent calculation using the given molar mass 56 g mol−1.
Key relation y% = (mass of Fe / mass of sample) × 100 = (nFe × 56 / 5.6) × 100 Molar mass of iron is given as 56 g mol−1.
Solution
1. From part x, n(Fe2+) in 250 mL = 1.875×10−2 mol, and all of it came from the dissolved iron.
2. Mass of iron = moles × molar mass = 1.875×10−2 mol × 56 g mol−1 = 1.05 g.
3. Percentage by weight = (mass of Fe / mass of sample) × 100 = (1.05 / 5.6) × 100. Sample mass is 5.6 g.
4. 1.05 / 5.6 = 0.1875, so y = 0.1875 × 100 = 18.75%.
5. Check: 18.75% of 5.6 g is 1.05 g, consistent with step 2, so the arithmetic closes.
6. The figure is plausible for an impure iron sample — well under 100% — and since 56/5.6 = 10 exactly, y is just the mole value 1.875×10−2 scaled by 1000, i.e. 18.75.
 n(Fe)   = 1.875e-2 mol
 mass Fe = 1.875e-2 x 56 = 1.05 g
 y = (1.05 / 5.6) x 100 = 18.75 %  <-- ans
⚡ JEE Trick Carry the full 1.875×10−2 mol from part x, not a rounded value; 56/5.6 = 10 exactly, so y is simply the mole figure ×1000, i.e. 18.75. Rounding x early would spoil the clean result.
Answer: y = 18.75.
68Aluminium reacts with sulfuric acid to form aluminium sulfate and hydrogen. What is the volume of hydrogen gas in liters (L) produced at 300 K and 1.0 atm pressure, when 5.4 g of aluminium and 50.0 mL of 5.0 M sulfuric acid are combined for the reaction? (Use molar mass of aluminium as 27.0 g mol−1, R = 0.082 atm L mol−1 K−1) [JEE Adv. 2020 (Paper-1)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Advanced 2020 · Paper 1Solution ↗
Hydrogen volume limiting reagentAluminium reacts with sulfuric acid to form aluminium sulfate and hydrogen — volume of hydrogen gas (JEE Adv 2020)🟢 Exact6.15
Concept This is a limiting-reagent gas problem. Balance aluminium with sulfuric acid, decide which reactant runs out by comparing the available mole ratio with the stoichiometric one, take the moles of H2 from the limiting reagent alone, then convert to volume with the ideal gas law at the stated 300 K and 1.0 atm. The excess reactant is irrelevant to the amount of gas.
Key relation 2Al + 3H2SO4 → Al2(SO4)3 + 3H2; V = nRT/P Moles H2 equal moles H2SO4 when acid is limiting (3:3).
Solution
1. n(Al) = 5.4 / 27.0 = 0.20 mol.
2. n(H2SO4) = 0.050 L × 5.0 M = 0.25 mol.
3. For 0.20 mol Al the ratio needs 0.20 × (3/2) = 0.30 mol acid, but only 0.25 mol is present, so H2SO4 is limiting. Acid runs out first.
4. From 3H2SO4 → 3H2 (1:1), n(H2) = n(H2SO4) = 0.25 mol.
5. V = nRT/P = 0.25 × 0.082 × 300 / 1.0 = 6.15 L. All quantities already in atm, L, mol, K.
6. Cross-check the leftover: 0.25 mol acid consumes (2/3)×0.25 = 0.167 mol Al, leaving 0.20 − 0.167 = 0.033 mol Al unreacted, which confirms aluminium was in excess.
 n(Al)   = 5.4/27  = 0.20 mol
 n(acid) = 0.050x5 = 0.25 mol  (limiting)
 n(H2)   = 0.25 mol
 V = 0.25 x 0.082 x 300 / 1.0 = 6.15 L  <--
⚡ JEE Trick Test the limiting reagent before trusting Al: 0.20 mol Al would need 0.30 mol acid but only 0.25 is there, so acid caps the yield. Using Al as limiting gives a wrong 0.30 mol H2 and 7.38 L.
Answer: 6.15 L.
695.00 mL of 0.10 M oxalic acid solution taken in a conical flask is titrated against NaOH from a burette using phenolphthalein indicator. The volume of NaOH required for the appearance of permanent faint pink color is tabulated below for five experiments. What is the concentration, in molarity, of the NaOH solution? [JEE Adv. 2020 (Paper-1)]
Volume of NaOH (ml): Exp 1 = 12.5, Exp 2 = 10.5, Exp 3 = 9.0, Exp 4 = 9.0, Exp 5 = 9.0
📝 PYQ: JEE Advanced 2020 · Paper 1Solution ↗
NaOH concentration from titration5.00 mL of 0.10 M oxalic acid titrated against NaOH with phenolphthalein — concentration of NaOH (JEE Adv 2020)🟢 Exact0.11
Concept In an acid–base titration only the concordant (closely agreeing) readings are used; the rough early trials that have not yet stabilised are discarded rather than averaged in. Oxalic acid is diprotic, so two NaOH neutralise one acid molecule. Use the concordant volume and that 1:2 stoichiometry to back out the NaOH molarity.
Key relation H2C2O4 + 2NaOH → Na2C2O4 + 2H2O n(NaOH) = 2 × n(oxalic acid); then M = n/V.
Solution
1. Concordant readings are Exp 3, 4, 5 = 9.0 mL; the 12.5 and 10.5 mL trials are rough and rejected. Only agreeing values count.
2. n(oxalic acid) = 5.00 mL × 0.10 M = 0.00500 L × 0.10 = 5.0×10−4 mol.
3. Being diprotic, n(NaOH) = 2 × 5.0×10−4 = 1.0×10−3 mol.
4. This NaOH is in 9.0 mL = 0.00900 L.
5. M(NaOH) = 1.0×10−3 / 0.00900 = 0.111 M ≈ 0.11 M.
6. Equivalently by the equivalence of equivalents: NacidVacid = NbaseVbase, i.e. (0.10×2)×5.0 = N×9.0, giving N = 1.0/9.0 = 0.111 N = 0.111 M for the monoacidic base NaOH — the same result.
7. Reporting to two significant figures as the data warrant gives 0.11 M. The three 9.0 mL trials set the precision.
 concordant V(NaOH) = 9.0 mL  (exp 3,4,5)
 n(acid)  = 0.005 x 0.10 = 5.0e-4 mol
 n(NaOH)  = 2 x 5.0e-4   = 1.0e-3 mol
 M = 1.0e-3 / 0.009 = 0.111 ~ 0.11 M  <--
⚡ JEE Trick Two traps in one: take only the concordant 9.0 mL (not an average of all five), and remember oxalic acid is diprotic (factor 2). Forgetting the 2 gives 0.056 M; averaging all readings gives 0.10 M.
Answer: 0.11 M.
70In the chemical reaction between stoichiometric quantities of KMnO4 and KI in weakly basic solution, what is the number of moles of I2 released for 4 moles of KMnO4 consumed? [JEE Adv. 2020 (Paper-2)]
📝 PYQ: JEE Advanced 2020 · Paper 2Solution ↗
Moles I2 releasedIn the chemical reaction between stoichiometric quantities of KMnO4 and KI in weakly basic solution — moles of I2 released🟢 Exact6
Concept “Weakly basic” fixes the permanganate product as MnO2 (Mn +7 → +4, a 3-electron gain), not Mn2+. Iodide is oxidised to I2. Balance the electrons to get the mole ratio of KMnO4 to I2.
Key relation 2MnO4− + 6I− + 4H2O → 2MnO2 + 3I2 + 8OH− 6 electrons balance: 2 Mn × 3e− = 3 I2 × 2e−.
Solution
1. Reduction (weakly basic): MnO4− + 2H2O + 3e− → MnO2 + 4OH− — 3 electrons per Mn.
2. Oxidation: 2I− → I2 + 2e− — 2 electrons per I2.
3. LCM of 3 and 2 is 6: take 2 MnO4− (6e− gained) with 3 I2 formed (6e− lost). Electrons must cancel.
4. So the ratio is 2 KMnO4 : 3 I2.
5. For 4 mol KMnO4: I2 = (3/2) × 4 = 6 mol. Scale the 2:3 ratio by 2.
6. Equivalently, 4 mol KMnO4 accept 4 × 3 = 12 mol electrons; each I2 releases 2, so I2 = 12/2 = 6 mol — the electron count gives the same 6.
 MnO4- + 2H2O + 3e- -> MnO2 + 4OH-   (x2)
 2 I- -> I2 + 2e-                     (x3)
 ratio  2 KMnO4 : 3 I2
 4 KMnO4 -> (3/2) x 4 = 6 mol I2   <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick “Weakly basic” means a 3-electron Mn change (to MnO2), giving the 2:3 ratio. If you wrongly used acidic Mn2+ (5e−), the ratio would be 2:5 and you would get 10, the classic distractor.
Answer: 6.
